Conventions. As in the book, \(k\) is an algebraically closed field and a surface is a nonsingular projective surface over \(k\) unless an exercise says otherwise. Results of the text are cited by their numbers in Hartshorne (“Thm. 1.9”, “Prop. 3.4”, …), and results of other chapters as “II, …”, “III, …” or “IV, …”. The numbering and statements of the exercises follow the edition scanned in this repository; each exercise is restated before its solution, and starred exercises are marked “Starred in the book” at the end of the statement.

Honest notes. Several printed statements are false as printed or need a hypothesis, and the solutions say what is proved instead: Ex. 2.15(c) claims a nonsingular curve \(Y\) which cannot exist (its arithmetic genus is \(4\) while it is birational to a curve of genus \(3\)); Ex. 3.8 as printed has two curves with different multiplicity sequences, so the intended phenomenon is proved for a corrected pair; characteristic assumptions are made in Ex. 1.4, 1.8, 3.5, 4.3, 4.4, 4.16. Ex. 4.15(c) is only partly proved: it is proved for very general points (outside a countable union of proper closed sets) and, for every general-position tuple, under one extra condition; existence of general-position tuples of every length, which is all that (d) and (e) use, is proved. Ex. 4.6 is open-ended and is answered under stated hypotheses. Ex. 1.8 depends on a normalization of the trace map (the one used in III Ex. 7.4), stated as an assumption. Some solutions use standard facts beyond the text (Weierstrass preparation, Tits' theorem, Nagata's criterion), which are named where used.

1. Geometry on a surface

Throughout, \(k\) is algebraically closed, \(X\) is a (nonsingular projective) surface, \(K\) is a canonical divisor, a “curve” on \(X\) is an effective divisor, and \(\equiv\) denotes numerical equivalence. We use freely the following consequences of the text.

  • •

    (F1) If \(C\subseteq X\) is an irreducible nonsingular curve and \(D\) is any divisor, then \(C.D=\deg_C(\mathcal L(D)\otimes\mathcal O_C)\). Indeed, both sides are additive in \(D\) and depend only on the linear equivalence class of \(D\); writing \(D\sim D_1-D_2\) with \(D_i\) very ample (proof of (1.1)) and choosing, by (1.2), representatives of \(|D_i|\) which are nonsingular and transversal to \(C\), the formula is (1.3). (This is how the text uses it in (1.4.1) and (1.5).)

  • •

    (F2) If \(C\ne C'\) are irreducible curves, then \(C.C'=\sum_P (C.C')_P\ge 0\) by (1.4), and \(C.C'\ge 1\) if \(C\cap C'\neq\emptyset\) (each local intersection multiplicity is the length of a nonzero module).

  • •

    (F3) If \(H\) is ample, then \(H.C>0\) for every curve \(C\) and \(H^2>0\): for \(m\gg0\), \(mH\) is very ample, and \(mH.C\) is the degree of \(C\) and \(m^2H^2\) the degree of \(X\) in the embedding (Ex. 1.2).

  • •

    (F4) (1.9) says: \(H\) ample, \(D\not\equiv 0\), \(D.H=0\) implies \(D^2<0\).

Exercise 1.1

Problem

Let \(C,D\) be any two divisors on a surface \(X\), and let the corresponding invertible sheaves be \(\mathcal L,\mathcal M\). Show that

\[ C.D=\chi(\mathcal O_X)-\chi(\mathcal L^{-1})-\chi(\mathcal M^{-1})+\chi(\mathcal L^{-1}\otimes\mathcal M^{-1}). \]
Solution

The proof of (1.6) shows (using Serre duality) that for every divisor \(E\)

\[ \chi(\mathcal L(E))=\tfrac12 E.(E-K)+\chi(\mathcal O_X).\tag{RR} \]

Apply (RR) to \(E=-C\), \(-D\), \(-C-D\), noting \(\mathcal L^{-1}=\mathcal L(-C)\), \(\mathcal M^{-1}=\mathcal L(-D)\), \(\mathcal L^{-1}\otimes\mathcal M^{-1}=\mathcal L(-C-D)\):

\begin{align*} \chi(\mathcal L^{-1})&=\tfrac12(C^2+C.K)+\chi(\mathcal O_X),\\ \chi(\mathcal M^{-1})&=\tfrac12(D^2+D.K)+\chi(\mathcal O_X),\\ \chi(\mathcal L^{-1}\otimes\mathcal M^{-1})&=\tfrac12(C+D).(C+D+K)+\chi(\mathcal O_X)\\ &=\tfrac12(C^2+C.K)+\tfrac12(D^2+D.K)+C.D+\chi(\mathcal O_X). \end{align*}

Hence \(\chi(\mathcal O_X)-\chi(\mathcal L^{-1})-\chi(\mathcal M^{-1})+\chi(\mathcal L^{-1}\otimes\mathcal M^{-1})=\chi(\mathcal O_X)(1-1-1+1)+C.D=C.D\). ∎

Exercise 1.2

Problem

Let \(H\) be a very ample divisor on the surface \(X\), corresponding to a projective embedding \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^N\). If we write the Hilbert polynomial of \(X\) (III, Ex. 5.2) as

\[ P(z)=\tfrac12 a z^2+bz+c, \]

show that \(a=H^2\), \(b=\frac12H^2+1-\pi\), where \(\pi\) is the genus of a nonsingular curve representing \(H\), and \(c=1+p_a\). Thus the degree of \(X\) in \(\mathbf P^N\), as defined in (I, Sect. 7), is just \(H^2\). Show also that if \(C\) is any curve in \(X\), then the degree of \(C\) in \(\mathbf P^N\) is just \(C.H\).

Solution

Here \(\mathcal O_X(1)=\mathcal L(H)\), and \(P(z)=\chi(\mathcal O_X(z))=\chi(\mathcal L(zH))\) (III, Ex. 5.2). By (RR) of Exercise 1.1,

\[ P(z)=\tfrac12 zH.(zH-K)+\chi(\mathcal O_X)=\tfrac12H^2z^2-\tfrac12 (H.K)\,z+1+p_a. \]

So \(a=H^2\), \(c=1+p_a\), \(b=-\frac12H.K\). By Bertini (II, 8.18) \(H\) is linearly equivalent to a nonsingular hyperplane section \(C_0\), which is connected (III, 7.9) and hence an irreducible nonsingular curve, of genus \(\pi\); the adjunction formula (1.5) gives \(2\pi-2=C_0.(C_0+K)=H^2+H.K\). Hence \(H.K=2\pi-2-H^2\) and \(b=-\frac12(2\pi-2-H^2)=\frac12H^2+1-\pi\). The degree of the surface \(X\) is \(2!\) times the leading coefficient of \(P\) (I, Sect. 7), i.e. \(a=H^2\).

Now let \(C\) be a curve (effective divisor) on \(X\). Tensoring \(0\to\mathcal L(-C)\to\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal O_C\to0\) with \(\mathcal L(zH)\) gives

\[ \chi(\mathcal O_C(z))=\chi(\mathcal L(zH))-\chi(\mathcal L(zH-C)). \]

By (RR),

\begin{align*} \chi(\mathcal L(zH))-\chi(\mathcal L(zH-C))&=\tfrac12\bigl[zH.(zH-K)-(zH-C).(zH-C-K)\bigr]\\ &=\tfrac12\bigl[2z\,H.C-C^2-C.K\bigr]=(H.C)\,z-\tfrac12C.(C+K). \end{align*}

Thus the Hilbert polynomial of \(C\subseteq\mathbf P^N\) has leading coefficient \(H.C\), i.e. \(\deg C=H.C\) (I, Sect. 7; for a one-dimensional scheme the degree is \(1!\) times the leading coefficient). ∎

Exercise 1.3

Problem

Recall that the arithmetic genus of a projective scheme \(D\) of dimension \(1\) is defined as \(p_a=1-\chi(\mathcal O_D)\) (III, Ex. 5.3).

  1. (a)

    If \(D\) is an effective divisor on the surface \(X\), use (1.6) to show that \(2p_a-2=D.(D+K)\).

  2. (b)

    \(p_a(D)\) depends only on the linear equivalence class of \(D\) on \(X\).

  3. (c)

    More generally, for any divisor \(D\) on \(X\), we define the virtual arithmetic genus (which is equal to the arithmetic genus if \(D\) is effective) by the same formula: \(2p_a-2=D.(D+K)\). Show that for any two divisors \(C,D\) we have

    \[ p_a(-D)=D^2-p_a(D)+2 \quad\text{and}\quad p_a(C+D)=p_a(C)+p_a(D)+C.D-1. \]
Solution

(a) From \(0\to\mathcal L(-D)\to\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal O_D\to0\) and additivity of \(\chi\), \(\chi(\mathcal O_D)=\chi(\mathcal O_X)-\chi(\mathcal L(-D))\). By (RR) (Exercise 1.1; this is (1.6) rewritten via Serre duality), \(\chi(\mathcal L(-D))=\frac12(-D).(-D-K)+\chi(\mathcal O_X)=\frac12D.(D+K)+\chi(\mathcal O_X)\). Hence \(\chi(\mathcal O_D)=-\frac12D.(D+K)\) and \(p_a(D)=1+\frac12D.(D+K)\), i.e. \(2p_a-2=D.(D+K)\).

(b) By (a), for effective \(D\) the number \(p_a(D)=1+\frac12(D^2+D.K)\) is expressed through intersection numbers, which depend only on the linear equivalence class of \(D\) by (1.1)(4).

(c) With the definition \(p_a(D)=1+\frac12D.(D+K)\) for every divisor \(D\):

\[ p_a(-D)=1+\tfrac12(-D).(-D+K)=1+\tfrac12(D^2-D.K), \]

while \(D^2-p_a(D)+2=D^2+2-1-\frac12D^2-\frac12D.K=1+\frac12(D^2-D.K)\). These agree. Next,

\begin{align*} p_a(C+D)&=1+\tfrac12(C+D).(C+D+K)=1+\tfrac12\bigl[C.(C+K)+D.(D+K)\bigr]+C.D\\ &=\bigl(1+\tfrac12C.(C+K)\bigr)+\bigl(1+\tfrac12D.(D+K)\bigr)-1+C.D=p_a(C)+p_a(D)+C.D-1.\qquad\square \end{align*}

Exercise 1.4

Problem
  1. (a)

    If a surface \(X\) of degree \(d\) in \(\mathbf P^3\) contains a straight line \(C=\mathbf P^1\), show that \(C^2=2-d\).

  2. (b)

    Assume \(\operatorname{char}k=0\). Show that for every \(d\ge1\), there exists a nonsingular surface \(X\) of degree \(d\) in \(\mathbf P^3\) containing the line \(x=y=0\).

Solution

(a) \(X\) is a nonsingular hypersurface of degree \(d\) in \(\mathbf P^3\), so \(\omega_X\cong\mathcal O_X(d-4)\) (II, 8.20.3), i.e. \(K\sim(d-4)H\) where \(H\) is the hyperplane class. By Exercise 1.2, \(C.H=\deg C=1\). Since \(C\cong\mathbf P^1\) has genus \(0\), (1.5) gives \(-2=C.(C+K)=C^2+(d-4)C.H=C^2+d-4\), so \(C^2=2-d\).

(b) For \(d=1\) take \(X=\{x=0\}\). For \(d\ge2\) let

\[ F=x z^{d-1}+y w^{d-1}+x^d+y^d, \]

which lies in the ideal \((x,y)\), so \(X=V(F)\supseteq\{x=y=0\}\). The partial derivatives are

\[ F_x=z^{d-1}+dx^{d-1},\quad F_y=w^{d-1}+dy^{d-1},\quad F_z=(d-1)xz^{d-2},\quad F_w=(d-1)yw^{d-2}. \]

Suppose they all vanish at a point. Since \(\operatorname{char}k=0\), \(d,d-1\neq0\). If \(d=2\), \(F_z=x\) and \(F_w=y\) vanish, so \(x=y=0\) and then \(F_x=z\), \(F_y=w\) give \(z=w=0\). If \(d\ge3\): \(F_z=0\) gives \(x=0\) or \(z=0\); if \(x=0\) then \(F_x=z^{d-1}=0\) so \(z=0\); if \(z=0\) then \(F_x=dx^{d-1}=0\) so \(x=0\). Thus \(x=z=0\), and likewise \(y=w=0\). So there is no common zero in \(\mathbf P^3\), and \(V(F)\) is nonsingular (in characteristic \(0\) the singular points of \(V(F)\) are the common zeros of the partials, \(F\) itself vanishing there by Euler's formula). A nonsingular hypersurface is irreducible (two components would meet along a curve at singular points), so \(X\) is a nonsingular surface of degree \(d\) containing the line \(x=y=0\). ∎

Exercise 1.5

Problem
  1. (a)

    If \(X\) is a surface of degree \(d\) in \(\mathbf P^3\), then \(K^2=d(d-4)^2\).

  2. (b)

    If \(X\) is a product of two nonsingular curves \(C,C'\), of genus \(g,g'\) respectively, then \(K^2=8(g-1)(g'-1)\). Cf. (II, Ex. 8.3).

Solution

(a) As in Exercise 1.4, \(K\sim(d-4)H\), and \(H^2=\deg X=d\) by Exercise 1.2. Hence \(K^2=(d-4)^2H^2=d(d-4)^2\).

(b) Let \(p_1,p_2\) be the projections. By (II, Ex. 8.3), \(\omega_X\cong p_1^*\omega_C\otimes p_2^*\omega_{C'}\). For points \(P\in C\), \(Q\in C'\) put \(m_P=\{P\}\times C'=p_1^*(P)\) and \(l_Q=C\times\{Q\}=p_2^*(Q)\). Write \(K_C\sim\sum n_iP_i\), \(K_{C'}\sim\sum n'_jQ_j\), with \(\sum n_i=2g-2\), \(\sum n'_j=2g'-2\). Then \(K\sim\sum n_im_{P_i}+\sum n_j'l_{Q_j}\).

Intersection numbers. \(m_P\) and \(l_Q\) are nonsingular and meet transversally in the single point \((P,Q)\) (local equations \(s-s(P)\) and \(t-t(Q)\) in independent coordinates generate the maximal ideal), so \(m_P.l_Q=1\). Next, \(\mathcal L(m_{P'})=p_1^*\mathcal L_C(P')\), whose restriction to \(m_P\) is trivial, since \(p_1\) is constant on \(m_P\). By (F1), \(m_{P'}.m_P=0\) for all \(P,P'\) (also if \(P=P'\)); similarly \(l_Q.l_{Q'}=0\).

Hence

\[ K^2=2\Bigl(\sum n_i\Bigr)\Bigl(\sum n'_j\Bigr)\,m.l=2(2g-2)(2g'-2)=8(g-1)(g'-1).\qquad\square \]

Exercise 1.6

Problem
  1. (a)

    If \(C\) is a curve of genus \(g\), show that the diagonal \(\Delta\subseteq C\times C\) has self-intersection \(\Delta^2=2-2g\). (Use the definition of \(\Omega_{C/k}\) in (II, Sect. 8).)

  2. (b)

    Let \(l=C\times\mathrm{pt}\) and \(m=\mathrm{pt}\times C\). If \(g\ge1\), show that \(l,m\), and \(\Delta\) are linearly independent in \(\operatorname{Num}(C\times C)\). Thus \(\operatorname{Num}(C\times C)\) has rank \(\ge3\), and in particular \(\operatorname{Pic}(C\times C)\neq p_1^*\operatorname{Pic}C\oplus p_2^*\operatorname{Pic}C\). Cf. (III, Ex. 12.6), (IV, Ex. 4.10).

Solution

(a) Let \(X=C\times C\) and let \(\mathcal I\) be the ideal sheaf of \(\Delta\) in \(X\). By (II, Sect. 8), \(\Omega_{C/k}\) is \(\Delta^*(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2)\), i.e. \(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2\) regarded on \(\Delta\cong C\). By (II, 6.18), \(\mathcal I=\mathcal L(-\Delta)\), so \(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2=\mathcal L(-\Delta)\otimes\mathcal O_\Delta\), and therefore \(\mathcal L(\Delta)\otimes\mathcal O_\Delta\cong(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2)^\vee\cong\Omega_{C/k}^\vee\). By (F1) and (IV, 1.3.3), \(\Delta^2=\deg\Omega_{C/k}^\vee=-(2g-2)=2-2g\).

(b) Intersection numbers (notation of Exercise 1.5(b) with \(C'=C\)): \(l^2=m^2=0\) and \(l.m=1\). Moreover \(\Delta\) meets \(l=C\times\{Q\}\) only at \((Q,Q)\), and transversally (local equations \(s-t\) and \(t-t(Q)\) generate the maximal ideal), so \(\Delta.l=1\), and likewise \(\Delta.m=1\). By (a), the matrix of the pairing on \((l,m,\Delta)\) is

\[ \begin{pmatrix}0&1&1\\1&0&1\\1&1&2-2g\end{pmatrix},\qquad \det=-(2-2g-1)+1=2g\neq0 . \]

If \(\alpha l+\beta m+\gamma\Delta\equiv0\) in \(\operatorname{Num}X\) (\(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbf Z\)), intersecting with \(l,m,\Delta\) shows that \((\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\) lies in the kernel of this matrix, so it is \(0\). Thus \(l,m,\Delta\) are independent in \(\operatorname{Num}X\), which has rank \(\ge3\).

Now consider \(p_1^*A+p_2^*B\) for divisors \(A,B\) on \(C\). The divisors \(m_P=p_1^*(P)\), \(P\in C\), form a family parametrized by \(C\): the divisor \(\mathcal D=\{(x,y,t):x=t\}=(p_{13})^*\Delta_C\subseteq X\times C\) is an effective Cartier divisor, irreducible and dominating \(C\), hence flat over \(C\) (see Exercise 1.7), with fibre \(m_P\) over \(t=P\). Hence the \(m_P\) are all prealgebraically equivalent, hence numerically equivalent by Exercise 1.7(c). Similarly the \(l_Q\) are numerically equivalent. So \(p_1^*A\equiv(\deg A)\,m\) and \(p_2^*B\equiv(\deg B)\,l\), and the image of \(p_1^*\operatorname{Pic}C+p_2^*\operatorname{Pic}C\) in \(\operatorname{Num}X\) lies in \(\mathbf Zl+\mathbf Zm\), which does not contain the class of \(\Delta\). So \(\Delta\notin p_1^*\operatorname{Pic}C+p_2^*\operatorname{Pic}C\), and \(\operatorname{Pic}(C\times C)\ne p_1^*\operatorname{Pic}C\oplus p_2^*\operatorname{Pic}C\). ∎

Exercise 1.7

Problem

Algebraic equivalence of divisors. Let \(X\) be a surface. Recall that we have defined an algebraic family of effective divisors on \(X\), parametrized by a nonsingular curve \(T\), to be an effective Cartier divisor \(D\) on \(X\times T\), flat over \(T\) (III, 9.8.5). In this case, for any two closed points \(0,1\in T\), we say the corresponding divisors \(D_0,D_1\) on \(X\) are prealgebraically equivalent. Two arbitrary divisors are prealgebraically equivalent if they are differences of prealgebraically equivalent effective divisors. Two divisors \(D,D'\) are algebraically equivalent if there is a finite sequence \(D=D_0,D_1,\dots,D_n=D'\) with \(D_i\) and \(D_{i+1}\) prealgebraically equivalent for each \(i\).

  1. (a)

    Show that the divisors algebraically equivalent to \(0\) form a subgroup of \(\operatorname{Div}X\).

  2. (b)

    Show that linearly equivalent divisors are algebraically equivalent. [Hint: If \((f)\) is a principal divisor on \(X\), consider the principal divisor \((tf-u)\) on \(X\times\mathbf P^1\), where \(t,u\) are the homogeneous coordinates on \(\mathbf P^1\).]

  3. (c)

    Show that algebraically equivalent divisors are numerically equivalent. [Hint: Use (III, 9.9) to show that for any very ample \(H\), if \(D\) and \(D'\) are algebraically equivalent, then \(D.H=D'.H\).]

(The Note on the Neron–Severi group following the exercise is a remark, not an assignment.)

Solution

Reading of the definition. We read “differences of prealgebraically equivalent effective divisors” as: \(D,D'\) are prealgebraically equivalent (written \(D\sim_pD'\)) if \(D=E_0-F_0\), \(D'=E_1-F_1\) with \(E_0,E_1\) effective and prealgebraically equivalent via a family, and likewise \(F_0,F_1\) (the two families may have different parameter curves). Algebraic equivalence \(\approx\) is the equivalence relation generated by \(\sim_p\). (\(\sim_p\) is reflexive, using the constant family \(E\times T\), and symmetric, as \(0,1\) are arbitrary points of \(T\).)

A flatness criterion. Let \(T\) be a nonsingular curve and \(\mathcal D\) an effective Cartier divisor on the nonsingular variety \(X\times T\). The local rings of \(X\times T\) are regular, hence factorial, so locally \(\mathcal D\) is defined by \(f=\prod\pi_i^{n_i}\) with prime elements \(\pi_i\), and the associated primes of \(\mathcal O_{\mathcal D}\) are exactly the \((\pi_i)\), i.e. the generic points of the irreducible components of \(\mathcal D\). By (III, 9.7) \(\mathcal D\) is flat over \(T\) iff no irreducible component of \(\mathcal D\) maps to a closed point of \(T\), i.e. iff no component of \(\mathcal D\) is a whole fibre \(X\times\{t\}\) (components have dimension \(2\) and fibres are irreducible). Consequences: (i) if \(\mathcal D_1,\mathcal D_2\) are flat effective divisors over \(T\), so is \(\mathcal D_1+\mathcal D_2\) (its components are those of \(\mathcal D_1,\mathcal D_2\)), and its fibre over \(t\) is the sum of the fibres (the restrictions of local equations to \(X\times\{t\}\) are nonzero and multiply); (ii) for an effective divisor \(G\) on \(X\), \(p_1^*G=G\times T\) is flat with all fibres \(G\).

(a) Claim: if \(D\sim_pD'\) and \(G\) is any divisor, then \(D+G\sim_pD'+G\). Write \(D=E_0-F_0\), \(D'=E_1-F_1\) as above and \(G=G_1-G_2\) with \(G_i\) effective. Then \(D+G=(E_0+G_1)-(F_0+G_2)\) and \(D'+G=(E_1+G_1)-(F_1+G_2)\). If \(\mathcal E\subseteq X\times T\) is a family with fibres \(E_0,E_1\), then \(\mathcal E+p_1^*G_1\) is a family with fibres \(E_0+G_1,E_1+G_1\) by (i),(ii); likewise for \(F+G_2\). This proves the claim. Applying the claim term by term along a chain, \(D\approx D'\) implies \(D+G\approx D'+G\) for all \(G\). Therefore, if \(D\approx D'\) and \(E\approx E'\), then \(D+E\approx D'+E\approx D'+E'\); and if \(D\approx D'\), adding \(-D-D'\) gives \(-D'\approx-D\). Consequently the set \(\{D:D\approx0\}\) contains \(0\) and is closed under sums (if \(D\approx0\), \(E\approx0\) then \(D+E\approx0\)) and negatives (\(D\approx0\Rightarrow-D\approx-0=0\)): it is a subgroup of \(\operatorname{Div}X\).

(b) Step 1: effective \(E\sim E'\) implies \(E\sim_pE'\). Write \(E'=E+(f)\) with \(f\) a rational function; if \(f\) is constant, \(E=E'\) and we are done, so let \(f\) be nonconstant. Then \(s_0=1\) and \(s_1=f\) are global sections of \(\mathcal L(E)\) (as subsheaf of the constant sheaf \(\mathcal K\)) with divisors \(E\) and \(E+(f)=E'\) respectively. On \(X\times\mathbf P^1\) consider the section

\[ \sigma=t\,s_0+u\,s_1\in H^0\bigl(X\times\mathbf P^1,\;p_1^*\mathcal L(E)\otimes p_2^*\mathcal O(1)\bigr) \]

(this is the section \(tf-u\) of the hint, up to renaming coordinates), and let \(\mathcal D\) be its divisor of zeros, an effective Cartier divisor. Its restriction to the fibre \(X\times\{(c_0:c_1)\}\) is the section \(c_0+c_1f\) of \(\mathcal L(E)\), which is nonzero for every \((c_0:c_1)\), because \(f\) is nonconstant. So no fibre is contained in \(\mathcal D\), and \(\mathcal D\) is flat over \(\mathbf P^1\) by the criterion. The fibre of \(\mathcal D\) over \((c_0:c_1)\) is the divisor of \(c_0+c_1f\), which is \(E\) at \((1:0)\) and \(E'\) at \((0:1)\). Hence \(E\sim_pE'\).

Step 2. Let \(D\sim D'\), \(D=A-B\), \(D'=A'-B'\) with \(A,B,A',B'\) effective. Then \(A+B'\sim A'+B\) are effective and linearly equivalent, so \(A+B'\sim_pA'+B\) by Step 1, hence \(A+B'\approx A'+B\). Adding \(-B-B'\) (by (a)) gives \(A-B\approx A'-B'\), i.e. \(D\approx D'\).

(c) Let \(H\) be very ample, \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^N\). Let \(\mathcal E\subseteq X\times T\) be a family of effective divisors; it is a closed subscheme of \(\mathbf P^N\times T\) flat over \(T\), so by (III, 9.9) the Hilbert polynomials of the fibres \(\mathcal E_t\subseteq\mathbf P^N\) are independent of \(t\). The fibre \(\mathcal E_t\) is the divisor \(E_t\), whose Hilbert polynomial has leading coefficient \(E_t.H\) by Exercise 1.2. Hence \(E_0.H=E_1.H\) whenever \(E_0,E_1\) are prealgebraically equivalent effective divisors, and then \(D.H=D'.H\) whenever \(D\sim_pD'\) (by additivity, \(D.H=E_0.H-F_0.H\)), and so whenever \(D\approx D'\).

Now let \(G\) be any divisor. As in the proof of (1.1), \(G\sim H_1-H_2\) with \(H_1,H_2\) very ample. So \(D.G=D.H_1-D.H_2=D'.H_1-D'.H_2=D'.G\). Thus \((D-D').G=0\) for all \(G\), i.e. \(D\equiv D'\). ∎

Exercise 1.8

Problem

Cohomology class of a divisor. For any divisor \(D\) on the surface \(X\), we define its cohomology class \(c(D)\in H^1(X,\Omega_X)\) by using the isomorphism \(\operatorname{Pic}X\cong H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\) of (III, Ex. 4.5) and the sheaf homomorphism \(d\log:\mathcal O_X^*\to\Omega_X\) (III, Ex. 7.4c). Thus we obtain a group homomorphism \(c:\operatorname{Pic}X\to H^1(X,\Omega_X)\). On the other hand, \(H^1(X,\Omega)\) is dual to itself by Serre duality (III, 7.13), so we have a nondegenerate bilinear map

\[ \langle\ ,\ \rangle:H^1(X,\Omega)\times H^1(X,\Omega)\to k. \]
  1. (a)

    Prove that this is compatible with the intersection pairing, in the following sense: for any two divisors \(D,E\) on \(X\), we have \(\langle c(D),c(E)\rangle=(D.E)\cdot1\) in \(k\). [Hint: Reduce to the case where \(D\) and \(E\) are nonsingular curves meeting transversally. Then consider the analogous map \(c:\operatorname{Pic}D\to H^1(D,\Omega_D)\), and the fact (III, Ex. 7.4) that \(c(\mathrm{point})\) goes to \(1\) under the natural isomorphism of \(H^1(D,\Omega_D)\) with \(k\).]

  2. (b)

    If \(\operatorname{char}k=0\), use the fact that \(H^1(X,\Omega_X)\) is a finite-dimensional vector space to show that \(\operatorname{Num}X\) is a finitely generated free abelian group.

Solution

Set-up. The pairing is: cup product \(H^1(X,\Omega_X)\times H^1(X,\Omega_X)\to H^2(X,\Omega^2_X)=H^2(X,\omega_X)\) followed by the trace \(t_X:H^2(X,\omega_X)\to k\) (this is the pairing of (III, 7.13) for \(n=2\), \(p=1\)). Serre duality determines the trace only up to a nonzero scalar, and the identity to be proved depends on this choice; so, as in (III, Ex. 7.4) (which the exercise invokes), we use trace maps normalized by: for a nonsingular projective variety \(Y\) and a point \(P\in Y\), \(t_Y(\eta(P))=1\). Ex. 7.4(a),(d) are taken as given for this normalization.

We use III, Ex. 7.4 in the following form. For a nonsingular irreducible projective variety \(Y\) of dimension \(n\) and a nonsingular subvariety \(Z\subseteq Y\) of codimension \(p\), the class \(\eta(Z)\in H^p(Y,\Omega^p_Y)\) is the element corresponding under (III, 7.13) to the linear form \(H^{n-p}(Y,\Omega^{n-p}_Y)\to H^{n-p}(Z,\Omega^{n-p}_Z)\xrightarrow{t_Z}k\). Ex. 7.4(a): \(t_Y(\eta(P))=1\) for a point \(P\); Ex. 7.4(d): for \(p=1\), \(\eta(Z)=c(\mathcal L(Z))\). Concretely, for \(p=1\) and \(Y=X\) a surface, \(Z=D\) a nonsingular curve:

\[ \langle c(D),y\rangle=t_D\bigl(y|_D\bigr)\qquad(y\in H^1(X,\Omega_X)),\tag{$*$} \]

where \(y\mapsto y|_D\) is \(H^1(X,\Omega_X)\to H^1(D,\Omega_X|_D)\to H^1(D,\Omega_D)\).

(a) Reduction. Both sides of the identity are bilinear in \((D,E)\) (as \(c\) is a homomorphism) and depend only on the linear equivalence classes of \(D,E\) (the left side because \(c\) is defined on \(\operatorname{Pic}X\), the right side by (1.1)(4)). As in the proof of (1.1), every class in \(\operatorname{Pic}X\) is of the form \([C_1]-[C_2]\) with \(C_1,C_2\) very ample, and by (1.2) we may take \(C_1,C_2\) irreducible and nonsingular, and, writing \(E\sim E_1-E_2\) likewise, choose \(E_1,E_2\) (applying (1.2) to \(C_1,C_2\)) nonsingular, irreducible, and transversal to both \(C_1\) and \(C_2\). So it suffices to treat \(D,E\) irreducible nonsingular curves meeting transversally.

The case of transversal nonsingular curves. Let \(D\cap E=\{P_1,\dots,P_r\}\), so that \(D.E=r\) by (1.1)(1). By \((*)\),

\[ \langle c(D),c(E)\rangle=t_D\bigl(c(E)|_D\bigr). \]

The class \(c(E)\) is the image of the class of the cocycle \((g_{ij})\) of \(\mathcal L(E)\) under \(d\log\); since \(d\) commutes with restriction of forms, \(d\log:\mathcal O_X^*\to\Omega_X\) is compatible with \(\mathcal O_X^*\to\mathcal O_D^*\), \(\Omega_X\to\Omega_D\), so \(c(E)|_D=c_D(\mathcal L(E)\otimes\mathcal O_D)\), where \(c_D:\operatorname{Pic}D\to H^1(D,\Omega_D)\) is the analogous map for the curve \(D\). As in the proof of (1.3), since the intersection is transversal, \(\mathcal L(E)\otimes\mathcal O_D\cong\mathcal L_D(P_1+\dots+P_r)\). Hence, \(c_D\) being a homomorphism and using (III, Ex. 7.4(d) and (a)) for the point \(P_i\) on the curve \(D\),

\[ t_D\bigl(c(E)|_D\bigr)=\sum_{i=1}^rt_D\bigl(c_D(\mathcal L_D(P_i))\bigr)=\sum_{i=1}^rt_D(\eta(P_i))=r\cdot1=(D.E)\cdot1. \]

This proves (a).

(b) Let \(V=H^1(X,\Omega_X)\), \(h=\dim_kV<\infty\) (III, 5.2), and \(\operatorname{char}k=0\), so \(\mathbf Z\subseteq k\). By (a), \(\langle c(D),c(E)\rangle=D.E\in\mathbf Z\subseteq k\).

Step 1: if \(D_1,\dots,D_m\in\operatorname{Pic}X\) and \(\det(D_i.D_j)\neq0\), then \(m\le h\). Indeed, we claim \(c(D_1),\dots,c(D_m)\) are \(k\)-linearly independent in \(V\): if \(\sum\lambda_ic(D_i)=0\) with \(\lambda_i\in k\), pairing with \(c(D_j)\) gives \(\sum_i\lambda_i(D_i.D_j)=0\) for all \(j\); the integer matrix \((D_i.D_j)\) has nonzero determinant, which stays nonzero in \(k\) (characteristic \(0\)), so all \(\lambda_i=0\).

Hence there is a maximal \(m\ge0\) for which some \(D_1,\dots,D_m\) has \(G:=(D_i.D_j)\) with \(\det G\ne0\) (for \(m=0\) the empty determinant is \(1\)). Fix such \(D_1,\dots,D_m\).

Step 2: if \(N\in\operatorname{Pic}X\) and \(N.D_j=0\) for all \(j\), then \(N\equiv0\). Suppose \(N\not\equiv0\) and choose \(E\) with \(N.E\ne0\). Let \(\lambda=\operatorname{adj}(G)\cdot(E.D_j)_j\in\mathbf Z^m\), so that \(\sum_i\lambda_i(D_i.D_j)=(\det G)(E.D_j)\), and put \(E_0=(\det G)E-\sum\lambda_iD_i\). Then \(E_0.D_j=0\) for all \(j\) and \(N.E_0=(\det G)(N.E)\ne0\). For \(s\in\mathbf Z\) the class \(M_s=N+sE_0\) is orthogonal to all \(D_j\) and \(M_s^2=N^2+2s\,N.E_0+s^2E_0^2\) is a nonzero polynomial in \(s\) (its linear coefficient is nonzero), so \(M_s^2\ne0\) for some \(s\). Then the Gram matrix of \((D_1,\dots,D_m,M_s)\) is block diagonal with determinant \(\det G\cdot M_s^2\ne0\), contradicting the maximality of \(m\).

Step 3. The homomorphism \(\operatorname{Num}X\to\mathbf Z^m\), \(D\mapsto(D.D_1,\dots,D.D_m)\), is well defined and, by Step 2, injective. Thus \(\operatorname{Num}X\) is isomorphic to a subgroup of \(\mathbf Z^m\) and therefore is a finitely generated free abelian group (of rank \(\le h\)). ∎

Exercise 1.9

Problem
  1. (a)

    If \(H\) is an ample divisor on the surface \(X\), and if \(D\) is any divisor, show that \((D^2)(H^2)\le(D.H)^2\).

  2. (b)

    Now let \(X\) be a product of two curves \(X=C\times C'\). Let \(l=C\times\mathrm{pt}\), and \(m=\mathrm{pt}\times C'\). For any divisor \(D\) on \(X\), let \(a=D.l\), \(b=D.m\). Then we say \(D\) has type \((a,b)\). If \(D\) has type \((a,b)\), with \(a,b\in\mathbf Z\), show that \(D^2\le2ab\), and equality holds if and only if \(D\equiv bl+am\). [Hint: Show that \(H=l+m\) is ample, let \(E=l-m\), let \(D'=(H^2)(E^2)D-(E^2)(D.H)H-(H^2)(D.E)E\), and apply (1.9). This inequality is due to Castelnuovo and Severi. See Grothendieck [2].]

Solution

(a) By (F3), \(H^2>0\). Put \(D'=(H^2)D-(D.H)H\). Then \(D'.H=(H^2)(D.H)-(D.H)(H^2)=0\) and

\[ D'^2=(H^2)^2D^2-2(H^2)(D.H)^2+(D.H)^2H^2=H^2\bigl(H^2D^2-(D.H)^2\bigr). \]

If \(D'\not\equiv0\), (1.9) gives \(D'^2<0\), hence \(H^2D^2-(D.H)^2<0\) because \(H^2>0\). If \(D'\equiv0\), then \(D'^2=0\) and \(H^2D^2=(D.H)^2\). In either case \((D^2)(H^2)\le(D.H)^2\).

(b) Intersection numbers. From Exercise 1.5(b): \(l^2=m^2=0\), \(l.m=1\).

\(H=l+m\) is ample. Here \(H^2=2l.m=2>0\). By the Nakai–Moishezon criterion (1.10) it remains to see \(H.Z>0\) for every irreducible curve \(Z\subseteq X\). Fix \(l=C\times\{Q\}\) and \(m=\{P\}\times C'\). If \(p_2(Z)\) is a point \(Q_0\), then \(Z=C\times\{Q_0\}\) (an irreducible curve contained in the irreducible fibre) and \(Z.m=1\) (transversal at \((P,Q_0)\)), \(Z.l=0\) (disjoint, or \(l^2=0\) if \(Q_0=Q\)), so \(H.Z=1\). Otherwise \(p_2(Z)=C'\) (\(p_2(Z)\) is closed and irreducible), so \(Z\) meets \(p_2^{-1}(Q)=l\) and \(Z\neq l\); by (F2), \(Z.l\ge1\). Also \(Z.m\ge0\): by (F2) if \(Z\neq m\), and \(m^2=0\) if \(Z=m\). Hence \(H.Z\ge1>0\). So \(H\) is ample.

The inequality. Put \(N=D-bl-am\). Then \(N.l=a-0-a\,(m.l)=0\) and \(N.m=b-b=0\), so \(N.H=0\). Also

\[ N^2=D^2-2D.(bl+am)+(bl+am)^2=D^2-2(ab+ab)+2ab=D^2-2ab. \]

If \(N\not\equiv0\), (1.9) gives \(N^2<0\); if \(N\equiv0\), \(N^2=0\). Hence \(D^2-2ab\le0\), with equality if and only if \(N\equiv0\), i.e. \(D\equiv bl+am\). (For \(E=l-m\) one has \(E^2=-2\), \(H^2=2\), \(E.H=0\), \(D.H=a+b\), \(D.E=a-b\), and the divisor \(D'\) of the hint is \(-4D+2(a+b)H-2(a-b)E=-4N\).) ∎

Exercise 1.10

Problem

Weil's Proof of the Analogue of the Riemann Hypothesis for Curves. Let \(C\) be a curve of genus \(g\) defined over the finite field \(\mathbf F_q\), and let \(N\) be the number of points of \(C\) rational over \(\mathbf F_q\). Then \(N=1-a+q\), with \(|a|\le2g\sqrt q\). To prove this, we consider \(C\) as a curve over the algebraic closure \(k\) of \(\mathbf F_q\). Let \(f:C\to C\) be the \(k\)-linear Frobenius morphism obtained by taking \(q\)th powers, which makes sense since \(C\) is defined over \(\mathbf F_q\) (IV, 2.4.1). Let \(\Gamma\subseteq C\times C\) be the graph of \(f\), and let \(\Delta\subseteq C\times C\) be the diagonal. Show that \(\Gamma^2=q(2-2g)\), and \(\Gamma.\Delta=N\). Then apply (1.9) to \(D=r\Gamma+s\Delta\) for all \(r\) and \(s\) to obtain the result. See (App. C, Ex. 5.7) for another interpretation of this result.

Solution

Setting. \(C=C_0\times_{\mathbf F_q}k\) for a nonsingular projective geometrically irreducible curve \(C_0/\mathbf F_q\), and \(f=F_{C_0}\times\mathrm{id}_k\), where \(F_{C_0}\) is the absolute \(q\)-power Frobenius (\(h\mapsto h^q\) on functions); \(f\) is a \(k\)-morphism. We write \(X=C\times C\) and use the notation \(m_P=\{P\}\times C\), \(l_Q=C\times\{Q\}\) of Exercise 1.5(b). The reference “(1.9)” is used in the form of Exercise 1.9(b) (itself a consequence of Theorem 1.9).

Facts about \(f\). (i) \(f\) is a finite morphism of degree \(q\) (the field extension \(k(C)\supseteq f^*k(C)=k(C)^q\) has degree \(q\), \(k\) being perfect). (ii) The fixed points of \(f\) on \(C(k)\) are exactly the \(\mathbf F_q\)-rational points of \(C_0\), so there are \(N\) of them. (iii) \(f^*\) kills differentials: \(d(h^q)=0\).

Intersection numbers with \(\Gamma\). \(\Gamma\) is the image of the closed immersion \(\gamma=(\mathrm{id},f):C\to X\), so \(\Gamma\cong C\) is a nonsingular irreducible curve of genus \(g\). Since \(p_1\gamma=\mathrm{id}\) and \(p_2\gamma=f\), (F1) and \(\deg f^*M=\deg f\cdot\deg M\) (IV, Ex. 2.6) give

\[ \Gamma.m_P=\deg\gamma^*p_1^*\mathcal L_C(P)=1,\qquad \Gamma.l_Q=\deg f^*\mathcal L_C(Q)=q. \]

Likewise for \(K=p_1^*K_C+p_2^*K_C\): \(\Gamma.K=\deg K_C+\deg f^*K_C=(2g-2)(1+q)\). By adjunction (1.5), \(2g-2=\Gamma.(\Gamma+K)\), whence

\[ \Gamma^2=(2g-2)-(2g-2)(1+q)=q(2-2g). \]

For the diagonal the same argument (\(\Delta\cong C\), \(f\) replaced by \(\mathrm{id}\)) gives \(\Delta.m_P=\Delta.l_Q=1\) and \(\Delta^2=2-2g\) (as in Exercise 1.6(a)).

\(\Gamma.\Delta=N\). Set-theoretically \(\Gamma\cap\Delta=\{(x,x):f(x)=x\}\), which has \(N\) points by (ii). At such a point \(x\) (a rational point), choose a uniformizer \(t\in\mathcal O_{C_0,x_0}\subseteq\mathcal O_{C,x}\). A local equation of \(\Delta\) at \((x,x)\) is \(p_1^*t-p_2^*t\), and its pullback to \(\Gamma\cong C\) via \(\gamma\) is \(t-f^*t=t-t^q\), which is \(t\) times a unit; so the scheme \(\Gamma\cap\Delta\) is reduced at \((x,x)\), i.e. \(\Gamma\) and \(\Delta\) meet transversally there. By (1.1)(1), \(\Gamma.\Delta=\#(\Gamma\cap\Delta)=N\).

The inequality. Let \(D=r\Gamma+s\Delta\) with \(r,s\in\mathbf Z\). Its type is \(a'=D.l=rq+s\), \(b'=D.m=r+s\), and

\[ D^2=r^2q(2-2g)+2rsN+s^2(2-2g). \]

Exercise 1.9(b) gives \(D^2\le2a'b'=2(rq+s)(r+s)\). Therefore, with \(a=q+1-N\) (so \(N=1-a+q\)),

\[ 0\le2a'b'-D^2=2gq\,r^2+2(q+1-N)\,rs+2g\,s^2=2\bigl(gq\,r^2+a\,rs+g\,s^2\bigr)\quad\text{for all }r,s\in\mathbf Z. \]

If \(g=0\) this reads \(a\,rs\ge0\) for all \(r,s\); taking \((r,s)=(-a,1)\) gives \(-a^2\ge0\), so \(a=0\), as \(|a|\le0\) requires. If \(g>0\) take \((r,s)=(-a,2gq)\): \(gq\,a^2-2gq\,a^2+4g^3q^2=gq(4g^2q-a^2)\ge0\), so \(a^2\le4g^2q\). In all cases \(|a|\le2g\sqrt q\) and \(N=1-a+q\). ∎

Exercise 1.11

Problem

In this problem, we assume that \(X\) is a surface for which \(\operatorname{Num}X\) is finitely generated (i.e., any surface, if you accept the Neron–Severi theorem (Ex. 1.7)).

  1. (a)

    If \(H\) is an ample divisor on \(X\), and \(d\in\mathbf Z\), show that the set of effective divisors \(D\) with \(D.H=d\), modulo numerical equivalence, is a finite set. [Hint: Use the adjunction formula, the fact that \(p_a\) of an irreducible curve is \(\ge0\), and the fact that the intersection pairing is negative definite on \(H^\perp\) in \(\operatorname{Num}X\).]

  2. (b)

    Now let \(C\) be a curve of genus \(g\ge2\), and use (a) to show that the group of automorphisms of \(C\) is finite, as follows. Given an automorphism \(\sigma\) of \(C\), let \(\Gamma\subseteq X=C\times C\) be its graph. First show that if \(\Gamma\equiv\Delta\), then \(\Gamma=\Delta\), using the fact that \(\Delta^2<0\), since \(g\ge2\) (Ex. 1.6). Then use (a). Cf. (IV, Ex. 2.5).

Solution

Let \(\Lambda=\operatorname{Num}X\), a finitely generated free abelian group by assumption (\(\Lambda\) is torsion free since \(nD\equiv0\Rightarrow D.E=0\) in \(\mathbf Z\)).

(a) Put \(n=H^2>0\), \(\kappa=K.H\).

Negative definiteness. Let \(W=\{v\in\Lambda\otimes\mathbf R:v.H=0\}\); this is the kernel of a linear form defined over \(\mathbf Q\), so \(W\) has a basis in \(\Lambda\otimes\mathbf Q\), and \(W_{\mathbf Q}=W\cap(\Lambda\otimes\mathbf Q)\) is dense. For \(0\ne v\in W_{\mathbf Q}\), a multiple \(Nv\) lies in \(\Lambda\), is nonzero in \(\operatorname{Num}X\) with \((Nv).H=0\), so \((Nv)^2<0\) by (1.9); hence \(q(v)<0\) for the quadratic form \(q(v)=v^2\) on \(W_{\mathbf Q}\). Diagonalize \(q\) over \(\mathbf Q\) in a \(\mathbf Q\)-basis \(e_1,\dots,e_r\) of \(W_{\mathbf Q}\): \(q(\sum y_ie_i)=\sum d_iy_i^2\) with \(d_i=q(e_i)<0\). So \(q\) is negative definite on \(W\) over \(\mathbf R\). In particular, Cauchy–Schwarz holds: \(|u.v|\le\sqrt{-u^2}\sqrt{-v^2}\) for \(u,v\in W\), and for each \(M\), the set \(\{v\in W:-v^2\le M\}\) is compact, so it contains only finitely many points of the discrete subgroup \(\Lambda\cap W\).

Irreducible curves of given degree. Fix an integer \(e\ge1\). We show that the classes of irreducible curves \(C\) with \(C.H=e\) form a finite subset of \(\Lambda\). Put \(C'=nC-eH\) and \(K'=nK-\kappa H\); both lie in \(\Lambda\cap W\). Compute

\[ C'^2=n^2C^2-ne^2,\qquad C'.K'=n^2\,C.K-n\kappa e. \]

Since \(C\) is an irreducible curve, \(H^0(\mathcal O_C)=k\), so \(p_a(C)=h^1(\mathcal O_C)\ge0\), and by Exercise 1.3(a), \(C^2+C.K=2p_a-2\ge-2\). Thus

\[ -C'^2=ne^2-n^2C^2\le ne^2+2n^2+n^2\,C.K=ne^2+2n^2+n\kappa e+C'.K'\le B+xy, \]

where \(B=|ne^2+2n^2+n\kappa e|\), \(x=\sqrt{-C'^2}\), \(y=\sqrt{-K'^2}\). So \(x^2\le B+xy\), which forces \(x\le y+\sqrt B\) (if \(x>y+\sqrt B\) then \(x^2-xy=x(x-y)>(y+\sqrt B)\sqrt B\ge B\)). Hence \(-C'^2\) is bounded in terms of \(e\), \(n\), \(\kappa\), \(K'\) only, so \(C'\) lies in a finite set; as \(nC=C'+eH\) and \(\Lambda\) is torsion free, the class of \(C\) is determined by \(C'\). This proves the claim.

Effective divisors. Let \(D=\sum n_iC_i\) be effective with \(D.H=d\), \(C_i\) distinct irreducible, \(n_i\ge1\). Then \(e_i=C_i.H\ge1\) by (F3) and \(\sum n_ie_i=d\). So (if \(d\le0\) only \(D=0\) is possible and there is nothing to prove) there are finitely many possible \((n_i,e_i)\) and, by the above, finitely many classes for each \(C_i\) with \(C_i.H=e_i\); hence \([D]=\sum n_i[C_i]\) takes only finitely many values in \(\Lambda\).

(b) Let \(X=C\times C\), \(g\ge2\), \(l,m\) as before; \(H=l+m\) is ample (Exercise 1.9(b)) and \(\operatorname{Num}X\) is finitely generated by assumption. Let \(\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}C\) and \(\Gamma_\sigma=\{(x,\sigma x)\}\) its graph, an irreducible nonsingular curve \(\cong C\).

As in Exercise 1.10 (with \(f\) replaced by \(\sigma\), of degree \(1\)): \(\Gamma_\sigma.m=\Gamma_\sigma.l=1\), hence \(\Gamma_\sigma.H=2\), \(\Gamma_\sigma.K=2(2g-2)\), and by adjunction \(\Gamma_\sigma^2=(2g-2)-2(2g-2)=2-2g<0\).

If \(\Gamma_\sigma\equiv\Gamma_\tau\) then \(\sigma=\tau\). Suppose \(\sigma\ne\tau\). Then \(\Gamma_\sigma\neq\Gamma_\tau\) are distinct irreducible curves, so \(\Gamma_\sigma.\Gamma_\tau\ge0\) by (F2). But \(\Gamma_\sigma\equiv\Gamma_\tau\) gives \(\Gamma_\sigma.\Gamma_\tau=\Gamma_\sigma.\Gamma_\sigma=2-2g<0\), a contradiction. (For \(\tau=\mathrm{id}\), \(\Gamma_\tau=\Delta\): this is the statement \(\Gamma\equiv\Delta\Rightarrow\Gamma=\Delta\) of the exercise.)

Thus \(\sigma\mapsto[\Gamma_\sigma]\in\operatorname{Num}X\) is injective. All \(\Gamma_\sigma\) are effective with \(\Gamma_\sigma.H=2\), so by (a) (with \(d=2\)) their classes lie in a finite set. Hence \(\operatorname{Aut}C\) is finite. ∎

Exercise 1.12

Problem

If \(D\) is an ample divisor on the surface \(X\), and \(D'\equiv D\), then \(D'\) is also ample. Give an example to show, however, that if \(D\) is very ample, \(D'\) need not be very ample.

Solution

Ampleness. \(D'\equiv D\) means \((D'-D).E=0\) for all \(E\). Taking \(E=D'\) and \(E=D\) gives \(D'^2=D.D'=D^2>0\) by (F3) (\(D\) ample), and \(D'.C=D.C>0\) for every irreducible curve \(C\). By the Nakai–Moishezon criterion (1.10), \(D'\) is ample.

Example. Let \(C\) be a nonsingular projective curve of genus \(3\) which embeds in \(\mathbf P^3\) with degree \(6\): e.g. a nonsingular irreducible member of \(|\mathcal O_Q(2,4)|\) on the quadric \(Q\cong\mathbf P^1\times\mathbf P^1\) (a very ample class, so Bertini applies and the connectedness is by (III, 7.9)); by (1.5.2) its genus is \(2\cdot4-2-4+1=3\) and its degree is \((2,4).(1,1)=6\). Let \(A=\mathcal O_C(1)\), a very ample line bundle of degree \(6\) on \(C\). Choose distinct points \(R,S\in C\) and put \(A'=\omega_C(R+S)\), also of degree \(6\).

\(A'\) is not very ample. Since \(6>2g-2=4\), \(h^0(A')=6-3+1=4\). Yet \(A'(-R-S)=\omega_C\) has \(h^0=3\), whereas a very ample divisor satisfies \(\dim|A'-R-S|=\dim|A'|-2\), i.e. \(h^0(A'(-R-S))=h^0(A')-2=2\) (IV, 3.1(b)). So \(A'\) is not very ample.

The surface. Let \(X=C\times\mathbf P^1\) with projections \(p_1,p_2\), and put \(D=p_1^*A+p_2^*\mathcal O(1)\) (i.e. \(\mathcal L(D)=p_1^*A\otimes p_2^*\mathcal O(1)\)), \(D'=p_1^*A'+p_2^*\mathcal O(1)\). Then \(D\) is very ample: it is the pullback of \(\mathcal O(1,1)\) under the closed immersion \(C\times\mathbf P^1\hookrightarrow\mathbf P^3\times\mathbf P^1\), followed by the Segre embedding. We have \(D-D'=p_1^*B\) with \(B=A-A'\) a divisor class of degree \(0\) on \(C\). Writing \(B=\sum n_iP_i\) with \(\sum n_i=0\), \(p_1^*B=\sum n_im_{P_i}\), and the divisors \(m_{P}=\{P\}\times\mathbf P^1\) are all numerically equivalent (Exercise 1.6(b): they form a family over \(C\), so Exercise 1.7(c) applies), so \(p_1^*B\equiv(\sum n_i)m_{P_1}=0\). Hence \(D'\equiv D\).

\(D'\) is not very ample. If it were, then its restriction to the closed subscheme \(C\times\{pt\}\cong C\) would be very ample (a closed immersion restricts to an embedding of the subscheme, and \(\mathcal O(1)\) restricts accordingly). But that restriction is \(A'\), which is not very ample.

So \(D\) is very ample, \(D'\equiv D\) (hence \(D'\) is ample by the first part), but \(D'\) is not very ample. ∎

2. Ruled surfaces

Throughout, \(k\) is algebraically closed, a “curve” means a nonsingular projective curve, a “surface” a nonsingular projective surface, and \(\equiv\), \(\sim\) denote numerical, resp. linear, equivalence. For a divisor \(D\) on a curve or surface, \(\mathcal L(D)\) is the associated invertible sheaf, and for a sheaf \(\mathcal G\) we write \(\mathcal G(D)=\mathcal G\otimes\mathcal L(D)\). For a locally free sheaf \(\mathcal E\) of rank 2 on \(C\) with \(X=\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\) we use the notation of (2.8.1): \(\mathcal E\) normalized, \(C_0\) the section with \(\mathcal L(C_0)\cong\mathcal O_X(1)\), \(\mathfrak e\) the divisor on \(C\) with \(\mathcal L(\mathfrak e)\cong\bigwedge^2\mathcal E\) (so \(\deg\mathfrak e=-e\)), \(f\) a fibre. We use freely: sections \(\sigma\colon C\to\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\) correspond to surjections \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal M\to0\) with \(\mathcal M\) invertible and \(\mathcal M\cong\sigma^*\mathcal O_X(1)\) (II, 7.12); \(\pi_*\mathcal O_X(n)=S^n\mathcal E\) (II, 7.11); a normalized \(\mathcal E\) sits in an exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal O_C\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(\mathfrak e)\to0\) (proof of (2.8)).

Exercise 2.1

Problem

If \(X\) is a birationally ruled surface, show that the curve \(C\), such that \(X\) is birationally equivalent to \(C\times\mathbf P^1\), is unique (up to isomorphism).

Solution

Suppose \(X\) is birational to \(C\times\mathbf P^1\) and to \(C'\times\mathbf P^1\). Then there is a birational map \(\theta\colon C\times\mathbf P^1\dashrightarrow C'\times\mathbf P^1\). Let \(p\colon C\times\mathbf P^1\to C\) and \(p'\colon C'\times\mathbf P^1\to C'\) be the projections, and put \(K=K(C\times\mathbf P^1)=K(C)(t)\). Via \(\theta^*\) we regard \(K(C'\times\mathbf P^1)\) as \(K\) itself, and consider the two subfields

\[ A=p^*K(C)=K(C),\qquad B=(p'\circ\theta)^*K(C')\ \subseteq K . \]

Both have transcendence degree 1 over \(k\), and \(B\cong K(C')\).

Lemma. Let \(C\) be a curve and \(B\subseteq K=K(C)(t)\) a subfield containing \(k\), of transcendence degree 1, whose function field curve \(C''\) has genus \(g(C'')\ge1\). Then \(B\subseteq K(C)\).

Proof of the Lemma. The inclusion \(B\subseteq K(C\times\mathbf P^1)\) is a dominant rational map \(\varphi\colon C\times\mathbf P^1\dashrightarrow C''\), defined on a dense open set \(U\). Let \(F_c=\{c\}\times\mathbf P^1\). The set \(V=p(U)\subseteq C\) is open and nonempty (\(p\) is flat, hence open). For \(c\in V\) the restriction of \(\varphi\) to the nonempty open subset \(U\cap F_c\) of \(F_c\cong\mathbf P^1\) is a rational map from a nonsingular curve to the projective curve \(C''\), hence extends to a morphism \(\mathbf P^1\to C''\) (I, 6.8). If this morphism were nonconstant it would be finite and surjective, and (IV, 2.5.4) would give \(0=g(\mathbf P^1)\ge g(C'')\ge1\), absurd. So \(\varphi\) is constant on \(U\cap F_c\) for every \(c\in V\).

Fix \(c_0\in V\). Since \(U\cap F_{c_0}\) is a nonempty open subset of \(F_{c_0}\), there is \(a\in k\) with \((c_0,a)\in U\). Let \(s_a\colon C\to C\times\mathbf P^1\), \(c\mapsto(c,a)\). Then \(s_a^{-1}(U)\) is a nonempty open subset of \(C\), and \(\varphi\circ s_a\) is a rational map \(C\dashrightarrow C''\), which extends to a morphism \(\rho\colon C\to C''\) (I, 6.8). Put \(V'=V\cap s_a^{-1}(U)\) (nonempty, open). For \(x\in U\) with \(p(x)=c\in V'\), both \(x\) and \(s_a(c)\) lie in \(U\cap F_c\), on which \(\varphi\) is constant; so \(\varphi(x)=\rho(p(x))\). Thus \(\varphi=\rho\circ p\) on the dense open set \(U\cap p^{-1}(V')\), whence \(B=\varphi^*K(C'')=p^*\rho^*K(C'')\subseteq p^*K(C)\). (Lemma)

Now we prove \(C\cong C'\). The situation is symmetric in \(C,C'\) (replace \(\theta\) by \(\theta^{-1}\), and exchange \(A\) and \(B\)).

Case \(g(C')\ge1\). By the Lemma, \(B\subseteq A\), i.e. \(K(C')\) is isomorphic to a subfield of \(K(C)\). If \(g(C)=0\), then \(K(C)\cong k(u)\) and by Lüroth's theorem (IV, 2.5.5) every subfield of transcendence degree 1 is purely transcendental, so \(g(C')=0\), a contradiction. Hence \(g(C)\ge1\) and the Lemma applied to \(\theta^{-1}\) gives \(A\subseteq B\). Therefore \(A=B\), i.e. \(K(C)\cong K(C')\) as \(k\)-algebras, and \(C\cong C'\) by (I, 6.12).

Case \(g(C')=0\). If \(g(C)\ge1\), the previous case with the roles of \(C\) and \(C'\) exchanged gives \(g(C')\ge1\), a contradiction. So \(g(C)=0\) and \(C\cong\mathbf P^1\cong C'\). ∎

Exercise 2.2

Problem

Let \(X\) be the ruled surface \(\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\) over a curve \(C\). Show that \(\mathcal E\) is decomposable if and only if there exist two sections \(C',C''\) of \(X\) such that \(C'\cap C''=\emptyset\).

Solution

Recall that the closed points of the fibre \(\pi^{-1}(y)=\mathbf P(\mathcal E(y))\) are the one-dimensional quotients of the fibre \(\mathcal E(y)=\mathcal E\otimes k(y)\), and that the point \(\sigma(y)\) of a section \(\sigma\) corresponding to \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal M\to0\) is the quotient \(\mathcal E(y)\to\mathcal M(y)\) (II, 7.12). Two quotients define the same point iff they have the same kernel.

(\(\Rightarrow\)) Let \(\mathcal E=\mathcal L_1\oplus\mathcal L_2\). The two projections \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal L_i\) are surjective and define sections \(\sigma_i\) with images \(C_i\). For each \(y\), the kernels of \(\mathcal E(y)\to\mathcal L_i(y)\) are \(\mathcal L_{3-i}(y)\), which are two different lines of \(\mathcal E(y)=\mathcal L_1(y)\oplus\mathcal L_2(y)\). So \(\sigma_1(y)\ne\sigma_2(y)\) for all \(y\), i.e. \(C_1\cap C_2=\emptyset\).

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Let \(C',C''\) be disjoint sections, corresponding to surjections \(u'\colon\mathcal E\to\mathcal L'\), \(u''\colon\mathcal E\to\mathcal L''\) (here \(\mathcal L'=\sigma'^*\mathcal O_X(1)\) etc.). Disjointness means \(\ker u'(y)\ne\ker u''(y)\) for every \(y\), so \(\mathcal E(y)\to\mathcal L'(y)\oplus\mathcal L''(y)\) is injective, hence bijective (both sides have dimension 2). Thus \(u=(u',u'')\colon\mathcal E\to\mathcal L'\oplus\mathcal L''\) is a homomorphism of locally free sheaves of rank 2 which is an isomorphism on every fibre. By Nakayama it is surjective, and a surjection between locally free sheaves of the same finite rank is an isomorphism. Hence \(\mathcal E\cong\mathcal L'\oplus\mathcal L''\) is decomposable. ∎

Exercise 2.3

Problem
  1. (a)

    If \(\mathcal E\) is a locally free sheaf of rank \(r\) on a (nonsingular) curve \(C\), then there is a sequence

    \[ 0=\mathcal E_0\subseteq\mathcal E_1\subseteq\dots\subseteq\mathcal E_r=\mathcal E \]

    of subsheaves such that \(\mathcal E_i/\mathcal E_{i-1}\) is an invertible sheaf for each \(i=1,\dots,r\). We say that \(\mathcal E\) is a successive extension of invertible sheaves. [Hint: Use (II, Ex. 8.2).]

  2. (b)

    Show that this is false for varieties of dimension \(\ge2\). In particular, the sheaf of differentials \(\Omega\) on \(\mathbf P^2\) is not an extension of invertible sheaves.

Solution

(a) Here \(C\) is a nonsingular projective curve (the argument works verbatim for affine \(C\)). We use induction on \(r\); \(r=1\) is trivial. Let \(r\ge2\).

Claim: there is an injection \(\mathcal M\hookrightarrow\mathcal E\) with \(\mathcal M\) invertible and \(\mathcal E/\mathcal M\) locally free of rank \(r-1\). Choose \(n\gg0\) so that \(\mathcal E(n)\) is generated by its global sections (II, 5.17), and let \(V\subseteq H^0(C,\mathcal E(n))\) be a finite-dimensional subspace generating \(\mathcal E(n)\). For each closed point \(y\) the evaluation \(V\to\mathcal E(n)(y)\) is surjective onto a space of dimension \(r\), so \(Z=\{(s,y)\in V\times C: s(y)=0\}\) is (the total space of) a vector bundle of rank \(\dim V-r\) over \(C\); hence \(\dim Z=\dim V-r+1\le\dim V-1\) since \(r\ge2\). The image of \(Z\) under the projection to \(V\) is a constructible set of dimension \(<\dim V\), hence not all of \(V\). Choose \(s\in V\) outside it (\(k\) is infinite): \(s\) vanishes at no point of \(C\). Locally, \(\mathcal E(n)\cong\mathcal O^r\) and \(s=(s_1,\dots,s_r)\) with \((s_1,\dots,s_r)=(1)\) at each point, so that \(s\) is part of a local basis. Thus \(s\colon\mathcal O_C\to\mathcal E(n)\) is injective with locally free cokernel; twisting by \(\mathcal O(-n)\) gives an injection \(\mathcal M=\mathcal O_C(-n)\hookrightarrow\mathcal E\) with locally free cokernel.

Now \(\mathcal E'=\mathcal E/\mathcal M\) is locally free of rank \(r-1\); by induction it has a filtration \(0=\mathcal E'_0\subseteq\dots\subseteq\mathcal E'_{r-1}=\mathcal E'\) with invertible quotients. Put \(\mathcal E_0=0\), \(\mathcal E_1=\mathcal M\) and \(\mathcal E_{i}=\) the inverse image of \(\mathcal E'_{i-1}\) in \(\mathcal E\) for \(i\ge2\). Then \(\mathcal E_i/\mathcal E_{i-1}\cong\mathcal E'_{i-1}/\mathcal E'_{i-2}\) for \(i\ge2\) are invertible, and so is \(\mathcal E_1/\mathcal E_0=\mathcal M\).

(b) We show that \(\Omega=\Omega_{\mathbf P^2}\) is not an extension of invertible sheaves, even not in a single step: there is no exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal L_1\to\Omega\to\mathcal L_2\to0\) with \(\mathcal L_i\) invertible (a successive extension of rank \(2\) is exactly such a sequence, as \(\mathcal E_1\) is invertible and \(\Omega/\mathcal E_1\) invertible). Since \(\operatorname{Pic}\mathbf P^2=\Z\), we would have \(\mathcal L_1=\mathcal O(a)\), \(\mathcal L_2=\mathcal O(b)\) with \(a,b\in\Z\). By the Euler sequence (II, 8.13) \(0\to\Omega\to\mathcal O(-1)^3\to\mathcal O\to0\), and \(\chi(\mathcal O(m))=\frac12(m+1)(m+2)\), we get for all \(m\)

\[ \chi(\Omega(m))=3\chi(\mathcal O(m-1))-\chi(\mathcal O(m))=\tfrac12(m+1)\bigl(3m-(m+2)\bigr)=m^2-1. \]

On the other hand, additivity of \(\chi\) gives

\[ \chi(\Omega(m))=\tfrac12(m+a+1)(m+a+2)+\tfrac12(m+b+1)(m+b+2)=m^2+(a+b+3)m+\tfrac12\bigl(a^2+b^2+3(a+b)+4\bigr). \]

Comparing the coefficients of \(m\) gives \(a+b=-3\); then comparing constant terms gives \(\frac12(a^2+b^2-5)=-1\), i.e. \(a^2+b^2=3\). This has no solution in integers (a sum of two squares is never \(\equiv3\pmod 4\)). So \(\Omega_{\mathbf P^2}\) is not an extension of two invertible sheaves, and statement (a) fails for \(\mathbf P^2\).

More generally, for \(\mathbf P^n\) with \(n\ge2\) the sheaf \(\Omega_{\mathbf P^n}\) is not a successive extension of invertible sheaves: if it were, with quotients \(\mathcal O(a_i)\) (Pic \(\mathbf P^n=\Z\)), the Chern polynomial (App. A, Sect. 3: the Chow ring of \(\mathbf P^n\) is \(\Z[H]/(H^{n+1})\), and \(c\) is multiplicative on exact sequences) would give

\[ \prod_{i=1}^n(1+a_iH)=c(\Omega)=(1-H)^{n+1}\quad\text{in }\Z[H]/(H^{n+1}),\]

i.e. \(\prod(1+a_it)=(1-t)^{n+1}-(-t)^{n+1}\) as polynomials in \(t\) (both sides have degree \(\le n\)). Its roots satisfy \(\frac{1-t}{-t}=\zeta\) with \(\zeta^{n+1}=1\), \(\zeta\ne1\), i.e. \(t=1/(1-\zeta)\), and for \(n\ge2\) there are \(\zeta\neq\pm1\) giving non-real roots; but the left side has only the real roots \(-1/a_i\). This contradiction shows that the statement of (a) fails in every dimension \(\ge2\) (for \(\mathbf P^n\)). ∎

Exercise 2.4

Problem

Let \(C\) be a curve of genus \(g\), and let \(X\) be the ruled surface \(C\times\mathbf P^1\). We consider the question, for what integers \(s\in\Z\) does there exist a section \(D\) of \(X\) with \(D^2=s\)? First show that \(s\) is always an even integer, say \(s=2r\).

  1. (a)

    Show that \(r=0\) and any \(r\ge g+1\) are always possible. Cf. (IV, Ex. 6.8).

  2. (b)

    If \(g=3\), show that \(r=1\) is not possible, and just one of the two values \(r=2,3\) is possible, depending on whether \(C\) is hyperelliptic or not.

Solution

Here \(X=\mathbf P(\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal O)\), \(e=0\), and we may take \(C_0=C\times\{\operatorname{pt}\}\), \(C_0^2=0\), \(C_0.f=1\), \(f^2=0\); by (2.3), \(\operatorname{Num}X=\Z C_0\oplus\Z f\). A section is the image \(D=\sigma(C)\) of a morphism \(\sigma\) with \(\pi\sigma=\operatorname{id}\), i.e. \(\sigma=(\operatorname{id},\varphi)\) with \(\varphi\colon C\to\mathbf P^1\) a morphism, and \(D\) is the graph of \(\varphi\). Let \(n=\deg\varphi\) (with \(n=0\) if \(\varphi\) is constant). Then \(D\equiv aC_0+bf\) with \(a=D.f=1\), and, using (1.3) (as \(D\cong C\) is nonsingular) and \(\mathcal L(C_0)\cong p_2^*\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(1)\):

\[ b=D.C_0=\deg_C\bigl(\sigma^*p_2^*\mathcal O(1)\bigr)=\deg\varphi^*\mathcal O(1)=n. \]

Hence \(D\equiv C_0+nf\) and \(D^2=2n\). So \(s=2n\) is even, and

\[ s=2r\ \text{is attained for a section }D\iff r=0\text{ or there is a morphism }C\to\mathbf P^1\text{ of degree }r. \]

(Conversely every morphism \(\varphi\) of degree \(r\) gives a section with \(D^2=2r\), \(r=0\) for constant \(\varphi\).)

(a) \(r=0\): a constant \(\varphi\), e.g. \(D=C_0\). Now let \(r\ge g+1\). We must produce a morphism \(C\to\mathbf P^1\) of degree \(r\), i.e. an invertible sheaf \(\mathcal L\) of degree \(r\) together with two sections without common zero. This is the content of (IV, Ex. 6.8); for completeness here is a direct argument, using only Riemann–Roch. It suffices to find a divisor \(\Delta\) of degree \(r\) with \(h^0(\Delta)\ge2\) such that \(|\Delta|\) has no base points.

Step 1. Choose points \(P_1,\dots,P_g\) one after the other such that \(P_{i+1}\) is not a base point of the linear system \(|K-P_1-\dots-P_i|\) (nonempty as long as \(i<g\), and with finitely many base points); then \(h^0\) drops by exactly \(1\) at each step, so \(\Delta'=P_1+\dots+P_g\) satisfies \(h^0(K-\Delta')=g-g=0\) (\(\Delta'=0\) if \(g=0\)). By Riemann–Roch \(h^0(\Delta')=1\), i.e. \(|\Delta'|=\{\Delta'\}\).

Step 2. For a point \(P\) the divisor \(K-\Delta'+P\) has degree \(g-1\), so Riemann–Roch gives \(h^0(K-\Delta'+P)=h^0(\Delta'-P)\). This is \(0\) if \(P\notin\{P_1,\dots,P_g\}\) (as \(|\Delta'|=\{\Delta'\}\)), and \(1\) if \(P=P_i\) (since \(\Delta'-P_i\ge0\) and \(h^0(\Delta'-P_i)\le h^0(\Delta')=1\)); in the latter case \(|K-\Delta'+P_i|=\{E_i\}\) for a unique effective divisor \(E_i\). Choose a point \(Q_1\notin E_1\cup\dots\cup E_g\) and arbitrary points \(Q_2,\dots,Q_{r-g}\) (note \(r-g\ge1\)), and put \(\Delta=\Delta'+Q_1+\dots+Q_{r-g}\), of degree \(r\).

Step 3: \(|\Delta|\) has no base points and \(h^0(\Delta)\ge2\). Since \(h^0(K-\Delta)\le h^0(K-\Delta')=0\), Riemann–Roch gives \(h^0(\Delta)=r+1-g\ge2\). For any point \(P\) we claim \(h^0(K-\Delta+P)=0\): if \(P\notin\{P_i\}\) this follows from \(h^0(K-\Delta'+P)=0\); if \(P=P_i\), a nonzero section of \(\mathcal L(K-\Delta+P_i)\) would give an effective divisor in \(|K-\Delta'+P_i|=\{E_i\}\) containing \(Q_1\), contradicting the choice of \(Q_1\). Hence by Riemann–Roch \(h^0(\Delta-P)=(r-1)+1-g=h^0(\Delta)-1\) for every \(P\), so no point is a base point of \(|\Delta|\).

Put \(L=\mathcal L(\Delta)\) and \(V=H^0(L)\), \(\dim V\ge2\), base-point-free. Pick \(s_1\ne0\) with zeros \(R_1,\dots,R_q\) (finitely many). Each \(V(-R_i)=\{s: s(R_i)=0\}\) is a proper subspace, so, \(k\) being infinite, there is \(s_2\in V\setminus\bigcup_iV(-R_i)\). Then \(s_1,s_2\) have no common zero and \(\varphi=(s_1:s_2)\colon C\to\mathbf P^1\) has degree \(\deg L=r\). Hence \(r\) occurs.

(b) Let \(g=3\). A morphism of degree 1 to \(\mathbf P^1\) is an isomorphism (a birational finite morphism of nonsingular curves), impossible as \(g=3>0\). So \(r=1\) does not occur. By definition (IV, Sect. 5), \(C\) is hyperelliptic iff there is a degree 2 morphism \(C\to\mathbf P^1\), so \(r=2\) occurs iff \(C\) is hyperelliptic. It remains to show: \(r=3\) occurs iff \(C\) is not hyperelliptic.

\(C\) not hyperelliptic \(\Rightarrow\) \(r=3\) occurs. Fix \(P\in C\). Since \(\deg K=4\) and \(h^0(K)=3\), Riemann–Roch gives \(h^0(K-P)=h^0(P)-1-1+3=2\) (here \(h^0(P)=1\) as \(g>0\)). If \(Q\) were a base point of \(|K-P|\), then \(h^0(K-P-Q)=2\); but Riemann–Roch gives \(h^0(K-P-Q)=h^0(P+Q)-2-1+3=h^0(P+Q)\), and \(h^0(P+Q)=1\): indeed if \(h^0(P+Q)\ge2\), then \(|P+Q|\) has no base point (a base point \(R\) would give \(h^0(P+Q-R)\ge2\) for a divisor of degree 1, forcing \(C\cong\mathbf P^1\)), so it defines a morphism of degree 2 to \(\mathbf P^1\) and \(C\) would be hyperelliptic. Hence \(|K-P|\) has no base points, and so \(|K-P|\) is a base-point-free pencil of degree \(3\), giving a morphism \(C\to\mathbf P^1\) of degree \(3\).

\(C\) hyperelliptic \(\Rightarrow\) \(r=3\) does not occur. Let \(\pi\colon C\to\mathbf P^1\) have degree 2 and let \(h=\pi^*\mathcal O(1)\) (degree 2). The pullbacks of the three independent sections of \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(2)\) are independent sections of \(2h\), so \(h^0(2h)\ge3\); by Riemann–Roch \(h^0(2h)=4+1-3+h^0(K-2h)\) and \(\deg(K-2h)=0\), so \(K\sim2h\) and \(H^0(K)=\pi^*H^0(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(2))\) (both of dimension 3). Suppose \(\psi\colon C\to\mathbf P^1\) has degree 3 and put \(L=\psi^*\mathcal O(1)\): it has degree 3 and \(|L|\) has no base points. Since \(\deg(K-L)=1\) we have \(h^0(K-L)\le1\), so \(h^0(L)=3+1-3+h^0(K-L)\le2\); hence \(h^0(L)=2\) and \(K-L\sim Q\) for a point \(Q\): \(L\sim K-Q\). But every section of \(K=\pi^*\mathcal O(2)\) vanishing at \(Q\) has the form \(\pi^*s\) with \(s(\pi(Q))=0\), so it vanishes on the whole divisor \(\pi^*(\pi(Q))=Q+Q'\) (\(Q'\) the conjugate point, \(Q'=Q\) if \(Q\) is ramified). Therefore \(Q'\) is a base point of \(|K-Q|\), contradicting that \(|L|=|K-Q|\) has no base points. So no degree \(3\) morphism exists.

Thus for \(g=3\): \(r=0\), \(r\ge4\) always occur, \(r=1\) never, and exactly one of \(r=2\) (hyperelliptic) and \(r=3\) (non-hyperelliptic) occurs. ∎

Exercise 2.5

Problem

Values of \(e\). Let \(C\) be a curve of genus \(g\ge1\).

  1. (a)

    Show that for each \(0\le e\le 2g-2\) there is a ruled surface \(X\) over \(C\) with invariant \(e\), corresponding to an indecomposable \(\mathcal E\).

  2. (b)

    Let \(e<0\), let \(D\) be a divisor of degree \(d=-e\), and let \(\xi\in H^1(\mathcal L(-D))\) be a nonzero element defining an extension

    \[ 0\to\mathcal O_C\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(D)\to0. \]

    Let \(H\subseteq|D+K|\) be the sublinear system of codimension 1 defined by \(\ker\xi\), where \(\xi\) is considered as a linear functional on \(H^0(\mathcal L(D+K))\). For any effective divisor \(E\) of degree \(d-1\), let \(L_E\subseteq|D+K|\) be the sublinear system \(|D+K-E|+E\). Show that \(\mathcal E\) is normalized if and only if for each \(E\) as above, \(L_E\not\subseteq H\). Cf. proof of (2.15).

  3. (c)

    Now show that if \(-g\le e<0\), there exists a ruled surface \(X\) over \(C\) with invariant \(e\). [Hint: For any given \(D\) in (b), show that a suitable \(\xi\) exists, using an argument similar to the proof of (II, 8.18).]

  4. (d)

    For \(g=2\), show that \(e\ge-2\) is also necessary for the existence of \(X\).

Note. It has been shown that \(e\ge-g\) for any ruled surface (Nagata [8]).

Solution

In (b) the letter \(E\) denotes an effective divisor, as in the book; to avoid confusion with the sheaf \(\mathcal E\), we write \(Z\) for it in the proof. Recall that \(\mathcal E\) is normalized if \(H^0(\mathcal E)\ne0\) and \(H^0(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M)=0\) for all invertible \(\mathcal M\) of negative degree, and then \(e=-\deg\mathcal E\).

(a) Let \(0\le e\le2g-2\). Choose an effective divisor \(Z_0\) of degree \(2g-2-e\ge0\) and let \(\mathcal N=\mathcal L(Z_0-K)\) be the invertible sheaf of the divisor \(Z_0-K\), so \(\deg\mathcal N=-(2g-2)+(2g-2-e)=-e\) and \(\mathcal N^{-1}=\mathcal L(K-Z_0)\). By Serre duality \(H^1(\mathcal N^{-1})=H^1(\mathcal L(K-Z_0))\cong H^0(\mathcal L(Z_0))^\vee\ne0\). Take \(0\ne\xi\in H^1(\mathcal N^{-1})=\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal N,\mathcal O_C)\) and the corresponding nonsplit extension

\[ 0\to\mathcal O_C\xrightarrow{\ j\ }\mathcal E\to\mathcal N\to0 . \]

\(\mathcal E\) is normalized: \(H^0(\mathcal E)\supseteq H^0(\mathcal O_C)\ne0\); if \(\deg\mathcal M<0\) then \(H^0(\mathcal M)=0\) and \(H^0(\mathcal N\otimes\mathcal M)=0\) (degree \(-e+\deg\mathcal M<0\)), so \(H^0(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M)=0\). Since \(\deg\mathcal E=\deg\mathcal N=-e\), the invariant is \(e\).

\(\mathcal E\) is indecomposable: suppose \(\mathcal E\cong\mathcal A\oplus\mathcal B\) with \(\mathcal A,\mathcal B\) invertible. One of the components of \(j\) is nonzero, say \(j_A\colon\mathcal O\to\mathcal A\); then \(\deg\mathcal A\ge0\). On the other hand \(\deg\mathcal A\le0\) since otherwise \(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal A^{-1}\supseteq\mathcal A\otimes\mathcal A^{-1}=\mathcal O\) would have a nonzero section with \(\deg\mathcal A^{-1}<0\), contradicting normalization. So \(\deg\mathcal A=0\) and the nonzero map \(j_A\) between invertible sheaves of degree \(0\) is an isomorphism. Then \(j_A^{-1}\circ\operatorname{pr}_A\) is a retraction of \(j\) and the extension splits, contradicting \(\xi\ne0\).

(b) We have \(\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal L(D),\mathcal O)=H^1(\mathcal L(-D))\) and, by Serre duality, \(H^1(\mathcal L(-D))\cong H^0(\mathcal L(D+K))^\vee\); this identification is meant in the statement. Note \(\deg(D+K)>2g-2\), so \(\dim V=g+d-1\) for \(V=H^0(\mathcal L(D+K))\).

First, \(H^0(\mathcal E)\ne0\) always. Moreover if \(H^0(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M)\ne0\) for some \(\mathcal M\) with \(\deg\mathcal M<0\), choose an effective divisor \(Z''\) with \(\deg\mathcal M(Z'')=-1\); multiplication by the section \(1_{Z''}\) of \(\mathcal L(Z'')\) embeds \(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M\hookrightarrow\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M(Z'')\), so \(H^0(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M(Z''))\ne0\). Hence \(\mathcal E\) is normalized iff \(H^0(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M)=0\) for all invertible \(\mathcal M\) of degree \(-1\).

Fix \(\mathcal M\) of degree \(-1\). From \(0\to\mathcal M\to\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M\to\mathcal L(D)\otimes\mathcal M\to0\) and \(H^0(\mathcal M)=0\) we get

\[ H^0(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M)=\ker\bigl(\delta_{\mathcal M}\colon H^0(\mathcal L(D)\otimes\mathcal M)\to H^1(\mathcal M)\bigr),\qquad \delta_{\mathcal M}(s)=s\cup\xi , \]

the cup product with \(\xi\in H^1(\mathcal L(-D))\). Now \(\mathcal L(D)\otimes\mathcal M\) has degree \(d-1\ge0\). A nonzero \(s\in H^0(\mathcal L(D)\otimes\mathcal M)\) has an effective divisor of zeros \(Z\) of degree \(d-1\), and \(\mathcal M\cong\mathcal L(Z-D)\), \(s\) corresponding to the canonical section of \(\mathcal L(Z)\). Under this identification \(s\cup\xi\) is the image of \(\xi\) under the map \(\iota_Z\colon H^1(\mathcal L(-D))\to H^1(\mathcal L(Z-D))\) induced by the inclusion \(\mathcal L(-D)\subseteq\mathcal L(Z-D)\). Conversely every effective \(Z\) of degree \(d-1\) arises (with \(\mathcal M=\mathcal L(Z-D)\)). Hence

\[ \mathcal E\text{ is not normalized}\iff\exists\,Z\ge0,\ \deg Z=d-1,\ \iota_Z(\xi)=0 . \]

By Serre duality (functorial), \(\iota_Z\) is dual to the inclusion \(H^0(\mathcal L(D+K-Z))\hookrightarrow H^0(\mathcal L(D+K))\) (multiplication by the canonical section of \(\mathcal L(Z)\)), whose image is the subspace \(V_Z\) of sections vanishing on \(Z\), projectivizing to \(L_Z\). So \(\iota_Z(\xi)=0\) iff \(\xi\) annihilates \(V_Z\), i.e. iff \(V_Z\subseteq\ker\xi\), i.e. iff \(L_Z\subseteq H\). This proves (b). (Note \(H\) is a hyperplane because \(\xi\ne0\).)

(c) Let \(-g\le e<0\), \(d=-e\in\{1,\dots,g\}\), and fix any divisor \(D\) of degree \(d\). By (b) we must find \(0\ne\xi\in V^\vee\), \(V=H^0(\mathcal L(D+K))\) (\(\dim V=g+d-1\)), such that \(\xi\) does not annihilate \(V_Z\) for any effective \(Z\) of degree \(d-1\). We have \(\deg(D+K-Z)=2g-1>2g-2\), so \(H^1(\mathcal L(D+K-Z))=0\) and \(\dim V_Z=g\) for every \(Z\); the annihilator \(\Lambda_Z=\operatorname{Ann}(V_Z)\subseteq V^\vee\) has dimension \(d-1\). We must show that \(\bigcup_Z\Lambda_Z\ne V^\vee\) (then a vector \(\xi\notin\bigcup\Lambda_Z\) is automatically nonzero because \(0\in\Lambda_Z\)).

Let \(T=C^{d-1}\) (ordered \((d-1)\)-tuples, \(T=\operatorname{pt}\) if \(d=1\)), \(\Delta\subseteq T\times C\) the divisor \(\sum_i\{y=P_i\}\), finite and flat of degree \(d-1\) over \(T\), and \(\mathcal N=p_2^*\mathcal L(D+K)\). Consider \(0\to\mathcal N(-\Delta)\to\mathcal N\to\mathcal N|_\Delta\to0\) and apply \(p_{1*}\). Since \(H^1(\mathcal L(D+K-Z))=0\) for all \(Z\), cohomology and base change (III, 12.11) shows that \(R^1p_{1*}\mathcal N(-\Delta)=0\) and \(p_{1*}\mathcal N(-\Delta)\) is locally free with fibres \(V_Z\). We get an exact sequence of vector bundles on \(T\)

\[ 0\to p_{1*}\mathcal N(-\Delta)\to V\otimes\mathcal O_T\to\mathcal G\to0,\qquad\mathcal G=p_{1*}(\mathcal N|_\Delta)\ \text{of rank }d-1 . \]

Dualizing, \(\mathcal G^\vee\subseteq V^\vee\otimes\mathcal O_T\) is a subbundle of rank \(d-1\) with fibre \(\Lambda_Z\) at \(Z=\sum P_i\). Its total space \(\mathbf V(\mathcal G^\vee)\) has dimension \((d-1)+(d-1)=2d-2\) and maps to \(V^\vee\) with image \(\bigcup_Z\Lambda_Z\). This image is constructible of dimension \(\le2d-2\), while \(\dim V^\vee=g+d-1\). As \(d\le g\) we have \(2d-2\le g+d-2<g+d-1\), so the image is a proper subset of \(V^\vee\), and a suitable \(\xi\ne0\) exists. By (b) the corresponding \(\mathcal E\) is normalized, with \(\deg\mathcal E=\deg\mathcal L(D)=d\), so \(\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\) is a ruled surface with invariant \(e=-d\).

(d) Let \(g=2\). By (a) and (c) the values \(e\ge0\) and \(-2\le e<0\) do occur. We show \(e\le-3\) is impossible. Suppose a ruled surface \(X=\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\), \(\mathcal E\) normalized, with \(e=-d<0\) exists.

Step 1: \(\mathcal E\) is a nonsplit extension \(0\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(D)\to0\) with \(\deg D=d\). By the proof of (2.8) there is such a sequence (\(\mathcal L(D)=\bigwedge^2\mathcal E\)); it is nonsplit because \(e<0\) and a decomposable normalized \(\mathcal E\) has \(e\ge0\) by (2.12)(a).

Step 2: if \(d\ge2\) then there is a normalized \(\mathcal E'\) with \(e'=e+1\). Choose a point \(Q\) and let \(\mathcal E'\subseteq\mathcal E\) be the inverse image of \(\mathcal L(D-Q)\subseteq\mathcal L(D)\). Then \(0\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal E'\to\mathcal L(D-Q)\to0\) is exact, \(H^0(\mathcal E')\supseteq H^0(\mathcal O)\ne0\), and \(H^0(\mathcal E'\otimes\mathcal M)\subseteq H^0(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M)=0\) if \(\deg\mathcal M<0\); so \(\mathcal E'\) is normalized with \(\deg\mathcal E'=d-1\), i.e. \(e'=-(d-1)=e+1\).

By Step 2, if \(e\le-3\) occurs, then \(e=-3\) occurs. So it suffices to exclude \(e=-3\), i.e. \(d=3\).

Step 3: \(d=3\) is impossible. Let \(\mathcal E\) be as in Step 1 with \(d=3\); \(\deg(D+K)=5=2g+1\), so \(|D+K|\) is very ample (IV, 3.2) and embeds \(C\) in \(\mathbf P(V^\vee)=\mathbf P^3\) (\(\dim V=g+d-1=4\)) as a curve of degree \(5\); the hyperplane sections are the divisors of \(|D+K|\). The functional \(\xi\in V^\vee\setminus0\) is a point \(p\in\mathbf P^3\). For an effective divisor \(Z\) of degree \(d-1=2\), \(\Lambda_Z=\operatorname{Ann}(V_Z)\) is the line \(\overline Z\) spanned by \(Z\) in \(\mathbf P^3\) (a secant line if \(Z=Q+Q'\), \(Q\ne Q'\), the tangent line at \(Q\) if \(Z=2Q\)): the hyperplanes containing \(Z\) are those containing the line. By the argument in (b)/(c), \(\mathcal E\) is normalized iff \(p\notin\overline Z\) for all \(Z\) of degree 2. We show every point \(p\in\mathbf P^3\) lies on such a line.

If \(p\in C\), take \(Z=p+Q\) with \(Q\in C\) arbitrary. Suppose \(p\notin C\) lies on no line \(\overline Z\). Let \(W=\ker\xi\subseteq V\) (the sections whose hyperplane passes through \(p\), \(\dim W=3\)); it defines the projection \(\pi_p\colon C\to\mathbf P^2\) from \(p\). For \(Z\) of degree 2, \(W\to V/V_Z\) (\(\dim V/V_Z=2\)) is surjective iff \(V_Z\not\subseteq W\) iff \(\xi\notin\Lambda_Z\) iff \(p\notin\overline Z\). By assumption this holds for all \(Z\) of degree 2; taking \(Z=Q+Q'\) with \(Q\ne Q'\) and \(Z=2Q\) we see (as \(V\to H^0(\mathcal L(D+K)|_Z)\) is onto with kernel \(V_Z\), so that \(W\to H^0(\mathcal L(D+K)|_Z)\) is onto) that \(W\) is base-point-free and separates points and tangent vectors. By the criterion (II, 7.8.2) \(\pi_p\) is a closed immersion, so \(C\) would be isomorphic to a nonsingular plane curve of degree 5 (degree \(=\deg(D+K)=5\)). But a nonsingular plane curve of degree 5 has genus \(\frac12\cdot4\cdot3=6\ne2\) (I, Ex. 7.2). This contradiction shows that every \(p\in\mathbf P^3\setminus C\) also lies on some line \(\overline Z\).

Hence for every \(\xi\ne0\) the sheaf \(\mathcal E\) fails to be normalized, so no ruled surface over a genus 2 curve has \(e=-3\), and consequently none has \(e\le-3\). Therefore \(e\ge-2\) is necessary (and, by (c), sufficient). ∎

Exercise 2.6

Problem

Show that every locally free sheaf of finite rank on \(\mathbf P^1\) is isomorphic to a direct sum of invertible sheaves. [Hint: Choose a subinvertible sheaf of maximal degree, and use induction on the rank.]

Solution

Let \(\mathcal E\) be locally free of rank \(r\) on \(\mathbf P^1\); induct on \(r\), the case \(r=1\) being trivial. Recall \(\operatorname{Pic}\mathbf P^1=\{\mathcal O(n)\}\) and \(\deg\mathcal O(n)=n\).

There is a maximal degree. For \(m\gg0\), \(\mathcal E^\vee(m)\) is generated by finitely many global sections, so there is a surjection \(\mathcal O^N\to\mathcal E^\vee(m)\); dualizing, \(\mathcal E(-m)\hookrightarrow\mathcal O^N\). Hence \(H^0(\mathcal E(-m-1))\subseteq H^0(\mathcal O(-1)^N)=0\). Thus there is no nonzero map \(\mathcal O(c)\to\mathcal E\) for \(c>m\). On the other hand \(\mathcal E(-c)\) is generated by global sections for \(-c\gg0\), so such maps exist for some \(c\). Let \(a\) be the largest integer with \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal O(a),\mathcal E)\ne0\), and fix a nonzero map \(\mathcal O(a)\to\mathcal E\); it is injective since \(\mathcal O(a)\) is torsion-free of rank 1 and \(\mathbf P^1\) is integral. Put \(\mathcal L=\mathcal O(a)\).

\(\mathcal Q=\mathcal E/\mathcal L\) is locally free. If \(\mathcal Q\) had torsion, the inverse image \(\mathcal L'\subseteq\mathcal E\) of the torsion subsheaf would be torsion-free of rank 1, hence invertible, \(\mathcal L\subsetneq\mathcal L'\), \(\mathcal L'\cong\mathcal O(c)\) with \(c>a\) (the quotient \(\mathcal L'/\mathcal L\) has finite positive length), contradicting maximality of \(a\). So \(\mathcal Q\) is torsion-free, hence locally free (the local rings are DVRs), of rank \(r-1\), and by induction \(\mathcal Q\cong\bigoplus_{i=2}^r\mathcal O(a_i)\).

All \(a_i\le a\). Twist \(0\to\mathcal L\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal Q\to0\) by \(\mathcal O(-a-1)\): since \(H^1(\mathcal O(-1))=0\), the map \(H^0(\mathcal E(-a-1))\to H^0(\mathcal Q(-a-1))=\bigoplus_iH^0(\mathcal O(a_i-a-1))\) is surjective. If some \(a_i\ge a+1\), the right side is nonzero, so \(H^0(\mathcal E(-a-1))\ne0\), i.e. there is a nonzero map \(\mathcal O(a+1)\to\mathcal E\), contradicting the choice of \(a\).

The sequence splits. It is classified by an element of \(\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal Q,\mathcal L)=\bigoplus_iH^1(\mathbf P^1,\mathcal O(a-a_i))\), and \(a-a_i\ge0\) gives \(H^1=0\) (II or III, 5.1). So \(\mathcal E\cong\mathcal L\oplus\mathcal Q\cong\mathcal O(a)\oplus\bigoplus_{i\ge2}\mathcal O(a_i)\). ∎

Exercise 2.7

Problem

On the elliptic ruled surface \(X\) of (2.11.6), show that the sections \(C_0\) with \(C_0^2=1\) form a one-dimensional algebraic family, parametrized by the points of the base curve \(C\), and that no two of them are linearly equivalent.

Solution

Recall (2.11.6): \(C\) is an elliptic curve, \(P\in C\), and \(\mathcal E\) is the nonsplit extension \(0\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal E\xrightarrow{q}\mathcal L(P)\to0\) given by \(0\ne\xi\in H^1(\mathcal L(-P))\) (\(\dim=1\)); \(\mathcal E\) is normalized with \(e=-1\), \(\bigwedge^2\mathcal E=\mathcal L(P)\), \(\mathfrak e\sim P\), \(C_0^2=-e=1\).

Step 1: description of the sections with \(D^2=1\). A section \(D\) has \(D\equiv C_0+mf\) (since \(D.f=1\)), so \(D.C_0=C_0^2+m=1+m\) and \(D^2=1+2m\). By (2.9) \(D.C_0=\deg\mathcal M\), where \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal M\to0\) is the quotient corresponding to \(D\). Hence \(D^2=1\) iff \(m=0\) iff \(\deg\mathcal M=1\) iff \(\mathcal M\cong\mathcal L(Q)\) for a (unique) point \(Q\in C\) (IV, 1.3.7 / \(\operatorname{Pic}^1C\cong C\) on an elliptic curve). So we must study surjections \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(Q)\).

Step 2: \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal E,\mathcal L(Q))\) is one-dimensional for each \(Q\in C\), and every nonzero element is surjective. Apply \(\operatorname{Hom}(-,\mathcal M)\), \(\mathcal M=\mathcal L(Q)\), to the defining sequence:

\[ 0\to\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal L(P),\mathcal M)\to\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal E,\mathcal M)\to H^0(\mathcal M)\xrightarrow{\ \partial\ }H^1(\mathcal M(-P)),\qquad\partial(t)=t\cup\xi . \]

Here \(h^0(\mathcal M)=1\) and the first term is \(H^0(\mathcal L(Q-P))\).

  • •

    If \(Q\ne P\): \(\mathcal L(Q-P)\) is of degree 0 and nontrivial, so \(H^0(\mathcal L(Q-P))=0=H^1(\mathcal L(Q-P))\), and \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal E,\mathcal M)\cong H^0(\mathcal M)\) is one-dimensional. A generator \(\varphi\) restricts on \(\mathcal O\subseteq\mathcal E\) to the section \(t_Q\) of \(\mathcal L(Q)\) with divisor \(Q\). So \(\varphi(y)\ne0\) at every \(y\ne Q\). If \(\varphi(Q)\) were \(0\), \(\varphi\) would factor through \(\mathcal M(-Q)=\mathcal O\), a nonzero element of \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal E,\mathcal O)\). But \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal E,\mathcal O)=0\): from the sequence, it injects into \(\ker(H^0(\mathcal O)\to H^1(\mathcal L(-P)),1\mapsto\xi)=0\), as \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal L(P),\mathcal O)=H^0(\mathcal L(-P))=0\). So \(\varphi\) is surjective.

  • •

    If \(Q=P\): \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal L(P),\mathcal L(P))=k\) gives the quotient \(q\). The map \(\partial\) sends the section \(1_P\) of \(\mathcal L(P)\) to the image of \(\xi\) under \(H^1(\mathcal L(-P))\to H^1(\mathcal O)\) induced by \(\mathcal L(-P)\subseteq\mathcal O\); this map is injective (its kernel is the image of \(H^0(\mathcal O_P)\), which is zero as \(H^0(\mathcal O)\to H^0(\mathcal O_P)\) is onto). So \(\partial\) is injective and \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal E,\mathcal L(P))=k\cdot q\).

Thus for each \(Q\in C\) there is, up to a scalar, exactly one surjection \(\varphi_Q\colon\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(Q)\), hence exactly one section \(\sigma_Q\) and one curve \(D_Q=\sigma_Q(C)\cong C\) with \(D_Q^2=1\) (and \(D_P=C_0\)). By Step 1 every section with self-intersection \(1\) is one of the \(D_Q\), and \(D_Q\ne D_{Q'}\) for \(Q\ne Q'\) (the sheaf \(\mathcal L(Q)=\sigma_Q^*\mathcal O_X(1)\) is determined by \(D_Q\)). So \(Q\mapsto D_Q\) is a bijection from \(C\) onto the set of sections with \(D^2=1\).

Step 3: they form an algebraic family. On \(C\times C\) (coordinates \((Q,y)\), projections \(p_1,p_2\), diagonal \(\Delta\)) consider \(\mathcal H=p_{1*}\mathcal{H}om(p_2^*\mathcal E,\mathcal L(\Delta))\), whose fibre over \(Q\) should be \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal E,\mathcal L(Q))\). We have \(H^1(\mathcal E^\vee(Q))=0\) for every \(Q\): indeed \(\mathcal E^\vee\cong\mathcal E\otimes(\bigwedge^2\mathcal E)^{-1}=\mathcal E(-P)\), and from \(0\to\mathcal L(Q-P)\to\mathcal E(Q-P)\to\mathcal L(Q)\to0\) we get \(H^1(\mathcal E(Q-P))=0\) when \(Q\ne P\) (both ends have vanishing \(H^1\)), while for \(Q=P\) the sequence \(H^0(\mathcal L(P))\to H^1(\mathcal O)\to H^1(\mathcal E)\to H^1(\mathcal L(P))=0\) and the isomorphism \(H^0(\mathcal L(P))\to H^1(\mathcal O)\) (\(1_P\mapsto\) image of \(\xi\ne0\), as above) give \(H^1(\mathcal E)=0\). Hence by cohomology and base change (III, 12.11) \(\mathcal H\) is invertible with fibres \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal E,\mathcal L(Q))\). The tautological map \(p_1^*\mathcal H\otimes p_2^*\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(\Delta)\) is surjective on each fibre \(\{Q\}\times C\) by Step 2, hence surjective, and gives a surjection \(p_2^*\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(\Delta)\otimes p_1^*\mathcal H^{-1}\), i.e. (II, 7.12) a morphism \(\Sigma\colon C\times C\to X\) with \(\pi\circ\Sigma=p_2\) and \(\Sigma(Q,\cdot)=\sigma_Q\). The morphism \((p_1,\Sigma)\colon C\times C\to C\times X\) is a closed immersion with image a divisor \(\mathcal D\subseteq C\times X\), flat over \(C\) (via the first factor) with fibre \(D_Q\) over \(Q\). This is an algebraic family of curves on \(X\), parametrized by \(C\), whose members are exactly the sections with \(D^2=1\).

Step 4: no two are linearly equivalent. By (2.9), the section \(D_Q\) corresponding to the quotient \(\mathcal L(Q)\) satisfies \(D_Q\sim C_0+(Q-\mathfrak e)f\sim C_0+(Q-P)f\). If \(D_Q\sim D_{Q'}\) then \(\pi^*(Q-Q')\sim0\) on \(X\), so \(Q-Q'\sim0\) on \(C\) because \(\pi^*\colon\operatorname{Pic}C\to\operatorname{Pic}X\) is injective (2.3). Since \(g(C)=1\), \(Q=Q'\). ∎

Exercise 2.8

Problem

A locally free sheaf \(\mathcal E\) on a curve \(C\) is said to be stable if for every quotient locally free sheaf \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal F\to0\), \(\mathcal F\ne0\), \(\mathcal F\ne\mathcal E\), we have

\[ (\deg\mathcal F)/\operatorname{rank}\mathcal F>(\deg\mathcal E)/\operatorname{rank}\mathcal E. \]

Replacing \(>\) by \(\ge\) defines semistable.

  1. (a)

    A decomposable \(\mathcal E\) is never stable.

  2. (b)

    If \(\mathcal E\) has rank 2 and is normalized, then \(\mathcal E\) is stable (respectively, semistable) if and only if \(\deg\mathcal E>0\) (respectively, \(\ge0\)).

  3. (c)

    Show that the indecomposable locally free sheaves \(\mathcal E\) of rank 2 that are not semistable are classified, up to isomorphism, by giving (1) an integer \(0<e\le2g-2\), (2) an element \(\mathcal L\in\operatorname{Pic}C\) of degree \(-e\), and (3) a nonzero \(\xi\in H^1(\mathcal L^\vee)\), determined up to a nonzero scalar multiple.

Solution

Write \(\mu(\mathcal G)=\deg\mathcal G/\operatorname{rank}\mathcal G\).

(a) Let \(\mathcal E=\mathcal E_1\oplus\mathcal E_2\) with \(\mathcal E_i\ne0\) locally free. Then \(\mathcal E_1\), \(\mathcal E_2\) are locally free quotients of \(\mathcal E\), different from \(0\) and \(\mathcal E\), and \(\mu(\mathcal E)=\frac{r_1}{r_1+r_2}\mu(\mathcal E_1)+\frac{r_2}{r_1+r_2}\mu(\mathcal E_2)\) is a weighted average of \(\mu(\mathcal E_1),\mu(\mathcal E_2)\). So \(\mu(\mathcal E_1)\le\mu(\mathcal E)\) or \(\mu(\mathcal E_2)\le\mu(\mathcal E)\), and \(\mathcal E\) is not stable.

(b) Let \(\mathcal E\) have rank 2 and be normalized. A locally free quotient \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal F\) with \(\mathcal F\ne0,\mathcal E\) has rank 1 (if the rank were 2 the kernel would be a torsion-free sheaf of rank 0, i.e. zero, and \(\mathcal F=\mathcal E\)); it is invertible with invertible kernel \(\mathcal N\), \(\deg\mathcal N+\deg\mathcal F=\deg\mathcal E\). The condition for stability is \(\deg\mathcal F>\frac12\deg\mathcal E\) for all such quotients (semistability: \(\ge\)).

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Since \(\mathcal E\) is normalized, \(\deg\mathcal N\le0\): otherwise \(\mathcal N\otimes\mathcal N^{-1}=\mathcal O\subseteq\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal N^{-1}\) gives \(H^0(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal N^{-1})\ne0\) with \(\deg\mathcal N^{-1}<0\). Hence \(\deg\mathcal F\ge\deg\mathcal E\). If \(\deg\mathcal E>0\), then \(\deg\mathcal F\ge\deg\mathcal E>\frac12\deg\mathcal E\), so \(\mathcal E\) is stable; if \(\deg\mathcal E\ge0\) then \(\deg\mathcal F\ge\deg\mathcal E\ge\frac12\deg\mathcal E\), so \(\mathcal E\) is semistable.

(\(\Rightarrow\)) By the proof of (2.8), a nonzero section of \(\mathcal E\) gives an exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal F\to0\) with \(\mathcal F\) invertible (of degree \(\deg\mathcal E\)). If \(\mathcal E\) is stable, \(\deg\mathcal E=\deg\mathcal F>\frac12\deg\mathcal E\) so \(\deg\mathcal E>0\); if semistable, \(\deg\mathcal E\ge\frac12\deg\mathcal E\) so \(\deg\mathcal E\ge0\).

(c) Convention. Tensoring by an invertible sheaf does not change (in)decomposability or (semi)stability type of a rank-2 sheaf (if \(\mathcal F\) is a quotient of \(\mathcal E\) then \(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal M\) is a quotient of \(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M\) and \(\mu\) shifts by \(\deg\mathcal M\)), and \(\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\cong\mathbf P(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal M)\) (2.2). So the classification is understood up to twisting; we fix the twist by requiring (see below) that \(\mathcal E\) be normalized, as in (2.8).

Let \(\mathcal E\) be locally free of rank 2, not semistable.
