Then there is an invertible quotient \(\mathcal F\) with \(\deg\mathcal F<\frac12\deg\mathcal E\) and kernel \(\mathcal N\), an invertible subbundle with \(\deg\mathcal N>\frac12\deg\mathcal E\). Claim: every invertible subsheaf \(\mathcal N'\subseteq\mathcal E\) with \(\deg\mathcal N'>\frac12\deg\mathcal E\) is contained in \(\mathcal N\). Indeed, if \(\mathcal N'\not\subseteq\mathcal N\), the composite \(\mathcal N'\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal F\) is a nonzero map of invertible sheaves, so \(\deg\mathcal N'\le\deg\mathcal F<\frac12\deg\mathcal E\). If \(\mathcal N'\) is itself a subbundle contained in \(\mathcal N\), then \(\mathcal N/\mathcal N'\subseteq\mathcal E/\mathcal N'\) is a torsion subsheaf of a locally free sheaf, hence zero, so \(\mathcal N'=\mathcal N\). Thus \(\mathcal N=\mathcal N(\mathcal E)\) is the unique invertible subsheaf of maximal degree, and this subbundle is determined by \(\mathcal E\) (it is preserved by isomorphisms). Replacing \(\mathcal E\) by \(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal N^{-1}\) we may assume \(\mathcal N(\mathcal E)=\mathcal O\). Such \(\mathcal E\) are normalized: \(H^0(\mathcal E)\supseteq H^0(\mathcal O)\ne0\), and if \(\deg\mathcal M<0\) and \(\mathcal M^{-1}\to\mathcal E\) were a nonzero map (\(\deg\mathcal M^{-1}>0\)), it could not factor through \(\mathcal N=\mathcal O\) (no nonzero maps from positive degree to \(\mathcal O\)), so it maps nontrivially to \(\mathcal F=\mathcal E/\mathcal O\) of degree \(\deg\mathcal E<0\), again impossible. (Conversely, if \(\mathcal E\) is normalized and not semistable, then \(\deg\mathcal E<0\) by (b), the saturation of \(\mathcal O\hookrightarrow\mathcal E\) is an invertible subsheaf of degree \(\ge0\), hence \(0\) by normalization, hence equal to \(\mathcal O\), and it has degree \(0>\frac12\deg\mathcal E\); so \(\mathcal N(\mathcal E)=\mathcal O\).) Let \(\mathfrak N\) be the set of isomorphism classes of indecomposable locally free \(\mathcal E\) of rank 2 which are not semistable and satisfy \(\mathcal N(\mathcal E)\cong\mathcal O\).

The map \(\mathfrak N\to\{\text{triples}\}\). For \(\mathcal E\in\mathfrak N\) we have \(0\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal L\to0\) with \(\mathcal L=\mathcal E/\mathcal O\) invertible. Non-semistability gives \(0=\deg\mathcal N>\frac12\deg\mathcal E=\frac12\deg\mathcal L\), so \(e:=-\deg\mathcal L>0\). The sequence does not split (otherwise \(\mathcal E=\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal L\) is decomposable), so its class \(\xi\in\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal L,\mathcal O)=H^1(\mathcal L^\vee)\) is nonzero; then \(H^1(\mathcal L^\vee)\cong H^0(\mathcal L\otimes\omega_C)^\vee\ne0\) forces \(\deg(\mathcal L\otimes\omega_C)=2g-2-e\ge0\), i.e. \(e\le2g-2\). The triple \((e,\mathcal L,[\xi])\) is well defined: an isomorphism \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal E'\) in \(\mathfrak N\) carries \(\mathcal N=\mathcal O\) to \(\mathcal N'=\mathcal O\) by a scalar \(c\in k^*\) and induces an isomorphism \(\mathcal L\to\mathcal L'\); the classes of the two extensions then correspond up to the scalar \(c\) (standard property of extension classes under morphisms of extensions).

Injectivity: the iso class of an extension is determined by \((\mathcal L,[\xi])\) (up to scalar): two extensions with the same \(\mathcal L\) and proportional classes are isomorphic extensions.

Surjectivity: given \((e,\mathcal L,[\xi])\) with \(0<e\le2g-2\), \(\deg\mathcal L=-e\), \(0\ne\xi\in H^1(\mathcal L^\vee)\) (nonzero space, as above), let \(0\to\mathcal O\xrightarrow{j}\mathcal E\to\mathcal L\to0\) be the corresponding nonsplit extension. Then \(\mathcal O\subseteq\mathcal E\) is a subbundle of degree \(0>-\frac e2=\frac12\deg\mathcal E\), so \(\mathcal E\) is not semistable and, by the Claim (the invertible subsheaf \(\mathcal O\) has degree \(>\frac12\deg\mathcal E\) so lies in \(\mathcal N(\mathcal E)\), and a subbundle inside \(\mathcal N(\mathcal E)\) equals it), \(\mathcal N(\mathcal E)=\mathcal O\). If \(\mathcal E=\mathcal A\oplus\mathcal B\) were decomposable (invertible summands), one component of \(j\), say \(j_A\), would be nonzero, so \(\deg\mathcal A\ge0\); but \(\deg\mathcal A\le\deg\mathcal N(\mathcal E)=0\). So \(j_A\) is an isomorphism and \(j\) splits, contradicting \(\xi\ne0\). Hence \(\mathcal E\in\mathfrak N\) and maps to the given triple.

So the indecomposable non-semistable locally free sheaves of rank 2, normalized so that their maximal destabilizing subsheaf is \(\mathcal O\) (equivalently: normalized in the sense of (2.8), with \(e>0\)), are classified by \((e,\mathcal L,[\xi])\) as stated; an arbitrary indecomposable non-semistable \(\mathcal E\) is \(\mathcal E_0\otimes\mathcal N(\mathcal E)\) with \(\mathcal E_0\in\mathfrak N\), so is determined by the triple together with \(\mathcal N(\mathcal E)\in\operatorname{Pic}C\). (In the book's statement, (1)–(3) classify up to the twisting which does not change \(\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\).) ∎

Exercise 2.9

Problem

Let \(Y\) be a nonsingular curve on a quadric cone \(X_0\) in \(\mathbf P^3\). Show that either \(Y\) is a complete intersection of \(X_0\) with a surface of degree \(a\ge1\), in which case \(\deg Y=2a\), \(g(Y)=(a-1)^2\), or, \(\deg Y\) is odd, say \(2a+1\), and \(g(Y)=a^2-a\). Cf. (IV, 6.4.1). [Hint: Use (2.11.4).]

Solution

Let \(P_0\) be the vertex of \(X_0\), the cone over a conic. By (2.11.4) (with \(C=\) conic \(\cong\mathbf P^1\), \(d=2\)), the blow-up \(\mu\colon X\to X_0\) of \(P_0\) is the ruled surface \(X=\mathbf P(\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal O(-2))\) over \(\mathbf P^1\), which is normalized with \(e=2\); \(C_0=\mu^{-1}(P_0)\) is the section with \(C_0^2=-2\). Moreover \(\mu\) is an isomorphism \(X\setminus C_0\to X_0\setminus P_0\). We use \(\operatorname{Num}X=\Z C_0\oplus\Z f=\operatorname{Pic}X\) (as \(\operatorname{Pic}\mathbf P^1=\Z\)), \(C_0.f=1\), \(f^2=0\), and by (2.11) \(K\equiv-2C_0+(2\cdot0-2-2)f=-2C_0-4f\).

The hyperplane class. Let \(H\) be a plane section of \(X_0\) not through \(P_0\) (a conic) and \(\tilde H=\mu^*H\). Then \(\tilde H\equiv aC_0+bf\) with \(a=\tilde H.f=1\) (a plane meets a ruling once) and \(0=\tilde H.C_0=-2a+b\), so \(b=2\): \(\tilde H\sim C_0+2f\).

The strict transform. Let \(\tilde Y\subseteq X\) be the strict transform of \(Y\); it is the blow-up of \(Y\) in the closed subscheme \(Y\times_{X_0}P_0\) (II, 7.15), and if \(P_0\in Y\) this is the reduced point \(P_0\), a Cartier divisor on the nonsingular curve \(Y\), so \(\tilde Y\cong Y\); if \(P_0\notin Y\), also \(\tilde Y\cong Y\). Write \(\tilde Y\equiv aC_0+bf\). If \(\tilde Y=f\) (a line through the vertex) then \(Y\) is a line, \(\deg Y=1=2\cdot0+1\), \(g=0=0^2-0\), in agreement with the second alternative with \(a=0\). Otherwise \(\tilde Y\ne f\), and \(\tilde Y\ne C_0\) (it is not contracted), so \(a=\tilde Y.f>0\).

Degree and genus in terms of \(a,b\). \(\deg Y=\tilde Y.\tilde H=(aC_0+bf).(C_0+2f)=-2a+2a+b=b\). By adjunction (1.5), since \(\tilde Y\cong Y\) is nonsingular,

\[ 2g(Y)-2=\tilde Y.(\tilde Y+K)=(aC_0+bf).((a-2)C_0+(b-4)f)=-2a(a-2)+a(b-4)+b(a-2)=2ab-2a^2-2b, \]

so \(g(Y)=1+ab-a^2-b=(a-1)(b-a-1)\). Finally \(\tilde Y.C_0=b-2a\).

Claim: \(\tilde Y.C_0=0\) if \(P_0\notin Y\), and \(\tilde Y.C_0=1\) if \(P_0\in Y\). The first is clear since then \(\tilde Y\cap C_0=\emptyset\). Suppose \(P_0\in Y\). The inverse image ideal \(\mu^{-1}\mathfrak m_{P_0}\cdot\mathcal O_X\) is the ideal sheaf \(\mathcal O_X(-E')\) of the exceptional divisor \(E'=\operatorname{Proj}\bigoplus\mathfrak m^n/\mathfrak m^{n+1}\); the associated graded ring of the vertex is the homogeneous coordinate ring of the conic, a domain, so \(E'\) is the reduced irreducible curve \(C_0\): \(\mathcal O_X(-C_0)=\mathfrak m_{P_0}\mathcal O_X\). Restricting to \(\tilde Y\cong Y\): the map \(\mathcal O_X(-C_0)\otimes\mathcal O_{\tilde Y}\to\mathcal O_{\tilde Y}\) is injective (nonzero map of an invertible sheaf on an integral curve) with image \(\mathfrak m_{P_0}\mathcal O_Y=\mathfrak m_{Y,P_0}=\mathcal O_Y(-P_0)\), as \(Y\subseteq X_0\) is a closed subscheme. So \(\mathcal O_X(-C_0)|_{\tilde Y}\cong\mathcal O_Y(-P_0)\) has degree \(-1\), and by (1.3) \(\tilde Y.C_0=1\).

Case \(P_0\notin Y\): \(b=2a\), so \(\deg Y=2a\), \(g(Y)=(a-1)(a-1)=(a-1)^2\), with \(a\ge1\). We show \(Y\) is a complete intersection of \(X_0\) with a surface of degree \(a\). Since \(\tilde Y\sim aC_0+2af=a\tilde H\), \(\tilde Y\in|a\tilde H|\). Now \(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(a\tilde H))=H^0(\mathbf P^1,S^a(\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal O(-2))\otimes\mathcal O(2a))=\bigoplus_{i=0}^aH^0(\mathcal O(2a-2i))\) has dimension \(\sum_{i=0}^a(2a-2i+1)=(a+1)^2\). The restriction map \(H^0(\mathbf P^3,\mathcal O(a))\to H^0(X_0,\mathcal O_{X_0}(a))\) has kernel the degree \(a\) part of the ideal \((q)\) of \(X_0\), which has dimension \(\binom{a+1}{3}\), so its image has dimension \(\binom{a+3}3-\binom{a+1}3=(a+1)^2\). The pull-back \(\mu^*\colon H^0(X_0,\mathcal O(a))\to H^0(X,\mathcal O(a\tilde H))\) is injective (\(\mu\) is birational and \(X_0\) is integral), hence, comparing dimensions, every divisor in \(|a\tilde H|\) is of the form \(\operatorname{div}(\mu^*s)\) with \(s=G|_{X_0}\) for a form \(G\) of degree \(a\). For our \(\tilde Y\) this reads \(\tilde Y=\operatorname{div}(\mu^*s)\) with \(s=G|_{X_0}\). As \(\tilde Y\cap C_0=\emptyset\), the section \(\mu^*s\) has no zero on \(C_0\), so \(s(P_0)\ne0\) and \(\operatorname{div}(s)=X_0\cap\{G=0\}\) lies in \(X_0\setminus P_0\cong X\setminus C_0\), where it corresponds to the reduced curve \(\tilde Y\). Hence \(X_0\cap\{G=0\}=Y\) scheme-theoretically: \(Y\) is the complete intersection of \(X_0\) with the surface \(G=0\) of degree \(a\) (consistent with (IV, 6.4.1): \(\deg Y=2a\), \(g=1+\frac12\cdot2a\,(a+2-4)=(a-1)^2\)).

Case \(P_0\in Y\): \(b=2a+1\), so \(\deg Y=2a+1\) is odd and \(g(Y)=(a-1)(2a+1-a-1)=(a-1)a=a^2-a\). ∎

Exercise 2.10

Problem

For any \(n>e\ge0\), let \(X\) be the rational scroll of degree \(d=2n-e\) in \(\mathbf P^{d+1}\) given by (2.19). If \(n\ge2e-2\), show that \(X\) contains a nonsingular curve \(Y\) of genus \(g=d+2\) which is a canonical curve in this embedding. Conclude that for every \(g\ge4\), there exists a nonhyperelliptic curve of genus \(g\) which has a \(g^1_3\). Cf. (IV, Sect. 5).

Solution

Here \(X=X_e=\mathbf P(\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal O(-e))\) over \(\mathbf P^1\) with \(\operatorname{Pic}X=\Z C_0\oplus\Z f=\operatorname{Num}X\) (as \(\operatorname{Pic}\mathbf P^1=\Z\); (2.3)), \(C_0^2=-e\), and the embedding is given by \(|H|\), \(H=C_0+nf\), which is very ample since \(n>e\) (2.17(c)); \(\dim H^0(\mathcal L(H))=d+2\) (2.19). By (2.11), \(K\sim-2C_0-(e+2)f\).

The curve. Let \(\mathfrak D=H-K\sim3C_0+(n+e+2)f\). Put \(a=3\), \(b=n+e+2\). By Cor. 2.18(b), \(|aC_0+bf|\) contains an irreducible nonsingular curve if \(a>0\) and either \(b>ae\) or (\(e>0\) and \(b=ae\)). Here \(b-3e=n-2e+2\ge0\) by hypothesis \(n\ge2e-2\), with equality only if \(n=2e-2\); and in that case \(e>2\) (since \(n>e\)), in particular \(e>0\). If \(e=0\) then \(b=n+2>0=ae\). So in all cases Cor. 2.18(b) provides a nonsingular irreducible curve \(Y\in|\mathfrak D|\).

Genus. By adjunction (1.5), \(K_Y=(K+Y)|_Y\) and \(K+Y\sim K+H-K=H\). So \(K_Y\cong\mathcal O_X(H)|_Y=\mathcal O_Y(1)\). Moreover \(2g(Y)-2=Y.(Y+K)=Y.H=(3C_0+bf).(C_0+nf)=-3e+3n+b=4n-2e+2=2d+2\), so \(g(Y)=d+2\).

Canonical embedding. From \(0\to\mathcal O_X(H-Y)\to\mathcal O_X(H)\to\mathcal O_Y(H)\to0\) and \(H-Y\sim K\) we get

\[ 0\to H^0(\mathcal L(K))\to H^0(\mathcal L(H))\to H^0(\mathcal O_Y(1))\to H^1(\mathcal L(K))\ . \]

By (2.5), \(p_g(X)=\dim H^0(\mathcal L(K))=0\) and \(q=\dim H^1(\mathcal O_X)=g(\mathbf P^1)=0\), so by Serre duality \(H^1(\mathcal L(K))\cong H^1(\mathcal O_X)^\vee=0\). Hence \(H^0(X,\mathcal L(H))\to H^0(Y,\mathcal O_Y(1))=H^0(Y,K_Y)\) is an isomorphism (both have dimension \(d+2=g\)). Therefore the embedding \(Y\subseteq X\subseteq\mathbf P^{d+1}=\mathbf P^{g-1}\) is given by the complete canonical linear system \(|K_Y|\): \(Y\) is a canonical curve.

Conclusion. Since \(|K_Y|\) is very ample, \(Y\) is nonhyperelliptic (IV, 5.2). The projection \(\pi|_Y\colon Y\to\mathbf P^1\) has degree \(Y.f=3\), so \(Y\) has a \(g^1_3\). Which genera occur? \(g=d+2=2n-e+2\) with \(n>e\ge0\), \(n\ge2e-2\). For \(e=0\): \(n\ge1\) gives all even \(g=2n+2\ge4\). For \(e=1\): \(n\ge2\) (conditions \(n>1\), \(n\ge0\)) gives all odd \(g=2n+1\ge5\). So for every \(g\ge4\) there is a nonhyperelliptic curve of genus \(g\) with a \(g^1_3\) (a trigonal canonical curve lying on a rational normal scroll). ∎

Exercise 2.11

Problem

Let \(X\) be a ruled surface over the curve \(C\), defined by a normalized bundle \(\mathcal E\), and let \(\mathfrak e\) be the divisor on \(C\) for which \(\mathcal L(\mathfrak e)\cong\bigwedge^2\mathcal E\) (2.8.1). Let \(\mathfrak b\) be any divisor on \(C\).

  1. (a)

    If \(|\mathfrak b|\) and \(|\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e|\) have no base points, and if \(\mathfrak b\) is nonspecial, then there is a section \(D\sim C_0+\mathfrak bf\), and \(|D|\) has no base points.

  2. (b)

    If \(\mathfrak b\) and \(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e\) are very ample on \(C\), and for every point \(P\in C\), we have \(\mathfrak b-P\) and \(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e-P\) nonspecial, then \(C_0+\mathfrak bf\) is very ample.

Solution

Put \(\mathcal G=\mathcal E(\mathfrak b)=\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)\), \(F=\mathcal O_X(1)\otimes\pi^*\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)=\mathcal L(C_0+\mathfrak bf)\). By (II, 7.11) and the projection formula, \(\pi_*F=\mathcal G\) and \(H^0(X,F)=H^0(C,\mathcal G)\). Tensoring \(0\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(\mathfrak e)\to0\) with \(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)\):

\[ 0\to\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)\to\mathcal G\to\mathcal L(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e)\to0.\tag{1} \]

We use the following three lemmas (valid for any normalized \(\mathcal E\) and any divisor \(\mathfrak b\) on \(C\)); they are also used in Exercises 2.12, 2.13. Here “no base points” for a complete linear system \(|\mathfrak c|\) means that \(\mathcal L(\mathfrak c)\) is generated by global sections (in particular \(H^0\ne0\)), and “nonspecial” means \(H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak c))=0\).

Lemma A (sections). Assume \(H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))=0\), \(H^0(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))\ne0\) and \(|\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e|\) has no base points. Then there is a section \(D\sim C_0+\mathfrak bf\) of \(\pi\).

Proof. By (2.9), a section \(D\sim C_0+\mathfrak bf\) corresponds to a surjection \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal M=\mathcal L(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e)\) (then \(\mathcal M\cong\mathcal L(\mathfrak b')\) with \(D\sim C_0+(\mathfrak b'-\mathfrak e)f\sim C_0+\mathfrak bf\)). Let \(j\colon\mathcal O\to\mathcal E\), \(q\colon\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(\mathfrak e)\) be as in the sequence above. Since \(\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak e),\mathcal M)=H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))=0\), the map \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal E,\mathcal M)\to\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal O,\mathcal M)=H^0(\mathcal M)\), \(\psi\mapsto\psi\circ j\), is surjective. Let \(B\) be the (finite) base locus of \(|\mathfrak b|\). For each \(Q\in B\) the set \(\{t\in H^0(\mathcal M): t(Q)=0\}\) is a proper subspace (as \(|\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e|\) has no base points), so we can choose \(t\in H^0(\mathcal M)\) with \(t(Q)\ne0\) for all \(Q\in B\) (\(k\) is infinite); \(t\ne0\) and its zeros \(y_1,\dots,y_m\) are finitely many points, none in \(B\). Choose \(\psi_0\) with \(\psi_0\circ j=t\) and for \(u\in H^0(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))\) put \(\psi_u=\psi_0+\mu_u\circ q\), where \(\mu_u\colon\mathcal L(\mathfrak e)\to\mathcal M\) is multiplication by \(u\). Then \(\psi_u\circ j=t\). A map to an invertible sheaf is surjective iff it is nonzero on every fibre. At \(y\notin\{y_i\}\), \(\psi_u(j(1))=t(y)\ne0\). At \(y_i\), \(\psi_u(y_i)\) vanishes on the line \(j(\mathcal O(y_i))\) and factors through \(\mathcal L(\mathfrak e)(y_i)\); it is an element \(\alpha_i+u(y_i)\) of the one-dimensional space \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal L(\mathfrak e)(y_i),\mathcal M(y_i))=\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)(y_i)\), with \(\alpha_i\) determined by \(\psi_0\). So \(\psi_u\) is surjective iff \(u(y_i)\ne-\alpha_i\) for all \(i\). Since \(y_i\notin B\), evaluation at \(y_i\) is a nonzero linear form on \(H^0(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))\), so \(\{u:u(y_i)=-\alpha_i\}\) is a proper affine hyperplane, and a finite union of proper affine hyperplanes cannot cover the vector space. Choose \(u\) outside them. (Lemma A)

Lemma B (very ampleness). Suppose that for every closed subscheme \(Z'\subseteq C\) of length 2 (i.e. every effective divisor of degree 2) the restriction \(H^0(C,\mathcal G)\to H^0(Z',\mathcal G|_{Z'})\) is surjective. Then \(F=\mathcal L(C_0+\mathfrak bf)\) is very ample; in particular it has no base points.

Proof. By (II, 7.8.2) it suffices to show that for every closed subscheme \(Z\subseteq X\) of length 2 the map \(H^0(X,F)\to H^0(Z,F|_Z)\) is surjective (taking \(Z\) reduced of two points this gives global generation and separation of points; taking \(Z\) supported at one point, it gives separation of tangent vectors, since a subspace of the 2-dimensional \(F\otimes\mathfrak m/\mathfrak m^2\) which surjects onto all its one-dimensional quotients is everything). Let \(Z\subseteq X\) have length 2, and \(Z'\) its scheme-theoretic image in \(C\); then either \(Z\subseteq\) a fibre \(\pi^{-1}(y)\), or \(\pi|_Z\colon Z\to Z'\) is an isomorphism with \(Z'\) of length 2.

Case A: \(Z\subseteq\pi^{-1}(y)=\mathbf P(\mathcal E(y))\). The restriction of \(F\) to the fibre is \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(1)\), with \(H^0=\mathcal G(y)\), and \(H^0(X,F)=H^0(C,\mathcal G)\to H^0(\pi^{-1}(y),F)=\mathcal G(y)\) is the evaluation. This is surjective: take \(Z'=y+y'\), \(y'\ne y\); surjectivity onto \(\mathcal G|_{Z'}=\mathcal G(y)\oplus\mathcal G(y')\) gives surjectivity onto \(\mathcal G(y)\). And \(H^0(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(1))\to H^0(\mathcal O_Z(1))\) is surjective (\(\mathcal O(1)\) is very ample on \(\mathbf P^1\)). Composition gives the claim.

Case B: \(Z\cong Z'\). Then \(Z\) is a section of \(\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\times_CZ'\to Z'\), given by a quotient \(\mathcal E|_{Z'}\to\mathcal M_Z\to0\) with \(\mathcal M_Z\) invertible on \(Z'\), and \(F|_Z=\mathcal M_Z\otimes\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)|_{Z'}\). The restriction \(H^0(X,F)=H^0(\mathcal G)\to H^0(Z,F|_Z)\) is the composite \(H^0(\mathcal G)\to H^0(\mathcal G|_{Z'})\to H^0(\mathcal M_Z\otimes\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)|_{Z'})\), the first map surjective by hypothesis, the second surjective because \(\mathcal G|_{Z'}\to\mathcal M_Z\otimes\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)|_{Z'}\) is surjective and \(Z'\) is affine. (Lemma B)

Lemma C (criteria for the hypothesis of B). The hypothesis of Lemma B holds if either

  • •

    [(i)] \(H^1(C,\mathcal G(-Z'))=0\) for every effective divisor \(Z'\) of degree 2; or

  • •

    [(ii)] \(\mathfrak b\) and \(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e\) are very ample on \(C\) and \(H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))=0\).

Moreover if \(H^1(\mathcal G(-y))=0\) for all points \(y\), then \(\mathcal G\) is generated by global sections, hence so is \(F\).

Proof. (i) The kernel of \(\mathcal G\to\mathcal G|_{Z'}\) is \(\mathcal G(-Z')\), and \(H^0(\mathcal G)\to H^0(\mathcal G|_{Z'})\to H^1(\mathcal G(-Z'))=0\). (ii) Tensor (1) with \(\mathcal O_{Z'}\) (exact, all sheaves locally free): \(0\to\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)|_{Z'}\to\mathcal G|_{Z'}\to\mathcal L(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e)|_{Z'}\to0\). Given \(w\in H^0(\mathcal G|_{Z'})\), its image in \(H^0(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e)|_{Z'})\) lifts to a global section of \(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e)\) (very ample), which lifts to \(s\in H^0(\mathcal G)\) since \(H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))=0\); then \(w-s|_{Z'}\in H^0(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)|_{Z'})\) is the restriction of a global section of \(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)\) (very ample). So \(w\) is in the image. For the last statement: \(H^0(\mathcal G)\to\mathcal G(y)\) is onto when \(H^1(\mathcal G(-y))=0\), so \(\mathcal G\) is generated by global sections; then \(F=\mathcal O_X(1)\otimes\pi^*\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)\) is a quotient of \(\pi^*\mathcal G\), which is generated by global sections (recall \(\pi^*\pi_*F\to F\) is surjective, as \(\mathcal O_X(1)\) is a quotient of \(\pi^*\mathcal E\)), so \(F\) is generated by global sections. (Lemma C)

Proof of (a). By Lemma A there is a section \(D\sim C_0+\mathfrak bf\) (the hypotheses \(H^1(\mathfrak b)=0\), \(H^0(\mathfrak b)\ne0\), \(|\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e|\) bpf hold). Next, \(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)\) and \(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e)\) are generated by global sections and \(H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))=0\), so from (1) \(H^0(\mathcal G)\to H^0(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e))\) is onto; hence at each point \(y\) the fibre \(\mathcal G(y)\) is spanned by the images of global sections of \(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b)\) (viewed in \(\mathcal G\) via (1)) and of lifts of global sections of \(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e)\), and so \(\mathcal G\) is generated by global sections. Then \(F\) has no base points as in the last statement of Lemma C, i.e. \(|D|=|C_0+\mathfrak bf|\) has no base points.

Proof of (b). The hypothesis “\(\mathfrak b-P\) nonspecial for all \(P\)” gives \(H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))=0\) (the map \(H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b-P))\to H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))\) is surjective). So (ii) of Lemma C holds, Lemma B applies, and \(F=\mathcal L(C_0+\mathfrak bf)\) is very ample. (We used only that \(\mathfrak b,\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e\) are very ample and \(\mathfrak b\) is nonspecial.) ∎

Exercise 2.12

Problem

Let \(X\) be a ruled surface with invariant \(e\) over an elliptic curve \(C\), and let \(\mathfrak b\) be a divisor on \(C\).

  1. (a)

    If \(\deg\mathfrak b\ge e+2\), then there is a section \(D\sim C_0+\mathfrak bf\) such that \(|D|\) has no base points.

  2. (b)

    The linear system \(|C_0+\mathfrak bf|\) is very ample if and only if \(\deg\mathfrak b\ge e+3\).

Note. The case \(e=-1\) will require special attention.

Solution

We use Lemmas A, B, C of Exercise 2.11. Here \(g=1\), and by (2.12), (2.15), \(e\ge-1\); \(\deg\mathfrak e=-e\); on an elliptic curve a divisor class of degree \(>0\) is nonspecial and a divisor of degree \(\ge2\) (resp. \(\ge3\)) has no base points (resp. is very ample) (IV, 3.2). Also \(h^0(\mathcal L(\mathfrak c))=\deg\mathfrak c\) for \(\deg\mathfrak c>0\). We need the following fact for \(e=-1\): then \(\mathcal E\) is the nonsplit extension \(0\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(P)\to0\) of (2.15) (\(\mathfrak e\sim P\)), and

\[ H^1(\mathcal E)=0.\tag{2} \]

Indeed, \(H^0(\mathcal L(P))\to H^1(\mathcal O)\to H^1(\mathcal E)\to H^1(\mathcal L(P))=0\), and the connecting map sends \(1_P\) to the image of \(\xi\ne0\) under \(H^1(\mathcal L(-P))\to H^1(\mathcal O)\), which is injective (see 2.7), hence an isomorphism of one-dimensional spaces.

(a) Let \(\deg\mathfrak b\ge e+2\). Then \(\deg\mathfrak b\ge1\) (as \(e\ge-1\)), so \(H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))=0\) and \(H^0(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b))\ne0\); and \(\deg(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e)=\deg\mathfrak b-e\ge2\), so \(|\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e|\) has no base points. By Lemma A there is a section \(D\sim C_0+\mathfrak bf\). For base points of \(|D|=|C_0+\mathfrak bf|\) we apply the last part of Lemma C: we show \(H^1(\mathcal G(-y))=0\) for every \(y\), where \(\mathcal G=\mathcal E(\mathfrak b)\). From \(0\to\mathcal L(\mathfrak b-y)\to\mathcal G(-y)\to\mathcal L(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e-y)\to0\): \(\deg(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e-y)=\deg\mathfrak b-e-1\ge1\), so its \(H^1\) vanishes. And \(\deg(\mathfrak b-y)=\deg\mathfrak b-1\ge e+1\). If \(e\ge0\) this is \(\ge1\) and \(H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b-y))=0\). If \(e=-1\), then \(\deg(\mathfrak b-y)\ge0\) and \(H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b-y))\ne0\) only if \(\mathfrak b-y\sim0\), in which case \(\mathcal G(-y)\cong\mathcal E\) and \(H^1=0\) by (2). So in all cases \(H^1(\mathcal G(-y))=0\), \(\mathcal G\) is generated by global sections, and \(|D|\) has no base points.

(b) (\(\Leftarrow\)) Let \(\deg\mathfrak b\ge e+3\) and let \(Z'\) be an effective divisor of degree 2. Using the sequence \(0\to\mathcal L(\mathfrak b-Z')\to\mathcal G(-Z')\to\mathcal L(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e-Z')\to0\): \(\deg(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e-Z')=\deg\mathfrak b-e-2\ge1\) gives \(H^1=0\). And \(\deg(\mathfrak b-Z')=\deg\mathfrak b-2\ge e+1\): for \(e\ge0\) this is \(\ge1\), so \(H^1(\mathcal L(\mathfrak b-Z'))=0\); for \(e=-1\) it is \(\ge0\), nonvanishing of \(H^1\) requires \(\mathfrak b-Z'\sim0\), and then \(\mathcal G(-Z')\cong\mathcal E\) has \(H^1=0\) by (2). So \(H^1(\mathcal G(-Z'))=0\) for all \(Z'\), and by Lemma C(i) and Lemma B, \(|C_0+\mathfrak bf|\) is very ample. (Note that for \(e\ge0\) one could also use 2.11(b), but for \(e=-1\), \(\deg\mathfrak b=2\) the divisor \(\mathfrak b\) is not very ample, so that exercise does not apply; the cohomological criterion handles it.)

(\(\Rightarrow\)) Suppose \(D=C_0+\mathfrak bf\) is very ample. Then \(D|_{C_0}\) is a very ample divisor on \(C_0\cong C\) (restriction of a very ample sheaf to a closed subvariety), of degree \(D.C_0=C_0^2+\deg\mathfrak b=\deg\mathfrak b-e\) (by (1.3)). A very ample sheaf \(\mathcal M\) of degree \(\delta>0\) on an elliptic curve has \(h^0(\mathcal M)=\delta\) and embeds \(C_0\) in \(\mathbf P^{\delta-1}\); for \(\delta\le2\) this is \(\mathbf P^0\) or \(\mathbf P^1\), which contain no curve of genus 1. Hence \(\deg\mathfrak b-e\ge3\), i.e. \(\deg\mathfrak b\ge e+3\). ∎

Exercise 2.13

Problem

For every \(e\ge-1\) and \(n\ge e+3\), there is an elliptic scroll of degree \(d=2n-e\) in \(\mathbf P^{d-1}\). In particular, there is an elliptic scroll of degree 5 in \(\mathbf P^4\).

Solution

Let \(C\) be an elliptic curve. For each \(e\ge-1\) there is a normalized \(\mathcal E\) on \(C\) with invariant \(e\) (for \(e=-1\) the indecomposable bundle of (2.15), for \(e=0\), \(\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal O\), and for \(e\ge1\), \(\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal L\) with \(\deg\mathcal L=-e\) by (2.12)(a), (2.11.1), (2.11.3)); let \(X=\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\). Choose a divisor \(\mathfrak b\) on \(C\) of degree \(n\) and put \(D=C_0+\mathfrak bf\). By Ex. 2.12(b), \(D\) is very ample as \(n\ge e+3\); so \(|D|\) embeds \(X\) in \(\mathbf P^N\), \(N+1=h^0(X,\mathcal L(D))=h^0(C,\mathcal E(\mathfrak b))\). We have \(D.f=1\), so each fibre is mapped to a line: \(X\) is a scroll. Its degree is \(D^2=C_0^2+2\deg\mathfrak b=-e+2n=d\). Finally \(\mathcal G=\mathcal E(\mathfrak b)\) has rank 2 and degree \(-e+2n=d\) and (as in the proof of (2.12)) \(H^1(\mathcal G)=0\) (from (1): \(\deg\mathfrak b>0\) and \(\deg(\mathfrak b+\mathfrak e)=n-e>0\)), so by Riemann–Roch \(h^0(\mathcal G)=\deg\mathcal G+2(1-g)=d\). Thus \(N=d-1\) and \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^{d-1}\) is an elliptic scroll of degree \(d=2n-e\).

For \(e=-1\), \(n=2\) (allowed: \(n\ge e+3=2\)) we get \(d=5\): an elliptic scroll of degree 5 in \(\mathbf P^4\). ∎

Exercise 2.14

Problem

Let \(X\) be a ruled surface over a curve \(C\) of genus \(g\), with invariant \(e<0\), and assume that \(\operatorname{char}k=p>0\) and \(g\ge2\).

  1. (a)

    If \(Y\equiv aC_0+bf\) is an irreducible curve \(\ne C_0,f\), then either \(a=1\), \(b\ge0\), or \(2\le a\le p-1\), \(b\ge\frac12ae\), or \(a\ge p\), \(b\ge\frac12ae+1-g\).

  2. (b)

    If \(a>0\) and \(b>a\bigl(\frac12e+(1/p)(g-1)\bigr)\), then any divisor \(D\equiv aC_0+bf\) is ample. On the other hand, if \(D\) is ample, then \(a>0\) and \(b>\frac12ae\).

Solution

We use \(C_0^2=-e\), \(C_0.f=1\), \(f^2=0\), \(K\equiv-2C_0+(2g-2-e)f\) (2.11), and \(\operatorname{Num}X=\Z C_0\oplus\Z f\).

(a) Since \(Y\ne f\) and \(\pi|_Y\) is surjective, \(Y.f=a>0\).

Case \(a=1\). Then \(\pi|_Y\colon Y\to C\) has degree \(Y.f=1\), so it is a finite birational morphism onto the nonsingular curve \(C\), hence an isomorphism: \(Y\) is a section, corresponding to a surjection \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal M\to0\) with kernel \(\mathcal N\) invertible, \(\deg\mathcal N+\deg\mathcal M=\deg\mathcal E=-e\). By normalization \(\deg\mathcal N\le0\) (otherwise \(\mathcal N\otimes\mathcal N^{-1}=\mathcal O\subseteq\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal N^{-1}\) contradicts \(H^0(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal N^{-1})=0\)). So \(\deg\mathcal M\ge-e\). By (2.9) \(\deg\mathcal M=C_0.Y=-e+b\). Hence \(b\ge0\).

Case \(a\ge2\). By adjunction (1.5), \(2p_a(Y)-2=Y.(Y+K)\), and

\[ Y^2=-a^2e+2ab,\qquad Y.K=ae+a(2g-2)-2b,\qquad\text{so}\qquad 2p_a(Y)-2=(a-1)(2b-ae)+a(2g-2). \tag{3}\]

Let \(\tilde Y\to Y\) be the normalization and \(\varphi\colon\tilde Y\to C\) the finite morphism of degree \(a\) induced by \(\pi\). Then \(p_a(Y)\ge g(\tilde Y)\) (IV, Ex. 1.8).

If \(a\le p-1\), the extension \(K(\tilde Y)/K(C)\) has degree \(a<p\), so its inseparable degree (a power of \(p\) dividing \(a\)) is 1: \(\varphi\) is separable and by Hurwitz (IV, 2.4) \(2g(\tilde Y)-2=a(2g-2)+\deg R\ge a(2g-2)\). Hence \(2p_a(Y)-2\ge a(2g-2)\) and by (3) \((a-1)(2b-ae)\ge0\), so \(b\ge\frac12ae\) (since \(a\ge2\)).

In any case (in particular for \(a\ge p\)) \(g(\tilde Y)\ge g\) by (IV, 2.5.4), so \(2p_a(Y)-2\ge2g-2\), and (3) gives \((a-1)(2b-ae)\ge-(a-1)(2g-2)\), i.e. \(2b-ae\ge-(2g-2)\), i.e. \(b\ge\frac12ae+1-g\). This proves (a).

(b) Suppose \(a>0\) and \(b>a\bigl(\frac e2+\frac{g-1}p\bigr)\). Then \(b-\frac12ae>a(g-1)/p>0\) (as \(g\ge2\)). We apply Nakai's criterion (1.10) to \(D\equiv aC_0+bf\). First \(D^2=a(2b-ae)>0\). Next, \(D.f=a>0\) and \(D.C_0=b-ae=(b-\frac12ae)+\frac12a(-e)>0\) since \(e<0\). Let \(Y\equiv a'C_0+b'f\) be an irreducible curve \(\ne C_0,f\); then \(D.Y=ab'+a'b-aa'e\) and, by (a):

  • •

    if \(a'=1\), \(b'\ge0\): \(D.Y\ge b-ae>0\);

  • •

    if \(2\le a'\le p-1\), \(b'\ge\frac12a'e\): \(D.Y\ge\frac12aa'e+a'b-aa'e=a'(b-\frac12ae)>0\);

  • •

    if \(a'\ge p\), \(b'\ge\frac12a'e+1-g\): \(D.Y\ge a'(b-\frac12ae)-a(g-1)>a'a\frac{g-1}p-a(g-1)=a(g-1)(\frac{a'}p-1)\ge0\).

So \(D.Y>0\) for all irreducible curves \(Y\), and \(D\) is ample by (1.10).

Conversely, if \(D\equiv aC_0+bf\) is ample then \(D.f=a>0\) and \(D^2=a(2b-ae)>0\), so \(2b>ae\), i.e. \(b>\frac12ae\). ∎

Exercise 2.15

Problem

Funny behavior in characteristic \(p\). Let \(C\) be the plane curve \(x^3y+y^3z+z^3x=0\) over a field \(k\) of characteristic 3 (IV, Ex. 2.4).

  1. (a)

    Show that the action of the \(k\)-linear Frobenius morphism \(f\) on \(H^1(C,\mathcal O_C)\) is identically 0 (Cf. (IV, 4.21)).

  2. (b)

    Fix a point \(P\in C\), and show that there is a nonzero \(\xi\in H^1(\mathcal L(-P))\) such that \(f^*\xi=0\) in \(H^1(\mathcal L(-3P))\).

  3. (c)

    Now let \(\mathcal E\) be defined by \(\xi\) as an extension

    \[ 0\to\mathcal O_C\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(P)\to0, \]

    and let \(X\) be the corresponding ruled surface over \(C\). Show that \(X\) contains a nonsingular curve \(Y\equiv3C_0-3f\), such that \(\pi\colon Y\to C\) is purely inseparable. Show that the divisor \(D=2C_0\) satisfies the hypotheses of (2.21b), but is not ample.

Solution

The curve \(C\) is nonsingular: the partial derivatives of \(F=x^3y+y^3z+z^3x\) in characteristic 3 are \(z^3,x^3,y^3\), which have no common zero in \(\mathbf P^2\). So \(C\) is a plane quartic and \(g=3\). The coefficients of \(F\) lie in \(\mathbf F_3\).

Frobenius. Let \(F_{\operatorname{abs}}\colon C\to C\) be the absolute Frobenius morphism (identity on points, \(u\mapsto u^3\) on functions; IV, Sect. 2). For any invertible sheaf \(\mathcal M\) it induces \(F_{\operatorname{abs}}^*\colon H^1(C,\mathcal M)\to H^1(C,F^*_{\operatorname{abs}}\mathcal M)=H^1(C,\mathcal M^{\otimes3})\), computed on Cech cocycles \((m_{ij})\mapsto(m_{ij}^{\otimes3})\); it is additive and \(3\)-semilinear (\(\lambda\mapsto\lambda^3\)). The \(k\)-linear Frobenius \(f\) of the exercise (IV, 2.4.1) is \((x:y:z)\mapsto(x^3:y^3:z^3)\) (as \(C\) is defined over \(\mathbf F_3\), \(C_p\cong C\)); it differs from \(F_{\operatorname{abs}}\) by the semilinear automorphism of \(C=C_0\times_{\mathbf F_3}k\) induced by \(\lambda\mapsto\lambda^3\) on \(k\), so that \(f^*\) and \(F^*_{\operatorname{abs}}\) agree on classes defined over \(\mathbf F_3\) and differ by a bijective semilinear map in general; in particular \(f^*=0\) iff \(F^*_{\operatorname{abs}}=0\), and similarly for the maps in (b). We write \(f^*\) for either and compute with \(F_{\operatorname{abs}}\).

(a) From \(0\to\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^2}(-4)\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^2}\to\mathcal O_C\to0\) and \(H^1(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^2})=H^2(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^2})=0\) we get an isomorphism \(\delta\colon H^1(\mathcal O_C)\to H^2(\mathbf P^2,\mathcal O(-4))\), and by (III, 5.1) the latter has basis the Cech classes of the Laurent monomials \(x^{-i}y^{-j}z^{-k}\) with \(i,j,k\ge1\), \(i+j+k=4\), i.e. \(c_1=x^{-2}y^{-1}z^{-1}\), \(c_2=x^{-1}y^{-2}z^{-1}\), \(c_3=x^{-1}y^{-1}z^{-2}\) (a monomial with some nonnegative exponent is zero in \(H^2\)).

Frobenius in terms of \(\delta\). Let \(u=(u_{ij})\) be a Cech 1-cocycle of \(\mathcal O_C\) and \(\tilde u_{ij}\) lifts to \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^2}\). Then \(\delta[u]\) is represented by the 2-cocycle \(\partial\tilde u/F\), where \(\partial\) is the Cech coboundary (which takes values in \(F\cdot\mathcal O(-4)\)). The cocycle \(u^3\) has the lifts \(\tilde u_{ij}^3\) and \(\partial(\tilde u^3)=(\partial\tilde u)^3\) (characteristic 3), so \(\delta[u^3]\) is represented by \((\partial\tilde u)^3/F=F^2\cdot(\partial\tilde u/F)^3\). Hence, under \(\delta\), \(F_{\operatorname{abs}}^*\) corresponds to the map \(c\mapsto F^2c^3\) on \(H^2(\mathbf P^2,\mathcal O(-4))\) (degrees: \(8-12=-4\)).

Now \(F^2=x^6y^2+y^6z^2+z^6x^2+2x^3y^4z+2xy^3z^4+2x^4yz^3\). For \(c=c_1=x^{-2}y^{-1}z^{-1}\), \(c^3=x^{-6}y^{-3}z^{-3}\), and multiplying out, each term of \(F^2c^3\) has a nonnegative exponent: \(y^{-1}z^{-3}\), \(x^{-6}y^{3}z^{-1}\), \(x^{-4}y^{-3}z^{3}\), \(2x^{-3}y^{1}z^{-2}\), \(2x^{-5}y^{0}z^{1}\), \(2x^{-2}y^{-2}z^{0}\). All vanish in \(H^2\). Since \(F\) is invariant under the cyclic permutation \(x\to y\to z\to x\), which permutes \(c_1,c_2,c_3\), also \(F^2c_i^3=0\) for \(i=2,3\). As \(F^*_{\operatorname{abs}}\) is semilinear and kills a basis, it is zero. This proves (a).

(b) Let \(\mathcal I=\mathcal L(-P)\subseteq\mathcal O_C\) be the ideal of \(P\). The cube map \(\mathcal I\to\mathcal I^{\otimes3}\cong\mathcal L(-3P)\subseteq\mathcal O_C\), \(u\mapsto u^3\), induces the map \(f^*\colon H^1(\mathcal L(-P))\to H^1(\mathcal L(-3P))\) on cohomology. Consider the diagram with the maps \(\iota_1\colon H^1(\mathcal L(-P))\to H^1(\mathcal O_C)\), \(\iota_3\colon H^1(\mathcal L(-3P))\to H^1(\mathcal O_C)\) induced by the inclusions; since cubing commutes with inclusions, \(\iota_3\circ f^*=f^*\circ\iota_1=0\) by (a). So \(f^*\) maps \(H^1(\mathcal L(-P))\) into \(\ker\iota_3\). Now compute dimensions. From \(0\to\mathcal L(-P)\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal O_P\to0\): \(H^0(\mathcal O)\to H^0(\mathcal O_P)\) is onto, so \(H^1(\mathcal L(-P))\cong H^1(\mathcal O_C)\) has dimension 3. From \(0\to\mathcal L(-3P)\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal O_{3P}\to0\), with \(\dim H^0(\mathcal O_{3P})=3\) and \(H^0(\mathcal L(-3P))=0\): the cokernel of \(H^0(\mathcal O)=k\to H^0(\mathcal O_{3P})\) has dimension 2 and equals \(\ker\iota_3\). Thus \(f^*\colon H^1(\mathcal L(-P))\ (\dim3)\to\ker\iota_3\ (\dim2)\) is an additive \(3\)-semilinear map. Choose a basis \(\xi_1,\xi_2,\xi_3\) of \(H^1(\mathcal L(-P))\) and put \(w_i=f^*\xi_i\in\ker\iota_3\). For \(\xi=\sum\lambda_i\xi_i\) we have \(f^*\xi=\sum\lambda_i^3w_i\). Since \(\dim\ker\iota_3=2<3\), the \(w_i\) are linearly dependent: there are \(\mu_i\in k\), not all 0, with \(\sum\mu_iw_i=0\). As \(\lambda\mapsto\lambda^3\) is bijective on \(k\), we may write \(\mu_i=\lambda_i^3\); then \(\xi=\sum\lambda_i\xi_i\ne0\) and \(f^*\xi=0\).

(c) Let \(\mathcal E\) be the extension defined by this \(\xi\in\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal L(P),\mathcal O)=H^1(\mathcal L(-P))\), \(X=\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\), \(\pi\colon X\to C\).

\(\mathcal E\) is normalized with \(e=-1\): \(H^0(\mathcal E)\ne0\); for \(\mathcal M\) of negative degree, \(H^0(\mathcal M)=0\) and \(H^0(\mathcal L(P)\otimes\mathcal M)=0\) unless \(\mathcal M\cong\mathcal L(-P)\); and \(H^0(\mathcal E(-P))=0\) because \(H^0(\mathcal E(-P))\hookrightarrow\ker(H^0(\mathcal O)\to H^1(\mathcal L(-P)),1\mapsto\xi)=0\). So \(\mathcal E\) is normalized, \(\deg\mathcal E=1\), \(e=-1\), \(C_0^2=1\).

The curve \(Y\). Apply \(F_{\operatorname{abs}}^*\) (flat, as \(C\) is a nonsingular curve) to the extension: \(0\to\mathcal O\to F^*_{\operatorname{abs}}\mathcal E\to\mathcal L(3P)\to0\), whose class is \(f^*\xi=0\in H^1(\mathcal L(-3P))\) (a class given by transition data \((u_{ij})\) becomes \((u_{ij}^3)\)). Thus \(F_{\operatorname{abs}}^*\mathcal E\cong\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal L(3P)\) and in particular there is a surjection \(F_{\operatorname{abs}}^*\mathcal E\to\mathcal O\) (a retraction of \(\mathcal O\to F^*\mathcal E\)). By (II, 7.12) this defines a morphism \(\nu\colon C\to X\) with \(\pi\circ\nu=F_{\operatorname{abs}}\) and \(\nu^*\mathcal O_X(1)\cong\mathcal O_C\). Let \(Y=\nu(C)\) (irreducible, closed) and \(\Gamma=\nu_*[C]=m[Y]\) with \(m=\deg(C\to Y)\). Numerically \(\Gamma\equiv a'C_0+b'f\) where, by (1.3) and the projection formula, \(a'=\Gamma.f=\deg\nu^*\pi^*\mathcal O(y)=\deg F^*_{\operatorname{abs}}\mathcal O(y)=3\) and \(\Gamma.C_0=\deg\nu^*\mathcal O_X(C_0)=\deg\nu^*\mathcal O_X(1)=0\) (here \(\mathcal L(C_0)\cong\mathcal O_X(1)\), (2.8)). With \(C_0^2=1\): \(0=\Gamma.C_0=3+b'\), so \(b'=-3\): \(\Gamma\equiv3C_0-3f\).

Now \(3=\deg F_{\operatorname{abs}}=m\cdot\deg(Y\to C)\). If \(m=3\), then \(Y\to C\) has degree 1, \(Y\) is a section with \(3Y\equiv3C_0-3f\), so \(Y\equiv C_0-f\) and by (2.9) \(\deg\mathcal M=Y.C_0=1-1=0\) for the corresponding quotient \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal M\), whose kernel then has degree \(1>0\), contradicting normalization (as above). So \(m=1\): \(C\to Y\) is birational, \(Y\equiv3C_0-3f\), \(\pi|_Y\colon Y\to C\) has degree 3 and \(K(Y)=K(C)\) (the source) contains \(K(C)^3\) (the target), so it is purely inseparable of degree 3.

Remark on “nonsingular”. The book says \(Y\) is nonsingular; this cannot be right. By (3) of Exercise 2.14 (valid in any characteristic) with \(a=3\), \(b=-3\), \(e=-1\), \(g=3\): \(2p_a(Y)-2=2(-6+3)+3\cdot4=6\), so \(p_a(Y)=4\), while \(Y\) is birational to \(C\), of genus 3. (Equivalently, \(d\nu\colon T_C\to\nu^*T_{X/C}=(\nu^*\Omega_{X/C})^\vee\) is a nonzero map \(\mathcal O(-K)\to\mathcal O(-3P)\) between sheaves of degrees \(-4\) and \(-3\) and so vanishes at one point: \(\nu\) is not an immersion there.) So \(Y\) has one (unibranch) singular point, with \(\delta\)-invariant \(1\). In fact no nonsingular curve \(Y'\) with \(\pi|_{Y'}\) purely inseparable of degree \(3\) can exist at all: then \(K(Y')^3=K(C)\) (as \([K(Y'):K(Y')^3]=3\)), so \(Y'\) is isomorphic to \(C\) up to the Frobenius twist of \(k\) and has genus \(3\), whereas any curve \(\equiv3C_0-3f\) has \(p_a=4\). So the word “nonsingular” in the printed statement has to be dropped. This does not affect what is needed below: only the irreducible curve \(Y\equiv3C_0-3f\) with \(\pi|_Y\) purely inseparable is used.

\(D=2C_0\). The hypotheses of (2.21b) are that \(D\equiv aC_0+bf\) has \(a>0\) and \(b>\frac12ae\) (the other hypothesis there, \(\operatorname{char}k=0\) or \(g\le1\), fails here, which is the point). For \(D=2C_0\): \(a=2\), \(b=0>\frac12\cdot2\cdot(-1)=-1\). So the numerical condition of (2.21b) holds. But \(D.Y=2C_0.(3C_0-3f)=6C_0^2-6C_0.f=6-6=0\), so \(D\) is not ample (\(D.Y>0\) is necessary for ampleness; (1.10) or the definition via the degree of \(Y\) in a projective embedding). Thus Prop. 2.21(b) fails in characteristic \(p\) (the case \(\operatorname{char}k=p>0\), \(g\ge2\), \(e<0\), cf. Ex. 2.14). ∎

Exercise 2.16

Problem

Let \(C\) be a nonsingular affine curve. Show that two locally free sheaves \(\mathcal E,\mathcal E'\) of the same rank are isomorphic if and only if their classes in the Grothendieck group \(K(C)\) (II, Ex. 6.10) and (II, Ex. 6.11) are the same. This is false for a projective curve.

Solution

Here \(K(C)\) is the quotient of the free abelian group on isomorphism classes of locally free sheaves by the relations \([\mathcal E]=[\mathcal E']+[\mathcal E'']\) for exact sequences \(0\to\mathcal E'\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal E''\to0\) (for regular \(C\) this agrees with the group generated by coherent sheaves, II, Ex. 6.9–6.11; we only use that \(\operatorname{rank}\) and \(\det\) are defined on locally free sheaves).

(\(\Rightarrow\)) is trivial. For (\(\Leftarrow\)): for an exact sequence of locally free sheaves, \(\det\mathcal E\cong\det\mathcal E'\otimes\det\mathcal E''\), and \(\det\colon\{\text{loc.\ free}\}\to\operatorname{Pic}C\) therefore induces a group homomorphism \(\det\colon K(C)\to\operatorname{Pic}C\). If \([\mathcal E]=[\mathcal E']\) then \(\det\mathcal E\cong\det\mathcal E'\). So it suffices to prove:

Claim. If \(C=\operatorname{Spec}A\) is a nonsingular affine curve and \(\mathcal E\) is locally free of rank \(r\ge1\), then \(\mathcal E\cong\mathcal O_C^{\,r-1}\oplus\det\mathcal E\).

Then, if the ranks agree and the determinants agree, \(\mathcal E\cong\mathcal O^{r-1}\oplus\det\mathcal E\cong\mathcal O^{r-1}\oplus\det\mathcal E'\cong\mathcal E'\).

Proof of the Claim. Induct on \(r\); \(r=1\) is trivial. Let \(r\ge2\). \(\mathcal E\) is generated by finitely many sections; let \(V\subseteq H^0(\mathcal E)\) be a finite-dimensional subspace generating \(\mathcal E\). As in 2.3(a) (with \(C\) affine): \(Z=\{(s,y)\in V\times C:s(y)=0\}\) is a vector bundle of rank \(\dim V-r\) over \(C\) of dimension \(\dim V-r+1\le\dim V-1\), so there is \(s\in V\) not vanishing at any point; \(s\colon\mathcal O\to\mathcal E\) is then injective with locally free cokernel \(\mathcal E_1\) of rank \(r-1\). Since \(C\) is affine, \(\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal E_1,\mathcal O)=H^1(C,\mathcal E_1^\vee)=0\), so the sequence splits: \(\mathcal E\cong\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal E_1\), and \(\det\mathcal E\cong\det\mathcal E_1\). By induction \(\mathcal E_1\cong\mathcal O^{r-2}\oplus\det\mathcal E_1\). (Claim)

Failure for projective curves. On \(C=\mathbf P^1\) the Euler sequence \(0\to\mathcal O(-1)\to\mathcal O^2\to\mathcal O(1)\to0\) gives \([\mathcal O(-1)]+[\mathcal O(1)]=2[\mathcal O]=[\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal O]\) in \(K(\mathbf P^1)\), i.e. \([\mathcal O(1)\oplus\mathcal O(-1)]=[\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal O]\) (the class of a direct sum is the sum of the classes, by the split exact sequence). These two sheaves have the same rank 2 and the same class, but they are not isomorphic: \(H^0\bigl((\mathcal O(1)\oplus\mathcal O(-1))\otimes\mathcal O(-1)\bigr)=H^0(\mathcal O\oplus\mathcal O(-2))\) has dimension 1, whereas \(H^0(\mathcal O^2\otimes\mathcal O(-1))=0\). ∎

Exercise 2.17

Problem
  1. (a)

    Let \(\varphi\colon\mathbf P^1_k\to\mathbf P^3_k\) be the 3-uple embedding (I, Ex. 2.12). Let \(\mathcal I\) be the sheaf of ideals of the twisted cubic curve \(C\) which is the image of \(\varphi\). Then \(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2\) is a locally free sheaf of rank 2 on \(C\), so \(\varphi^*(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2)\) is a locally free sheaf of rank 2 on \(\mathbf P^1\). By (2.14), therefore, \(\varphi^*(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2)\cong\mathcal O(l)\oplus\mathcal O(m)\) for some \(l,m\in\Z\). Determine \(l\) and \(m\).

  2. (b)

    Repeat part (a) for the embedding \(\varphi\colon\mathbf P^1\to\mathbf P^3\) given by \(x_0=t^4\), \(x_1=t^3u\), \(x_2=tu^3\), \(x_3=u^4\), whose image is a nonsingular rational quartic curve. [Answer: If \(\operatorname{char}k\ne2\), then \(l=m=-7\); if \(\operatorname{char}k=2\), then \(l,m=-6,-8\).]

(Starred in the book.)

Solution

Let \(\varphi=(\varphi_0,\dots,\varphi_3)\colon\mathbf P^1\to\mathbf P^3\) be given by forms of degree \(d\) in \(t,u\) (no common zero), which is an embedding with image \(C\). We treat (a) (\(d=3\), \(\varphi=(t^3,t^2u,tu^2,u^3)\)) and (b) (\(d=4\)) simultaneously, in every characteristic. Put \(\mathcal Q=\varphi^*(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2)\); this is locally free of rank 2.

Step 1: \(\mathcal Q\) as a kernel. Since \(C\) is a nonsingular subvariety of \(\mathbf P^3\), (II, 8.17) gives an exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2\to\Omega_{\mathbf P^3}|_C\to\Omega_C\to0\). Pull back by \(\varphi\colon\mathbf P^1\xrightarrow{\sim}C\):

\[ 0\to\mathcal Q\to\varphi^*\Omega_{\mathbf P^3}\to\Omega_{\mathbf P^1}\to0 . \]

By the Euler sequences (II, 8.13), \(\Omega_{\mathbf P^3}=\ker\bigl(\mathcal O(-1)^4\xrightarrow{(x_i)}\mathcal O\bigr)\), so \(\varphi^*\Omega_{\mathbf P^3}=\ker(\mathcal O(-d)^4\xrightarrow{(\varphi_i)}\mathcal O)\) (the pull-back of the surjection stays surjective and the kernel is locally free), and \(\Omega_{\mathbf P^1}=\ker(\mathcal O(-1)^2\xrightarrow{(t,u)}\mathcal O)\). The map \(\varphi^*\Omega_{\mathbf P^3}\to\Omega_{\mathbf P^1}\) is induced (by functoriality of the Euler sequence for the ring map \(x_i\mapsto\varphi_i\), \(dx_i\mapsto d\varphi_i\)) by

\[ \mathcal O(-d)^4\to\mathcal O(-1)^2,\qquad (g_i)\mapsto\Bigl(\sum_ig_i\frac{\partial\varphi_i}{\partial t},\ \sum_ig_i\frac{\partial\varphi_i}{\partial u}\Bigr). \]

Since \(\Omega_{\mathbf P^1}\subseteq\mathcal O(-1)^2\) we get

\[ \mathcal Q=\Bigl\{(g_i)\in\mathcal O(-d)^4:\ \sum g_i\varphi_i=0,\ \sum g_i\varphi_{i,t}=0,\ \sum g_i\varphi_{i,u}=0\Bigr\} . \]

Its degree is \(\deg\varphi^*\Omega_{\mathbf P^3}-\deg\Omega_{\mathbf P^1}=-4d+2\). By (2.14), \(\mathcal Q\cong\mathcal O(l)\oplus\mathcal O(m)\), \(l\ge m\), with \(l+m=-4d+2\). Left exactness of \(H^0\) gives, for every \(s\),

\[ H^0(\mathcal Q(s))=\Bigl\{(g_i)\in H^0(\mathcal O(s-d))^4:\ \sum g_i\varphi_i=\sum g_i\varphi_{i,t}=\sum g_i\varphi_{i,u}=0\Bigr\}.\tag{4} \]

(a) \(d=3\): \(\varphi=(t^3,t^2u,tu^2,u^3)\), \(l+m=-10\). Take \(s=4\), so \(g_i=a_it+b_iu\) are linear forms. With \(\varphi_{,t}=(3t^2,2tu,u^2,0)\), \(\varphi_{,u}=(0,t^2,2tu,3u^2)\) the three equations in (4) become, coefficientwise:

\begin{align*} \textstyle\sum g_i\varphi_i=0:&\quad a_0=0,\ \ b_0+a_1=0,\ \ b_1+a_2=0,\ \ b_2+a_3=0,\ \ b_3=0;\\ \textstyle\sum g_i\varphi_{i,t}=0:&\quad 3a_0=0,\ \ 3b_0+2a_1=0,\ \ 2b_1+a_2=0,\ \ b_2=0;\\ \textstyle\sum g_i\varphi_{i,u}=0:&\quad a_1=0,\ \ b_1+2a_2=0,\ \ 2b_2+3a_3=0,\ \ 3b_3=0 . \end{align*}

From these (valid in every characteristic): \(a_0=b_3=0\), \(a_1=0\), \(b_2=0\); then \(b_0=-a_1=0\), \(a_3=-b_2=0\); and \(b_1=-a_2\) with \(2b_1+a_2=0\) gives \(b_1=0=a_2\). So all coefficients vanish and \(H^0(\mathcal Q(4))=0\). Hence \(l+4<0\), \(l\le-5\), and with \(l+m=-10\), \(l\ge m\): \(l=m=-5\).

Answer (a): \(\varphi^*(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2)\cong\mathcal O(-5)\oplus\mathcal O(-5)\) (in all characteristics), i.e. the normal bundle of the twisted cubic is \(\mathcal O(5)\oplus\mathcal O(5)\).

(b) \(d=4\): \(\varphi=(t^4,t^3u,tu^3,u^4)\), \(l+m=-14\). Here \(\varphi_{,t}=(4t^3,3t^2u,u^3,0)\), \(\varphi_{,u}=(0,t^3,3tu^2,4u^3)\). Take \(s=6\), so \(g_i=a_it^2+b_itu+c_iu^2\) (quadrics). The equations (4) give, coefficientwise:

\begin{align*} \textstyle\sum g_i\varphi_i=0:&\quad a_0=0,\ b_0+a_1=0,\ c_0+b_1=0,\ c_1+a_2=0,\ b_2+a_3=0,\ c_2+b_3=0,\ c_3=0;\\ \textstyle\sum g_i\varphi_{i,t}=0:&\quad 4a_0=0,\ 4b_0+3a_1=0,\ 4c_0+3b_1=0,\ 3c_1+a_2=0,\ b_2=0,\ c_2=0;\\ \textstyle\sum g_i\varphi_{i,u}=0:&\quad a_1=0,\ b_1=0,\ c_1+3a_2=0,\ 3b_2+4a_3=0,\ 3c_2+4b_3=0,\ 4c_3=0 . \end{align*}

(For instance \(g_0\cdot4t^3+g_1\cdot3t^2u+g_2u^3=0\) has the coefficients \(4a_0\) of \(t^5\), \(4b_0+3a_1\) of \(t^4u\), \(4c_0+3b_1\) of \(t^3u^2\), \(3c_1+a_2\) of \(t^2u^3\), \(b_2\) of \(tu^4\), \(c_2\) of \(u^5\); and similarly for the others.) In every characteristic: \(a_0=c_3=0\), \(b_2=c_2=0\), \(a_1=b_1=0\); hence \(b_0=-a_1=0\), \(c_0=-b_1=0\), \(a_3=-b_2=0\), \(b_3=-c_2=0\). What remains are \(c_1,a_2\) with

\[ c_1+a_2=0,\qquad 3c_1+a_2=0,\qquad c_1+3a_2=0, \]

i.e. \(a_2=-c_1\) and \(2c_1=0\).

  • •

    If \(\operatorname{char}k\ne2\): \(c_1=a_2=0\), all coefficients vanish, \(H^0(\mathcal Q(6))=0\). Then \(l+6<0\), \(l\le-7\), and with \(l+m=-14\), \(l\ge m\): \(l=m=-7\).

  • •

    If \(\operatorname{char}k=2\): \(c_1=-a_2\) is free, so \(\dim H^0(\mathcal Q(6))=1\) (spanned by \((0,u^2,t^2,0)\): indeed \(u^2\cdot t^3u+t^2\cdot tu^3=2t^3u^3=0\) and the derivative conditions hold since \(4=0\) and \(3c_1+a_2=c_1+a_2=0\)). Since \(h^0(\mathcal O(l+6))+h^0(\mathcal O(m+6))=1\) with \(l+m=-14\), \(l\ge m\), we must have \(l+6=0\) and \(m+6=-2\): \(l=-6\), \(m=-8\).

Answer (b): \(\varphi^*(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2)\cong\mathcal O(-7)\oplus\mathcal O(-7)\) if \(\operatorname{char}k\ne2\), and \(\cong\mathcal O(-6)\oplus\mathcal O(-8)\) if \(\operatorname{char}k=2\). ∎

3. Monoidal transformations

Conventions. As throughout Chapter V, \(k\) is algebraically closed and a surface is a nonsingular projective surface over \(k\). For a monoidal transformation \(\pi:\widetilde X\to X\) with centre \(P\) I write \(E=\pi^{-1}(P)\) for the exceptional curve. I use freely the results of the text: \(E\cong\mathbf P^1\), \(E^2=-1\) (Prop. 3.1); \(\pi^*C.\pi^*D=C.D\), \(\pi^*C.E=0\) (Prop. 3.2); \(\pi^*C=\widetilde C+\mu_P(C)E\) for an effective divisor \(C\) (Prop. 3.6), hence \(\widetilde C.E=\mu_P(C)\) and \(p_a(\widetilde C)=p_a(C)-\tfrac12 r(r-1)\) for \(r=\mu_P(C)\) (Cor. 3.7); and the notion of infinitely near points (Section 3, before Example 3.9.2). Standard facts of commutative algebra beyond the text are named where they are used.

Exercise 3.1

Problem

Let \(X\) be a nonsingular projective variety of any dimension, let \(Y\) be a nonsingular subvariety, and let \(\pi:\widetilde X\to X\) be obtained by blowing up \(Y\). Show that \(p_a(\widetilde X)=p_a(X)\).

Solution

Let \(n=\dim X\) and \(r=\operatorname{codim}(Y,X)\). Recall (III, Ex. 5.3) that \(p_a(X)=(-1)^n(\chi(\mathcal O_X)-1)\), so it suffices to show \(H^i(\widetilde X,\mathcal O_{\widetilde X})\cong H^i(X,\mathcal O_X)\) for all \(i\). If \(r=1\) the ideal of \(Y\) is invertible and \(\pi\) is an isomorphism, so assume \(r\ge2\). By (II, 8.24), \(\widetilde X\) is a nonsingular projective variety of dimension \(n\), \(E=\pi^{-1}(Y)\) is a nonsingular divisor, \(f=\pi|_E:E\to Y\) is a projective bundle with fibres \(\mathbf P^{r-1}\), and the ideal \(\mathcal J\) of \(E\) satisfies \(\mathcal J/\mathcal J^2\cong\mathcal O_E(1)\) (the tautological sheaf of the projective bundle), so that the conormal sheaf of \(E\) in \(\widetilde X\) is \(\mathcal O_E(1)\).

Step 1: \(\pi_*\mathcal O_{\widetilde X}=\mathcal O_X\). The sheaf \(\pi_*\mathcal O_{\widetilde X}\) is a coherent sheaf of \(\mathcal O_X\)-algebras (II, 5.20), contained in the constant sheaf \(K(\widetilde X)=K(X)\) because \(\pi\) is birational. Hence it is a finite \(\mathcal O_X\)-algebra consisting of elements of \(K(X)\), so each of its sections is integral over \(\mathcal O_X\). As \(X\) is nonsingular, hence normal, \(\pi_*\mathcal O_{\widetilde X}\subseteq\mathcal O_X\), and the other inclusion is clear.

Step 2: \(R^i\pi_*\mathcal O_{\widetilde X}=0\) for \(i>0\). These are coherent sheaves supported on \(Y\) (over \(X-Y\) the map \(\pi\) is an isomorphism), so it suffices to show that the stalk at every closed point \(y\in Y\) vanishes. By the theorem on formal functions (III, 11.1),

\[ \bigl(R^i\pi_*\mathcal O_{\widetilde X}\bigr)^\wedge_y\cong\varprojlim_m H^i(\widetilde X_m,\mathcal O_{\widetilde X_m}), \]

where \(\widetilde X_m\) is the closed subscheme of \(\widetilde X\) defined by \(\mathcal I^m\), \(\mathcal I=\mathfrak m_y\mathcal O_{\widetilde X}\). The subscheme defined by \(\mathcal I\) is the scheme-theoretic fibre \(F=\pi^{-1}(y)=f^{-1}(y)\cong\mathbf P^{r-1}\), a nonsingular subvariety of the nonsingular variety \(\widetilde X\). Hence its ideal is locally generated by a regular sequence and \(\mathcal I^a/\mathcal I^{a+1}\cong S^a(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2)\), where \(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2\) is the conormal bundle of \(F\) in \(\widetilde X\). For the chain \(F\subset E\subset\widetilde X\) of nonsingular varieties we have an exact sequence of conormal bundles

\[ 0\to(\mathcal J/\mathcal J^2)|_F\to\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2\to\mathcal N^\vee_{F/E}\to0 . \]

Here \((\mathcal J/\mathcal J^2)|_F=\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^{r-1}}(1)\), and \(\mathcal N^\vee_{F/E}\) is free of rank \(n-r\), because \(F\) is the fibre of the smooth morphism \(f\) over the reduced point \(y\), so its ideal in \(E\) is generated by the pull-backs of \(n-r\) local parameters of \(Y\) at \(y\). A symmetric power of an extension has a filtration whose graded pieces are \(S^a(\mathcal O(1))\otimes S^b(\mathcal O^{n-r})=\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^{r-1}}(a)^{\oplus c_{a,b}}\) with \(a,b\ge0\), \(a+b\) equal to the power. Since \(H^i(\mathbf P^{r-1},\mathcal O(a))=0\) for \(i>0\), \(a\ge0\), we get \(H^i(F,\mathcal I^a/\mathcal I^{a+1})=0\) for all \(i>0\), \(a\ge0\). The exact sequences \(0\to\mathcal I^m/\mathcal I^{m+1}\to\mathcal O_{\widetilde X_{m+1}}\to\mathcal O_{\widetilde X_m}\to0\) and induction on \(m\) (\(\widetilde X_1=F\) is covered by the case \(a=0\)) give \(H^i(\widetilde X_m,\mathcal O_{\widetilde X_m})=0\) for all \(i>0\) and all \(m\). So the completion of the stalk \((R^i\pi_*\mathcal O_{\widetilde X})_y\) is \(0\); as this stalk is a finitely generated module over the local ring \(\mathcal O_{X,y}\) and completion is faithful (Krull), the stalk is \(0\).

Step 3. By Steps 1, 2 and (III, Ex. 8.1) (the Leray spectral sequence degenerates), \(H^i(\widetilde X,\mathcal O_{\widetilde X})\cong H^i(X,\pi_*\mathcal O_{\widetilde X})=H^i(X,\mathcal O_X)\) for all \(i\ge0\). Hence \(\chi(\mathcal O_{\widetilde X})=\chi(\mathcal O_X)\) and \(p_a(\widetilde X)=p_a(X)\). (For \(n=2\) and \(Y\) a point this is Prop. 3.4 and Cor. 3.5.) ∎

Exercise 3.2

Problem

Let \(C\) and \(D\) be curves on a surface \(X\), meeting at a point \(P\). Let \(\pi:\widetilde X\to X\) be the monoidal transformation with center \(P\). Show that \(\widetilde C.\widetilde D=C.D-\mu_P(C)\cdot\mu_P(D)\). Conclude that \(C.D=\sum\mu_P(C)\cdot\mu_P(D)\), where the sum is taken over all intersection points of \(C\) and \(D\), including infinitely near intersection points.

Solution

The formula. Put \(r=\mu_P(C)\), \(s=\mu_P(D)\). By Prop. 3.6, \(\pi^*C=\widetilde C+rE\) and \(\pi^*D=\widetilde D+sE\), so \(\widetilde C=\pi^*C-rE\), \(\widetilde D=\pi^*D-sE\). By Prop. 3.2, \(\pi^*C.\pi^*D=C.D\), \(\pi^*C.E=\pi^*D.E=0\) and \(E^2=-1\), hence

\[ \widetilde C.\widetilde D=(\pi^*C-rE).(\pi^*D-sE)=C.D+rs\,E^2=C.D-rs . \]

(Equivalently, \(\widetilde C.E=r\), \(\widetilde D.E=s\) by Cor. 3.7 and \(\pi^*C.\pi^*D=(\widetilde C+rE).(\widetilde D+sE)=\widetilde C.\widetilde D+rs+rs-rs\).)

The consequence. Let \(C,D\) have no common component (otherwise \(C.D\) is not a sum over finitely many intersection points); then \(C\cap D\) is finite. Blow up the points of \(C\cap D\) one after another. A monoidal transformation with centre \(P\) does not change the strict transforms of \(C\) and \(D\) near the other points of \(C\cap D\) (it is an isomorphism away from \(P\)), so applying the formula repeatedly we get, with \(C^{(1)},D^{(1)}\) the strict transforms on the resulting surface \(X^{(1)}\),

\[ C^{(1)}.D^{(1)}=C.D-\sum_{P\in C\cap D}\mu_P(C)\mu_P(D). \]

The strict transforms \(C^{(1)},D^{(1)}\) still have no common component, so \(C^{(1)}.D^{(1)}\ge0\) (Prop. 1.4), and every term \(\mu_P(C)\mu_P(D)\) is \(\ge1\) because \(P\in C\cap D\) (Remark 3.5.2). Hence \(C^{(1)}.D^{(1)}<C.D\) as soon as \(C\cap D\ne\emptyset\). Repeating with \(C^{(1)},D^{(1)}\) (their common points lie on the exceptional curves; they are the intersection points of \(C\) and \(D\) that are infinitely near, of the first order, to points of \(C\cap D\)), the nonnegative integer \(C^{(j)}.D^{(j)}\) strictly decreases until \(C^{(m)}\cap D^{(m)}=\emptyset\), at which stage \(C^{(m)}.D^{(m)}=0\) by Prop. 1.4. Summing the identities gives

\[ C.D=\sum_{Q}\mu_Q(C)\,\mu_Q(D), \]

the sum over all (ordinary and infinitely near) points \(Q\) lying on both (strict transforms of) \(C\) and \(D\), where \(\mu_Q\) denotes the multiplicity of the strict transform at \(Q\) (the process is finite, so the sum is). ∎

Exercise 3.3

Problem

Let \(\pi:\widetilde X\to X\) be a monoidal transformation, and let \(D\) be a very ample divisor on \(X\). Show that \(2\pi^*D-E\) is ample on \(\widetilde X\). [Hint: Use a suitable generalization of (I, Ex. 7.5) to curves in \(\mathbf P^n\).]

Solution

I use Nakai's criterion (Thm. 1.10): put \(H=2\pi^*D-E\); we must show \(H^2>0\) and \(H.\Gamma>0\) for every irreducible curve \(\Gamma\subseteq\widetilde X\).

Self-intersection. By Prop. 3.2, \(H^2=4D^2-4\pi^*D.E+E^2=4D^2-1\). Since \(D\) is very ample, \(D.C>0\) for every curve \(C\) (Remark 1.6.2), and applying this with \(C\) an effective divisor in \(|D|\) gives \(D^2>0\), so \(D^2\ge1\) and \(H^2\ge3>0\).

Curves. \(H.E=2\pi^*D.E-E^2=1>0\). Let \(\Gamma\ne E\) be an irreducible curve, \(C=\pi(\Gamma)\), so \(\Gamma=\widetilde C\) and \(\pi^*C=\widetilde C+rE\) with \(r=\mu_P(C)\ge0\). Then

\[ H.\widetilde C=2\pi^*D.(\pi^*C-rE)-E.(\pi^*C-rE)=2D.C-r . \]

Claim: \(D.C\ge r\). If \(r=0\) this is clear (\(D.C>0\) since \(D\) is ample), so let \(r\ge1\), i.e. \(P\in C\). Embed \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^N\) by \(|D|\). Hyperplanes through \(P\) cut out divisors \(D'\in|D|\) with \(P\in D'\), and there is such a hyperplane not containing \(C\) (the hyperplanes through \(P\) have intersection \(\{P\}\), and \(C\) is a curve). Then \(D'\) and \(C\) have no common component, \(\mu_P(D')\ge1\), and by Ex. 3.2 (the first formula), applied to \(C\) and \(D'\),

\[ 0\le\widetilde C.\widetilde{D'}=C.D'-\mu_P(C)\mu_P(D')\le D.C-r, \]

where the first inequality uses Prop. 1.4 for the strict transforms (they have no common component) and \(C.D'=C.D\) since \(D'\sim D\). This proves the claim (it is the generalization of I, Ex. 7.5: the degree of a curve in projective space is at least its multiplicity at any point).

So \(H.\widetilde C=2D.C-r\ge 2r-r\) if \(r\ge1\), i.e. \(H.\widetilde C\ge r\ge1\), and \(H.\widetilde C=2D.C>0\) if \(r=0\). Thus \(H.\Gamma>0\) for all irreducible curves and \(H\) is ample. ∎

Exercise 3.4

Problem

Multiplicity of a Local Ring. (See Nagata [7, Ch III, Section 23] or Zariski–Samuel [1, vol 2, Ch VIII, Section 10].) Let \(A\) be a noetherian local ring with maximal ideal \(\mathfrak m\). For any \(l>0\), let \(\psi(l)=\operatorname{length}(A/\mathfrak m^l)\). We call \(\psi\) the Hilbert–Samuel function of \(A\).

  1. (a)

    Show that there is a polynomial \(P_A(z)\in\mathbf Q[z]\) such that \(P_A(l)=\psi(l)\) for all \(l\gg0\). This is the Hilbert–Samuel polynomial of \(A\). [Hint: Consider the graded ring \(\operatorname{gr}_{\mathfrak m}A=\bigoplus_{d\ge0}\mathfrak m^d/\mathfrak m^{d+1}\), and apply (I, 7.5).]

  2. (b)

    Show that \(\deg P_A=\dim A\).

  3. (c)

    Let \(n=\dim A\). Then we define the multiplicity of \(A\), denoted \(\mu(A)\), to be \((n!)\cdot(\text{leading coefficient of }P_A)\). If \(P\) is a point on a noetherian scheme \(X\), we define the multiplicity of \(P\) on \(X\), \(\mu_P(X)\), to be \(\mu(\mathcal O_{P,X})\).

  4. (d)

    Show that for a point \(P\) on a curve \(C\) on a surface \(X\), this definition of \(\mu_P(C)\) coincides with the one in the text just before (3.5.2).

  5. (e)

    If \(Y\) is a variety of degree \(d\) in \(\mathbf P^n\), show that the vertex of the cone over \(Y\) is a point of multiplicity \(d\).

Solution

(a) Let \(\kappa=A/\mathfrak m\) and let \(x_1,\dots,x_s\) generate \(\mathfrak m\). Then \(G=\operatorname{gr}_{\mathfrak m}A\) is a quotient of the polynomial ring \(S=\kappa[X_1,\dots,X_s]\) (map \(X_i\mapsto\) the class of \(x_i\) in \(\mathfrak m/\mathfrak m^2\)), so \(G\) is a finitely generated graded \(S\)-module, and each \(G_d=\mathfrak m^d/\mathfrak m^{d+1}\) is a finite-dimensional \(\kappa\)-vector space whose length as an \(A\)-module is \(\dim_\kappa G_d\). By the Hilbert polynomial theorem (I, 7.5; its proof works over any field \(\kappa\)) there is \(Q(z)\in\mathbf Q[z]\) with \(\dim_\kappa G_d=Q(d)\) for \(d\gg0\). Since \(\operatorname{length}(\mathfrak m^d/\mathfrak m^{d+1})=\dim_\kappa G_d\) and lengths add in the filtration \(A\supset\mathfrak m\supset\dots\supset\mathfrak m^l\),

\[ \psi(l)=\sum_{d=0}^{l-1}\dim_\kappa G_d . \]

Choose \(R(z)\in\mathbf Q[z]\) with \(R(z)-R(z-1)=Q(z-1)\) (a polynomial in the binomial basis \(\binom{z}{j}\) can be summed termwise; \(\deg R=\deg Q+1\)). For \(l\ge l_0\) large, \(\psi(l)-\psi(l-1)=Q(l-1)=R(l)-R(l-1)\), so \(\psi(l)-R(l)\) is a constant \(c\) for \(l\ge l_0\), and \(P_A=R+c\in\mathbf Q[z]\) works.

(b) This is the dimension theorem for noetherian local rings: \(\deg P_A=d(A)=\dim A\) (Atiyah–Macdonald, Thm. 11.14; Matsumura, Thm. 13.4). (The proof there shows \(d(A)\le\delta(A)\) – the least number of generators of an \(\mathfrak m\)-primary ideal – by comparing \(\mathfrak m^l\) with the powers of such an ideal, and then \(\dim A\le d(A)\) by induction on \(\dim A\), using the exact sequence \(0\to A/(\mathfrak q:x)\to A/\mathfrak q\to A/(\mathfrak q+xA)\to0\); Krull's theorem gives \(\delta(A)\le\dim A\).)

(c) This is a definition. Note that \(\mu(A)\) is a positive integer: \(P_A\) is an integer-valued polynomial of degree \(n\) (it is a sum of binomial coefficients \(\binom{z}{j}\) with integral coefficients), so its leading coefficient is \(c/n!\) with \(c\in\mathbf Z\), and \(c>0\) because \(P_A(l)\to\infty\).

(d) Let \(A=\mathcal O_{P,X}\), a regular local ring of dimension \(2\) (as \(X\) is a surface) with maximal ideal \(\mathfrak m\) and residue field \(k\), and let \(f\in A\) be a local equation of \(C\), so \(\mathcal O_{P,C}=B=A/fA\) (dimension \(1\) for \(P\in C\)). Let \(r\) be the largest integer with \(f\in\mathfrak m^r\) (the definition in the text). Because \(A\) is regular, \(\operatorname{gr}_{\mathfrak m}A\cong k[X,Y]\) is a domain. For \(g\in A\), \(g\neq0\), let \(j\) be the integer with \(g\in\mathfrak m^j\setminus\mathfrak m^{j+1}\) (it exists by Krull's intersection theorem). Then the initial forms multiply: \(\operatorname{in}(fg)=\operatorname{in}(f)\operatorname{in}(g)\ne0\), so \(fg\in\mathfrak m^{r+j}\setminus\mathfrak m^{r+j+1}\). Consequently

\[ (\mathfrak m^l:f)=\mathfrak m^{l-r}\qquad(l\ge r). \]

Now \(\operatorname{length}(B/\mathfrak m_B^l)=\operatorname{length}(A/(\mathfrak m^l+fA))=\operatorname{length}(A/\mathfrak m^l)-\operatorname{length}((fA+\mathfrak m^l)/\mathfrak m^l)\), and \((fA+\mathfrak m^l)/\mathfrak m^l\cong fA/(fA\cap\mathfrak m^l)\cong A/(\mathfrak m^l:f)=A/\mathfrak m^{l-r}\). Since \(P_A(l)=\sum_{d<l}(d+1)=\binom{l+1}{2}\), we get for \(l\ge r\)

\[ \psi_B(l)=\binom{l+1}2-\binom{l-r+1}2=rl-\binom r2 . \]

This is a polynomial of degree \(1=\dim B\) with leading coefficient \(r\), so \(\mu(B)=1!\cdot r=r\), as claimed.

(e) Let \(Y\subseteq\mathbf P^n\) be a variety of dimension \(t\) and degree \(d\), with homogeneous coordinate ring \(R=S(Y)=k[x_0,\dots,x_n]/I(Y)\). Embed \(\mathbf P^n\) as a hyperplane in \(\mathbf P^{n+1}\) and let \(V=(0:\dots:0:1)\). The cone over \(Y\) with vertex \(V\) is the subscheme of \(\mathbf P^{n+1}\) with homogeneous ideal \(I(Y)k[x_0,\dots,x_{n+1}]\); in the affine chart \(x_{n+1}=1\) it is \(\operatorname{Spec}R\) with \(V\) the origin, so \(\mathcal O_{V}=A=R_{\mathfrak m}\), \(\mathfrak m=R_+=(x_0,\dots,x_n)\). As \(R\) is graded and generated by \(R_1\) over \(k=R_0\), we have \(\mathfrak m^j/\mathfrak m^{j+1}=R_j\), and since \(R/\mathfrak m^l\) has finite length and is supported at the origin, \(\operatorname{length}(A/\mathfrak m^lA)=\dim_kR/\mathfrak m^l=\sum_{j<l}\dim_kR_j=\sum_{j<l}\varphi_Y(j)\). By (I, 7.5 and the definition of degree), \(\varphi_Y(j)=P_Y(j)\) for \(j\gg0\), where \(P_Y\) has degree \(t\) and leading coefficient \(d/t!\). Therefore \(P_A(l)=\sum_{j<l}P_Y(j)+\mathrm{const}\) has degree \(t+1=\dim A\) and leading coefficient \(\dfrac{d}{t!\,(t+1)}=\dfrac d{(t+1)!}\), so \(\mu(A)=(t+1)!\cdot\dfrac d{(t+1)!}=d\). ∎

Exercise 3.5

Problem

Let \(a_1,\dots,a_r\), \(r\ge5\), be distinct elements of \(k\), and let \(C\) be the curve in \(\mathbf P^2\) given by the (affine) equation \(y^2=\prod_{i=1}^r(x-a_i)\). Show that the point \(P\) at infinity on the \(y\)-axis is a singular point. Compute \(\delta_P\), and \(g(\widetilde Y)\), where \(\widetilde Y\) is the normalization of \(Y\). Show in this way that one obtains hyperelliptic curves of every genus \(g\ge2\).

Solution

Hypothesis. I assume \(\operatorname{char}k\neq2\) (as is implicit: in characteristic \(2\) the function field \(k(x,y)\), \(y^2=f(x)\), is purely inseparable over \(k(x)\) and the curve has genus \(0\)). Write \(Y=C\) and \(m=r-2\ge3\).

The curve. Homogenizing, \(C:\ y^2z^{r-2}=\prod_{i=1}^r(x-a_iz)\), a plane curve of degree \(r\); it is irreducible, since \(f(x)=\prod(x-a_i)\) is squarefree of positive degree, hence not a square in \(k(x)\), so \(y^2-f\) is irreducible in \(k(x)[y]\). Thus \(p_a(C)=\frac12(r-1)(r-2)\) (Ex. 1.5.1). Its affine points are nonsingular: \(\partial/\partial y=2y\) and \(\partial/\partial x=-f'(x)\) vanish simultaneously with \(y^2=f(x)\) only if \(f(x)=f'(x)=0\), impossible since \(f\) has simple roots. The only point at infinity is \(P=(0:1:0)\) (\(z=0\) forces \(x=0\)).

The point \(P\). In the chart \(y=1\), with coordinates \((u,w)=(x,z)\), \(P\) is the origin and \(C\) is \(F(u,w)=w^{m}-\prod_{i=1}^r(u-a_iw)=0\). The second term is homogeneous of degree \(r\) and \(w^m\) has degree \(m=r-2<r\), so \(\mu_P(C)=m=r-2\ge3\ge2\): \(P\) is a singular point, with tangent cone \(w^m\).

Embedded resolution. Blow up \(P\). In the chart \(w=tu\) we have \(F=u^m\bigl(t^m-u^2U(t)\bigr)\) where \(U(t)=\prod(1-a_it)\), \(U(0)=1\). In the other chart \(u=sw\) one gets \(w^m(1-w^2\prod(s-a_i))\), whose strict transform \(1-w^2\prod(s-a_i)\) does not meet the exceptional curve \(w=0\). So the strict transform \(C_1\) is \(G_1=t^m-u^2U(t)\) near \(Q_1=(t,u)=(0,0)\) and has multiplicity \(\min(m,2)=2\) there (as \(m\ge3\)), with tangent cone \(u^2\). Blow up \(Q_1\), in the chart \(u=vt\) (the tangent direction): \(G_1=t^2(t^{m-2}-v^2U(t))\); in the other chart \(t=su\) one gets \(u^2(s^mu^{m-2}-U(su))\), whose strict transform takes the value \(-1\) on \(u=0\), so does not meet the exceptional curve. Inductively, the strict transform \(C_j\) is, near the only point \(Q_j=(0,0)\) of the new exceptional curve through which it passes with multiplicity \(\ge 1\), given by

\[ G_j=t^{m_j}-v^2U(t),\qquad m_j=m-2(j-1), \]

and its multiplicity at \(Q_j\) is \(\min(m_j,2)\) as long as \(m_j\ge1\) (with the same argument for the other chart). If \(m_j\ge3\) the tangent cone is \(v^2\) and blowing up gives \(G_{j+1}=t^{m_j-2}-v^2U\); if \(m_j=2\) then \(G_j=t^2-v^2U(t)\) has two distinct tangents \(t=\pm v\) (as \(U(0)=1\), \(\operatorname{char}\ne2\)) and one more blow-up separates the two branches into two nonsingular points; if \(m_j=1\) then \(G_j\) is nonsingular. Hence the singular points of \(C\) and its infinitely near points are \(P\) (multiplicity \(r-2\)) and \(Q_1,\dots,Q_N\), each of multiplicity \(2\), where \(N\) is the number of \(j\ge1\) with \(m-2(j-1)\ge2\), i.e. \(N=\lfloor m/2\rfloor\). By Example 3.9.3 (and IV, Ex. 1.8)

\[ \delta_P=\tfrac12m(m-1)+\bigl\lfloor\tfrac m2\bigr\rfloor=\tfrac12(r-2)(r-3)+\Bigl\lfloor\frac{r-2}{2}\Bigr\rfloor . \]

The genus. \(C\) has no other singular points, so by Example 3.9.2 the normalization \(\widetilde Y\) (the strict transform in the last blown-up surface, a nonsingular projective curve birational to \(C\)) has

\[ g(\widetilde Y)=\tfrac12(r-1)(r-2)-\tfrac12(r-2)(r-3)-\Bigl\lfloor\frac{r-2}2\Bigr\rfloor=(r-2)-\Bigl\lfloor\frac{r-2}2\Bigr\rfloor=\Bigl\lfloor\frac{r-1}{2}\Bigr\rfloor . \]

Hyperelliptic curves. \(K(\widetilde Y)=k(x,y)\) is a quadratic extension of \(k(x)\) (as \(y^2=f(x)\) is irreducible), so \(x\) defines a finite morphism \(\widetilde Y\to\mathbf P^1\) of degree \(2\). For \(r=2g+1\) (\(g\ge2\), so \(r\ge5\)) we obtain \(g(\widetilde Y)=g\). Hence \(\widetilde Y\) is hyperelliptic of genus \(g\) for every \(g\ge2\). (E.g. \(r=5,6\) give \(g=2\); \(r=7,8\) give \(g=3\).) ∎

Exercise 3.6

Problem

Show that analytically isomorphic curve singularities (I, 5.6.1) are equivalent in the sense of (3.9.4), but not conversely.

Solution

Conventions. By (I, 5.6.1) points \(P\in C\subset X\) and \(P'\in C'\subset X'\) (curves on surfaces) are analytically isomorphic if \(\widehat{\mathcal O}_{P,C}\cong\widehat{\mathcal O}_{P',C'}\) as \(k\)-algebras. For equivalence in the sense of (3.9.4) I use the following reading: the sequences of monoidal transformations resolving \(C\) and \(C'\) blow up, at each stage, corresponding singular points of the reduced total transforms, and the correspondence of components and singular points preserves incidence and multiplicities; in particular the multiplicity of the strict transform of \(C\) at each blown-up point, and the incidence graph of the total transform, are the same. (If both singular points are nonsingular points of the curves they are trivially equivalent, so assume \(P,P'\) singular.)

Analytically isomorphic \(\Rightarrow\) equivalent. Choose isomorphisms \(\widehat{\mathcal O}_{P,X}\cong k[[x,y]]\), \(\widehat{\mathcal O}_{P',X'}\cong k[[x',y']]\) (\(X,X'\) nonsingular surfaces, \(k\) algebraically closed, Cohen structure theorem); then \(\widehat{\mathcal O}_{P,C}=k[[x,y]]/(f)\), \(\widehat{\mathcal O}_{P',C'}=k[[x',y']]/(f')\) for local equations \(f,f'\) of \(C,C'\), and since \(P,P'\) are singular, \(f,f'\in\mathfrak m^2\). Let \(\theta:k[[x,y]]/(f)\to k[[x',y']]/(f')\) be an isomorphism. Lift \(\theta(\bar x),\theta(\bar y)\) to elements \(\alpha(x),\alpha(y)\in\mathfrak m'\), giving a continuous local \(k\)-algebra homomorphism \(\alpha:k[[x,y]]\to k[[x',y']]\) inducing \(\theta\). On \(\mathfrak m/\mathfrak m^2\to\mathfrak m'/\mathfrak m'^2\) it is an isomorphism (\(f,f'\in\mathfrak m^2\), so these spaces are the cotangent spaces of the curves, and \(\theta\) is an isomorphism), hence \(\alpha\) is an isomorphism (formal inverse function theorem). The kernel of \(k[[x,y]]\xrightarrow{\alpha}k[[x',y']]\to k[[x',y']]/(f')\) is \((f)\), because this composite induces the injective map \(\theta\) on \(k[[x,y]]/(f)\) and kills \(f\). So \(\alpha^{-1}((f'))=(f)\), i.e. \(\alpha(f)=u\,f'\) with \(u\) a unit.

Now the data entering the definition of equivalence are computed on the completions. Blowing up the maximal ideal \(\mathfrak m\) of \(k[[x,y]]\) gives, over the closed points \(Q\) of the exceptional curve \(E=\mathbf P(\mathfrak m/\mathfrak m^2)\), local rings \(\widehat{\mathcal O}_{\widetilde X,Q}\cong k[[x_1,t_1]]\) described by the formulas \(y=x_1t_1\) or \(x=y_1t_1'\) (in terms of the generators \(x,y\)), the exceptional curve being \(x_1=0\) and the strict transform of \(C\) being given by \(f(x_1,x_1t_1)/x_1^{r}\), \(r=\) multiplicity. The isomorphism \(\alpha\) induces a bijection \(E\to E'\) (\(\mathbf P(\mathfrak m/\mathfrak m^2)\cong\mathbf P(\mathfrak m'/\mathfrak m'^2)\)) and, for corresponding points \(Q\leftrightarrow Q'\), an isomorphism of the completed local rings of the blown-up surfaces carrying the ideal of \(E\) to that of \(E'\) and the ideal of the strict transform \(\widetilde C\) to that of \(\widetilde C'\) (the strict transform is the closure of the complement of \(E\) in the total transform \(\alpha(f)=uf'\) and the exceptional divisor is divided out; both are intrinsic to the pair \((\widehat{\mathcal O},(f))\)). This gives equal multiplicities \(\mu_P(C)=\mu_{P'}(C')\), a bijection of the points \(Q\in\widetilde C\cap E\), and for every such point an isomorphism of the completions of the pairs (surface; total reduced transform). Repeating at each singular point of the reduced total transform, we obtain by induction that the multiplicities of the strict transforms at the infinitely near points, the incidence of the components and the position of all singular points of the reduced total transforms correspond at every stage. In particular a resolution \(U_n\to\dots\to U_0\) of \(C\) by blowing up successively the singular points of the total transform induces the corresponding sequence \(U_n'\to\dots\to U_0'\) for \(C'\), which is also an embedded resolution (normal crossings is a property of the completed local rings), and the required correspondence exists. So \(P\in C\) and \(P'\in C'\) are equivalent.

Not conversely. Let \(\lambda\in k\), \(\lambda\ne0,1\), and let \(C_\lambda\subset\mathbf A^2\) be the union of four lines through the origin, \(f_\lambda=xy(x-y)(x-\lambda y)=0\) (an ordinary quadruple point).

(i) All \(C_\lambda\) are equivalent at \(0\). Blow up the origin (\(\mu=4\)): the strict transform consists of four disjoint smooth branches meeting the exceptional curve \(E\) transversally in four distinct points (the four tangent directions), so the reduced total transform has only nodes (normal crossings), and this single blow-up is an embedded resolution for every \(\lambda\). Any bijection between the four branches (and the four intersection points with \(E\)) is a correspondence preserving incidence and multiplicities. Hence all \(C_\lambda\) are equivalent.

(ii) \(C_\lambda\) and \(C_\mu\) are analytically isomorphic only if \(\mu\) lies in the \(S_3\)-orbit \(\{\lambda,\lambda^{-1},1-\lambda,(1-\lambda)^{-1},\lambda(\lambda-1)^{-1},(\lambda-1)\lambda^{-1}\}\) of \(\lambda\). The ideal \((f_\lambda)\) is homogeneous, so \(\operatorname{gr}_{\mathfrak m}\widehat{\mathcal O}_{0,C_\lambda}=k[x,y]/(f_\lambda)\). An isomorphism \(\widehat{\mathcal O}_{0,C_\lambda}\cong\widehat{\mathcal O}_{0,C_\mu}\) induces an isomorphism of these graded \(k\)-algebras, which are generated in degree \(1\); so it is given by an invertible linear substitution \(T\) of \((x,y)\) with \(T(f_\lambda)=c\,f_\mu\), \(c\in k^*\). Thus the projective transformation of \(\mathbf P^1\) induced by \(T\) carries the four zeros \(\{0,\infty,1,\lambda\}\) of \(f_\lambda\) to the four zeros \(\{0,\infty,1,\mu\}\) of \(f_\mu\), so the two sets of four points have the same cross-ratio class, i.e. \(\mu\) lies in the \(S_3\)-orbit of \(\lambda\). Since \(k\) is infinite and orbits have at most six elements, we can choose \(\mu\) outside the orbit of \(\lambda\); then \(C_\lambda,C_\mu\) are equivalent by (i) but not analytically isomorphic. ∎

Exercise 3.7

Problem

For each of the following singularities at \((0,0)\) in the plane, give an embedded resolution, compute \(\delta_P\), and decide which ones are equivalent.

  1. (a)

    \(x^3+y^5=0\).

  2. (b)

    \(x^3+x^4+y^5=0\).

  3. (c)

    \(x^3+y^4+y^5=0\).

  4. (d)

    \(x^3+y^5+y^6=0\).

  5. (e)

    \(x^3+xy^3+y^5=0\).

Solution

Notation: \(P_0\) is the origin, \(E_i\) the exceptional curve of the \(i\)-th blow-up and \(\widetilde E_i\) its strict transform at later stages, \(C_i\) the strict transform of the curve on the \(i\)-th surface; \(\delta_P=\sum\frac12 r_Q(r_Q-1)\) over \(P_0\) and the infinitely near singular points \(Q\) of the curve with multiplicity \(r_Q\) (Example 3.9.3). I describe each step in the chart containing the relevant point, computing with the substitutions \(x=vy\) (the tangent cone is \(x^3\) in all five cases, so the tangent direction is \(x=0\)) and the subsequent ones; in each case I checked the other chart: it contains no point of the strict transform of the curve. No assumption on the characteristic is needed.

(a), (b), (d) together. Write \(f=x^3A(x)+y^5B(y)\) with \(A,B\) polynomials, \(A(0)=B(0)=1\): (a) \(A=B=1\); (b) \(A=1+x\), \(B=1\); (d) \(A=1\), \(B=1+y\).

Step 1 (centre \(P_0\), multiplicity \(3\)). With \(x=vy\): \(f=y^3\bigl(v^3A(vy)+y^2B(y)\bigr)\). The strict transform \(C_1:\ v^3A(vy)+y^2B(y)=0\) has multiplicity \(2\) at \(Q_1=(v,y)=(0,0)\) with tangent cone \(y^2\) (the line \(y=0\) is the tangent line of \(E_1\)). The only singular point of the reduced total transform \(C_1+E_1\) is \(Q_1\) (mult. \(3\)).

Step 2 (centre \(Q_1\), mult. \(2\)). With \(y=sv\): \(v^3A(sv^2)+s^2v^2B(sv)=v^2\bigl[vA(sv^2)+s^2B(sv)\bigr]\). So \(C_2:\ G=vA(sv^2)+s^2B(sv)=0\) is nonsingular at \(Q_2=(0,0)\) with tangent line \(v=0\); \(E_2:\ v=0\), \(\widetilde E_1:\ s=0\). So \(C_2\) is tangent to \(E_2\) (since \(G|_{v=0}=s^2B(0)=s^2\): contact order exactly \(2\)) and transversal to \(\widetilde E_1\) (\(G|_{s=0}=v\)). The only singular point of the reduced total transform is \(Q_2\), where three smooth branches \(C_2,E_2,\widetilde E_1\) meet, \(C_2,E_2\) being tangent (mult. \(3\)).

Step 3 (centre \(Q_2\), mult. \(1\) of \(C_2\)). With \(v=as\): \(G=s\,[aA(as^3)+sB(as^2)]\). So \(C_3:\ aA(as^3)+sB(as^2)=0\) is smooth with tangent line \(a+s=0\) at \(Q_3=(a,s)=(0,0)\); \(E_3:\ s=0\), \(\widetilde E_2:\ a=0\); \(\widetilde E_1\) meets \(E_3\) elsewhere. At \(Q_3\) the three curves \(C_3,E_3,\widetilde E_2\) are smooth with three different tangents (a triple point of the reduced total transform).

Step 4 (centre \(Q_3\), mult. \(1\)). Blowing up \(Q_3\) separates the three branches (different tangents). The reduced total transform now consists of \(C_4,\widetilde E_1,\dots,\widetilde E_3,E_4\) with only nodes: \(E_4\) meets \(C_4,\widetilde E_2,\widetilde E_3\) at three distinct points, and \(\widetilde E_1\) meets \(\widetilde E_3\) transversally. So this is an embedded resolution, by four blow-ups with multiplicities \(3,2,1,1\) of \(C\) at the centres. The singular infinitely near points of \(C\) are \(P_0\) (\(3\)), \(Q_1\) (\(2\)), hence

\[ \delta_P=\tfrac12\cdot3\cdot2+\tfrac12\cdot2\cdot1=4 . \]

(For (a) this agrees with \(\delta=(3-1)(5-1)/2=4\) for \(x^3+y^5\), a cusp with semigroup \(\langle3,5\rangle\).) The data – multiplicities \(3,2,1,1\) of \(C\) at the successive centres, the incidence of the total transforms at each stage, the fact that the two new singular points at each stage are as described – are identical for (a), (b), (d); so (a), (b), (d) are equivalent.

(c) \(x^3+y^4+y^5\). Step 1: \(x=vy\) gives \(y^3(v^3+y+y^2)\); \(C_1:\ v^3+y(1+y)=0\) is nonsingular at \(Q_1\), tangent to \(E_1:\ y=0\) with contact order \(3\) (\(C_1|_{y=0}=v^3\)); so \(P_0\) has mult. \(3\) and the total transform \(C_1+E_1\) has a singular point \(Q_1\) (mult. \(2\), tangent branches). Step 2 (centre \(Q_1\), mult. \(1\) of \(C_1\)), \(y=sv\): \(v(v^2+s(1+sv))\); \(C_2:\ v^2+s+s^2v=0\) is smooth at \(Q_2=(0,0)\), tangent to \(\widetilde E_1:\ s=0\) (contact \(2\)), transversal to \(E_2:\ v=0\). Step 3 (centre \(Q_2\)), \(s=av\): \(v(v+a+a^2v^2)\); \(C_3:\ v+a+a^2v^2=0\) is smooth, and \(C_3,E_3\ (v=0),\widetilde E_1\ (a=0)\) pass through \(Q_3=(0,0)\) with three distinct tangents. Step 4 (centre \(Q_3\)) separates them. Thus an embedded resolution needs four blow-ups, with multiplicities \(3,1,1,1\) of \(C\) at the centres, and \(\delta_P=\tfrac12\cdot3\cdot2=3\) (equivalent to the cusp of type \(E_6\), \(x^3+y^4\), with \(\delta=(3-1)(4-1)/2=3\)).

(e) \(x^3+xy^3+y^5\). Step 1: \(x=vy\) gives \(y^3(v^3+vy+y^2)\); \(C_1:\ v^3+vy+y^2=0\) has multiplicity \(2\) at \(Q_1=(0,0)\) with tangent cone \(y(v+y)\): two distinct tangents, so \(Q_1\) is a node of \(C_1\). Step 2 (centre \(Q_1\)), \(y=sv\): \(v^2(v+s+s^2)\); \(C_2:\ v+s+s^2=0\) is smooth and meets \(E_2:\ v=0\) transversally in the two points \(s=0\) and \(s=-1\). At \(Q_2=(0,0)\) the three curves \(C_2\) (\(v+s=0\)), \(E_2\) (\(v=0\)), \(\widetilde E_1\) (\(s=0\)) have three distinct tangents; at \((0,-1)\) only \(C_2\) and \(E_2\) meet, transversally.
